- [x] **QuickJS is linked, and JavaScript runs beside Luau.** `Runtime` is an interface; `LuauRuntime` and `JavaScriptRuntime` are two files in one module and adding the second changed no caller - which is what the module's `VENDOR`-not-`VENDOR_PUBLIC` row was for. The extension picks the VM, so `Rings.luau` and `Rings.js` build **the same world** through the same bindings and every program loads either. **Three things the tests caught rather than the design:** `JS_NewContext` adds `Date`, so the context is built from `JS_NewContextRaw` and an explicit intrinsic list; `JS_AddIntrinsicEval` turned out to be required by `JS_Eval` itself, so excluding it produced "eval is not supported" for every script rather than a sandbox, and the global `eval` is deleted afterwards instead; and `JS_AddIntrinsicBigInt` over a raw context leaves an object alive so `JS_FreeRuntime` asserts, reproduced against upstream in isolation. Instances are sealed and scripts run in strict mode, because in sloppy mode assigning to a non-extensible object **silently does nothing** - `part.Transparency = 0.5` would have looked like it worked
- [x] **`Instance.new`, properties and the hierarchy bind to the class table - the same one C++ calls.** A calling convention rather than a second mechanism: `Instance.new` goes through `Store::CreateInstance`, `part.Size = v` through `Store::SetProperty`, and `part.Parent = workspace` through `Store::SetParent`. The marshalling switches on `PropertyType` and **never on a property's name**, so a property `scene` declares tomorrow is reachable from both languages today. **What is not bound yet is named in v0.6 rather than implied**: `.Changed`, `:IsA`, `:Destroy`, `:Clone` and `:GetChildren` all exist in C++ and none of them has a script spelling
- [x] **`--script PATH` stops warning and starts loading**, which closes the oldest bullet in `D00001` - it had been accepted and ignored since v0.1. `--script` on the client, `--game` on the server (ignored since v0.3), `--scene` on the unified harness, and the extension picks the VM
- [x] **the C++ test scene is a script, a version early** - `Rings.luau` and `Rings.js` build the 512-cube ring scene and **animate it themselves** off `RunService.Heartbeat`, composing `CFrame`s the way a Roblox script does. There is no `Orbit` component on that path and no engine system reading one: the engine does not know what an orbit is. `Demo.hpp`/`Demo.cpp` do **not** go away yet, because the C++ path is still what `--entities` builds and is the honest comparison - a scene format and a scripting layer, side by side, with the cost of each on the record
- [x] **`game` is the universe and `workspace` is the world a script runs on**, which is the foundation the rest sits on. `game:GetService` reaches services; `workspace` is the world itself and **not an instance in it**, because a world is what entities live in and making it an entity would put a phantom row in every scene. A script holds one `Store` and no binding hands it another - asserted, not assumed - so **the only way out of a world is a service in the universe**: `MessagingService:PublishAsync` / `SubscribeAsync` over `world::Postbox`, proved across two worlds in one universe. Rule 3 as an API rather than a convention
- [x] the datatypes and globals a scene actually needs: `Instance`, `Vector3`, `CFrame` (with `*` composition and `Angles` in **radians**, matching Roblox, while `Orientation` stays degrees - Roblox's own split, reproduced deliberately), `Color3` with `new` and `fromRGB`, `game`, `workspace`, `RunService.Heartbeat:Connect`, `MessagingService`, `print`, and `nil` as a JavaScript global so one idiom reads in both. The standard libraries a deterministic script may have - `bit32`, `buffer`, `coroutine`, `math`, `string`, `table`, `utf8`, `vector` - and **deliberately not `os` or `debug` in Luau, nor `Date`, `eval` or `Proxy` in JavaScript**, because each is either a wall clock or a way out of the sandbox


### v0.6

Where a world *contains* its scripts rather than being handed one, and where the renderer catches up with what a script can already say. v0.5 made the façade real; what is left is the half a Roblox author reaches for next - signals, the instance methods, the datatype vocabulary - and the rendering those scripts describe.

**The rule everything script-side lands under is already written**: `SCRIPT_CONCURRENCY.md` §1 - a script may only resume from something the barrier delivers in a deterministic order. Every item below that yields answers to it.

**Where this version stands.** The script half is built and measured; the render half is not started. That split is not arbitrary - everything below the line needs `mono.engine/graph/` at L9, and everything above it needed only the class table `scene` already declared. The items are marked individually rather than the version being called done.

- [x] `.Changed` per instance, and the fan-out the projection model creates. `ecs/docs/TODO.md` had the engine half - `OnChanged<T>` is per *type* and Roblox's is per instance, so filtering a whole-world signal per script would walk every change for every connection. The half nothing had written down: **one component write maps to N property names**, so writing `Transform` fires `CFrame`, `Position` *and* `Orientation`. `PropertyDescriptor::Reads` is exactly the map that needs, which is why it was declared rather than left to a conversion to know privately. `script/src/Changes.cpp` is the fan-out; it subscribes **once per component for the whole world** and filters to watched instances with a hash lookup, so the cost scales with what somebody watched and not with what moved - `benchmarks/Surface.cpp` measures 97 ns per watched instance and 1.9 us for a quiet tick. Fires at the **next** beat rather than at the write: `FlushSignals` queues, `Heartbeat` drains, and firing at the barrier instead would put a handler's own writes on the far side of the next `ClearChanges`
- [x] `RBXScriptSignal` and `RBXScriptConnection` as real types - `:Connect` returns a connection with `:Disconnect` and `.Connected`, and `:Once` besides. `script/src/Signals.cpp` holds the ordering rules **shared by both VMs**, because ordering is what a recording depends on: insertion order, a connection made during a fire does not fire in that pass, a disconnection during one takes effect immediately, and a handle is never reused so a double-disconnect kills nothing. No `:Wait()`, and that is the design - a signal fires from inside a handler pump, which is not a barrier
- [x] the instance methods that existed in C++ and had no script spelling: `:IsA`, `:Destroy`, `:Clone`, `:GetChildren`, `:GetDescendants`, `:FindFirstChild`, `:IsDescendantOf`, `:ClearAllChildren`, `:GetPropertyChangedSignal`, and `Instance.new(class, parent)`'s second argument. `workspace` answers `GetChildren` and `FindFirstChild` too, which needed `Store::EachRoot` - the world heads no sibling list, so `EachChild(NULL_ENTITY)` could never have answered it
- [x] **`task`, and the yield rule made real** - `task.wait`, `task.spawn`, `task.defer`, `task.delay`, `task.cancel`. §2 settled the unit and the spelling: seconds in, **ticks underneath**, rounded up so a wait is never shorter than asked; and `wait`, `spawn` and `delay` are refusals that name their replacements rather than absences. A yield is legal now **only when something is scheduled to resume it**, which is a lookup rather than a judgement - v0.5 refused every yield because nothing could
- [x] **the value ↔ bytes codec**. `script/src/Codec.cpp`, and every one of §3's three requirements is a property of the *format* rather than of either binding: keys sorted **by bytes** (not by either language's collation - `strcoll` and UTF-16 code units answer differently and neither may reach the wire), a number always eight bytes so the encoding cannot depend on the value's history, and depth, size and cycles refused rather than truncated. `MessagingService` takes a table now
- [x] `RunService:IsServer()` / `IsClient()` / `IsStudio()` (studio defaults to false), plus **`IsReplica()`, which is not Roblox's and is the more precise question** - `IsServer()` is about the host and a single-player process is a server whose client-side world is still a replica. `Store::SetProperty`'s refusal now names the replica case so a script that did not ask first is told why
- [x] **`Enum` as a property type.** `ecs::EnumTable` holds the named sets, `PropertyType::Enum` and `PropertyDescriptor::EnumName` carry which, and `Store::SetProperty` checks membership - so `part.Material = "Plsatic"` is refused where it was written rather than landing in a component. `Material` is the first, with Roblox's set. The manifest lists the enums and both declaration files emit **branded** aliases, so `Enum.EasingStyle.Linear` does not type-check against a `Material`
- [x] the datatypes a game reaches for once it is past a scene: `Vector2`, `UDim`, `UDim2`, `Rect`, `NumberRange`, `NumberSequence`, `ColorSequence`, `TweenInfo`, and `Random` over `core::Random` - **which is what closes `D00004`**, because a script with a seeded generator is the consumer that item was waiting for. `Region3` and `Ray` are shims over `core::AABB` and `core::Ray`, which the engine already had; adding a second spelling of either would have been the duplicate `AGENTS.md` calls the most expensive kind of debt. `RaycastParams`/`RaycastResult` are **not** done - they want `spatial`'s queries bound, which is a script-to-physics edge this version did not open
- [x] `DateTime` and `tick`/`time`/`elapsedTime`, all over `store.Time()`. **`tick()` returns the tick count and not a Unix timestamp**, which is a deliberate break from Roblox: the name is right for what this engine counts, and a `tick()` that returned real seconds is the single most effective way to make a recording stop replaying. `DateTime.now()` refuses and names `fromSimulated()` and `fromUnixTimestamp(n)`
- [x] `typeof`, `warn`, and the base-library gaps. `typeof` is **not** a bound global - Luau's is a fastcall builtin the VM reaches directly, so the mechanism is the `__type` metafield, which every value metatable now sets. `loadstring`, `getfenv`/`setfenv` and `require` are refusals that say why rather than absences
- [x] **`CollisionGroup`**, and the name-to-layer registry it needed. `spatial::CollisionGroups` - beside `LayerMask`, because that module owns the bits and `scene` deciding a naming scheme would be a component module choosing a physics policy. A **name** crosses and never the index; the setter writes the layer *and* the mask from the matrix, and a group nobody registered is refused rather than silently defaulted
- [x] **`Transparency`'s component half** - a float on `scene::Visual`, bound as a property, distinct from `Visible`. The struct grew by four rather than absorbing `Reserved[3]`, and `scene/tests/Components.cpp` caught that the moment it happened. **The sorted pass is not done** - see below
- [x] **camera from script.** `scene::Camera` is a class now, deriving from `PVInstance`, with `FieldOfView` in degrees over the stored radians. `workspace.CurrentCamera` reads and writes `ActiveCamera`, refuses an instance carrying no `Camera`, and keeps the consumer's aspect ratio across a change
- [x] **`MemoryStoreService` and `DataStoreService`.** A `Ticket` suspends the caller and the barrier's reply resumes it, which is §1's *first* legal source - a coroutine in Luau and a promise in JavaScript, both resolved by the host. Tested against a **real `Universe`** rather than a bare `Store`, because asserting on the script alone would have proved a promise was made and nothing about whether the barrier kept it. That test found two bugs a bare store could not have: `lua_resume` returning `LUA_YIELD` was being read as failure when a resumed script called a second store method, and `emplace` was silently keeping the *stale* thread reference. §5's refusals are values a script can test - `Ok`, `NotFound`, `Conflict`, `OverBudget` - and a replica's write is refused against **both** the bus's `Replica` resource and the store's adopt-only flag, because those are one fact recorded in two places
- [x] **the second consumer for every binding, which is the point of having two VMs**: every item above lands in Luau *and* JavaScript. `JsSurface.cpp` and `JsDatatypes.cpp` are the second half, and **everything about ordering is shared rather than reimplemented** - one `SignalTable`, one `ChangeQueue`, one `TaskQueue`, one codec, so which handler fires first and which tick a wait returns on are answered once for both languages. What differs is what the languages differ about: `a.mul(b)` and `a.Equals(b)` because JavaScript has no operator overloading, `typeOf(v)` because `typeof` is a keyword it cannot rebind, and a **`Promise` rather than a coroutine** - resolved by the host at a barrier, which is the same contract and legal for the same reason. `Random` is asserted to produce the *same sequence* from one seed in both, which is the strongest form the shared-machinery claim takes
- [x] **scripts are instances in a world, loaded from it rather than from a flag.** `Script`, `LocalScript` and `LuaSourceContainer` are classes; `Source` names the file and `Disabled` is a tag, so a disabled script is in another archetype and the run loop never visits one. `Runtime::RunWorldScripts` runs them in **creation order** - one script may build what another expects to find - filtered by the host's role, which is Roblox's own `Script`/`LocalScript` split. Each chunk gets a `script` global naming itself, set on the *thread* rather than the state because `luaL_sandboxthread` gives each chunk its own globals. **The source is a path and not the text**, which is the v0.6 shape: an unbounded string is not trivially copyable, so it could not be a column or cross a bus, and `Visual::Mesh` is a `core::Name` for the same reason. The example scene now creates its own `Script` instance, so the world contains what animates it
- [x] transparency's **sorted pass**. `scene::OrderForDrawing` partitions a draw list into an opaque head and a transparent tail sorted back-to-front from the eye, and the renderer draws the two as two ranges of one buffer through `first_instance` - a second pipeline with blending on and **depth writes off but the depth test on**, so a pane is hidden by a wall in front of it and does not erase the pane behind it. An **index order rather than an in-place sort**, because the consumer holds a span of a view it does not own. A test putting three panes at one distance caught that a stable sort does not fix a tail that was filled backwards, which is what makes "equal distances keep world order" true and a recording of coincident faces replay. `Visible` and `Transparency == 1` stay different facts
- [x] **`mono.engine/graph` at L9, and the pipeline it describes.** The module is `shared`, which is load-bearing: a headless host publishing a view culls it with the same arithmetic. **The split is what makes any of it testable** - a frustum test is six dot products and can be asserted against, a draw call needs a GPU and can only be looked at. `Frustum` extracts six planes from the view-projection matrix rather than from a camera's fields, so it cannot disagree with what was actually projected; `Cull` produces an index list that composes with the ordering; `FitDirectionalLight` frames the shadow map, fitted to the scene's bounding **sphere** so the map does not resize as the sun turns and make its own edges crawl; `Pipeline` is the stage list with the one check that matters at five passes - a stage reading a target nothing earlier wrote, which on a GPU is a frame lit by whatever was in that memory rather than a crash. `RENDER_PIPELINE.md` is not in this repository - see the note at the top of this file - so "stages 1 to 7" could not be followed by number and the line's own text was built to instead
- [x] **shadows.** A depth-only pass from the light, over the **whole** draw list rather than the culled one - a caster off screen still shadows into it, and culling to the eye is the classic version of that bug. Front faces are culled into the map so self-shadowing acne lands behind the surface that would show it, and the fragment bias is **slope-scaled**: a constant one large enough to stop an edge-on surface shadowing itself is large enough to detach every shadow from its caster. Four taps in a rotated square, which is a soft edge rather than a filter - the difference between four and one is most of what a viewer notices and the difference between four and sixteen is not. Only the opaque range casts, because a transparent pane writing full depth would cast a solid shadow
- [x] **surface camera.** `scene::SurfaceCamera` on a camera entity makes it render into a texture instead of onto the screen; `Visual::Surface` on a part names which one it shows. **`world::ViewChannel`'s shape with a texture on the far end.** The surface pass runs before the screen pass, so what a mirror shows is *this* frame. **The two ping-ponged textures buy legality and not recursion, and this line claimed both until the claim produced a visible bug**: binding a render target as its own sampler is undefined behaviour, and writing one while binding the other fixes that - but a mirror inside a mirror never happened, because surface instances are partitioned out of the surface pass and so no mirror is ever drawn into a mirror's texture. What the recursion story did produce was `Flags.z` being set for the whole surface pass rather than for the mirrors in it, so the *floor* sampled the previous frame's reflection and showed the clear colour wherever that projection landed on untouched texels - a black wedge in the pane, found by eye. Real recursion needs the pass to exclude only the surface it is rendering for, which is a per-view exclusion this pipeline has no shape for and the render-node system's job. The projection is **planar** - right for a flat pane, wrong for anything curved - and the narrowness is the design rather than a shortcut: a general reflection needs a cube map or a screen-space trace and neither belongs in a pipeline this size. The surface pass renders the whole scene minus the surfaces themselves, because a mirror sits between its own reflection camera and the world and would otherwise fill its texture with itself
- [x] **`mono.engine/examples/Mirrors-1-world.luau` and `.ts`** - written, staged and loaded. `Rings` proves the *loading* path; this proves the **rendering** one: a floor for shadows to fall on, twenty-four casters that move so both the shadows and the reflection are legible, a mirror pane with a reflected camera behind it, and a separate camera to watch through - which is the distinction `ActiveCamera` exists for. `engine.examples.scene` asserts what a test can without a GPU: that the scene builds the inputs each pass needs, and that the reflected camera really is the eye mirrored through the plane. On the GPU it runs at **60.2 fps with five draw calls and one surfaced instance**, against three for `Rings`
- [x] **`Demo.hpp`/`Demo.cpp` die here.** `BuildDemoWorld` is deleted and the client has one path: `--script`, defaulting to `Rings.luau`. Keeping the C++ scene beside the Luau one would have been two ways to do one job - and only one of them exercises the bindings. The files are `Scene.hpp`/`Scene.cpp` now, holding what is genuinely the client's half: `DrawList`, `CollectInstances`, `MoveCamera` and the loader. `Spin` and `Orbit` went with it, because a scripted scene writes `CFrame` directly
- [x] **`RaycastParams` and `RaycastResult`**, as `workspace:Raycast(origin, direction, params)` in both languages. Against **`physics::Raycast` and the exact shapes**, not `spatial::Raycast` and the proxy boxes - a ray reporting the bounding box of a rotated part would be plausible and wrong, which is the failure `physics/Query.hpp` says that function exists for. The direction carries the distance, as Roblox's does, and the unit split happens at the binding rather than being lost. A miss is **nil, not a result with a flag**, for the reason `core::RayHit` carries no validity field. `RaycastParams` filters on a **collision group** rather than on `FilterDescendantsInstances`: the engine has a bit test where Roblox has a per-ray walk of a subtree, and the omission is stated rather than silent. Opens the one edge from `script` to a simulation module, and it is one-way
- [x] Flamegraph `BUSY` and `IDLE` work separation.
- [x] Line count tooling for C++ and Header files.

Deferred:
- D00017 - **new, and older than every version that missed it.** The hosting half of L12 has carried a `TODO(v0.2+)` in `mono.engine/CMakeLists.txt` since v0.0, naming a version three releases back. It has no caller - `--worlds N` is a harness, not a hosting layer - so it is filed against a trigger rather than re-versioned, and the marker now reads `TODO(D00017)` the way `net::Handshake`'s already reads `TODO(D00006)`
- D00016 - **new, filed by this version rather than found by a later one, and then closed far enough to matter.** A frame is described twice: `Renderer::Render` submits five passes by name and `graph::StandardPipeline` is the same five as data. `render::PassOrder` now names the renderer's half and `tests/Passes.cpp` compares the two in order, so the sentence in `mono.engine/render/AGENTS.md` became a check - **proved by mutation**, a sixth stage added to the pipeline alone fails with the count and the entry number. `PassRecorder` checks the submission order at runtime, which is the half no headless test can see. What is left open is that the frame still does not *run* from the list, which is the render-node system and stays v0.8's
- D00002 - **the graph renderer, half-moved.** `mono.engine/graph/` exists at L9 with the *description* of a frame - `Frustum`, `Cull`, `Shadow`, `Pipeline`, `Validate` - and none of the execution: no nodes, no capabilities, no cache, no compiler, no executor. `render` calls into it as functions. Three of the entry's four bullets were false by the end of this version and are corrected in place; its trigger is stated for the first time and is v0.8's extended pipeline, which is the first line that cannot be met by making the function longer
- D00004 - **the old trigger fired and the narrowed one turned out to be phrased wrong.** The demo died here, so "does anything still need `core::Random`" could be asked, and the answer is yes: `Random.new(seed)` is bound into both VMs, which is the first *userland* consumer. Separately, `mono.tools/bindings` is the shape the narrowed trigger named - links `core`, neither `net` nor `assets` - and it pays **zero CryptoPP symbols**, because linking `core` was never the thing that costs; *calling* `Random` is. Trigger re-phrased against the call graph rather than the link line
- D00001 - **`Vector2` shipped**, which the entry had recorded as considered and refused on the grounds that only an overlay or an editor would want it. `UDim2` and `Rect` are made of it. The `AABB` half closed exactly as predicted, in `graph::Cull`. macOS SPIR-V→MSL is now the entry's only open bullet and still has no trigger, because nobody has a Mac
- D00015, D00014, D00008, D00007, D00006, D00005 - untouched, and none of their triggers fired. Nothing in this version listens on a socket, hosts a server in its own process, fires a hitscan weapon, or adds a second contributor

### v0.7

**Where this version stands.** The editor is built, drives all three programs off one game file, and has had several rounds of work since the items below were first written - a render gate, a keybind table, a world lifecycle, the shadow half of the draw list and the recording contract, all recorded here rather than left in the commits. **One item is open and it is not the editor being unfinished**: porting Roblox places waits on a class tree that does not exist yet, and is a trigger rather than a task. Everything else is closed, including the two that were left standing longest - the frame of input latency, which was an ordering problem rather than a setting, and the server-view/client-view split, which needed `RunMode::Play` to grow the client half it had been claiming to have.

- [x] **`mono.studio`, and `RunService:IsStudio()` is true in it and in nothing else.** `script/Runtime.hpp` has carried that sentence since v0.6 with the default set to `false`, on the grounds that editor-only behaviour must never appear in a shipped game because a default was optimistic. `Editor::BeginRun` is the one place in the repository that sets it, and the three programs disagree the way they should: the studio reports `studio = true, server = true`, `server --game` reports `studio = false`, and `client --game` reports `studio = false` while being both halves. The `game -> universe, workspace -> the world this script runs on` mapping needed no new code at all - `Services.cpp` established it at v0.6 and the explorer draws exactly that tree, so what an author clicks and what a script reaches through `game` are one object rather than two models kept in step
- [x] **the game file, and it is a different format from a snapshot on purpose.** `mono.engine/game` at L10 `shared`: a `<Game>` holds a `<Universe>` and one `<World>` per subworld, each with its instance tree and its script text. `world::Universe::Save` already writes a binary snapshot and keeps working - that is a *running* universe frozen mid-tick, same-build only, and restoring one into another build was never promised. A game file is authored content: it has to survive an engine version, read in a diff, merge between two people and be repairable by hand. **Nothing in it writes an entity id**; an instance carries a document-local `id` used only to resolve references inside the same file, which is rule 4 arriving at a save format. **Properties equal to their class default are not written**, so a scene of a thousand parts is a file somebody can read the diff of - and the consequence is stated rather than hidden: a file loaded by a later build picks up that build's defaults for anything it did not name, which is the behaviour you want and the choice Roblox made
- [x] **the format is written against `ecs::Classes` and has no schema of its own.** The properties a document holds are the ones a class declares, so adding a property to a class adds it to the save file with nothing in `game` changing. Two names are special-cased and both are structural - `Name` is an attribute and `Parent` is the nesting - and both live in one function. That is the same property the properties panel has, for the same reason, and it is why `mono.studio` never mentions `Part`
- [x] **safe XML, and the subset is the feature.** No DTD, so no billion laughs and no quadratic blowup; no external entities, so no XXE; no processing instructions, no namespaces, no schema. What is left is elements, attributes, text, CDATA and the five predefined entities, which is everything a save file needs and nothing an exploit does. A refusal is **counted apart from a parse error** for the reason `assets::Grant` counts forged tokens apart from expired ones - a document that tried to declare an entity is somebody trying something, and burying it in "malformed" means nobody sees it. `mono.engine/game/tests/Xml.cpp` is mostly negative cases and every one of them is a named attack. The `]]>` split in the writer is the other half: a program containing that sequence is ordinary code, and a writer that emitted it raw would drop the rest of the script into the document as markup
- [x] **script text lives in the world.** `script/Instances.hpp` predicted this field and named this version: `Source::Path` is a `core::Name` because a component must be trivially copyable to be a column, so a game file carrying paths would be a game file that does not contain the game. `script::SourceCache` is a world resource keyed by that same path and consulted **before** the filesystem, which is the arrangement `scene::SurfaceTable` already uses. `ReadSource` is the one function that knows both, so a studio's unsaved edit is what runs - a second resolver would be a second place to forget the cache, and the symptom would be edited code that runs from one entry point and not another
- [x] **explorer and properties, both driven by reflection and neither naming a class.** The explorer draws universe → worlds → instances with selection, drag-and-drop reparenting, insert, duplicate, delete and a fuzzy filter. The properties panel walks `ClassInfo::Properties` and groups by **which class in the ancestry first declares each one** - Instance / PVInstance / BasePart / Part - which is both the right grouping and impossible to forget to update, because Roblox's hand-maintained category field does not exist here and adding one would be a second declaration of what the class tree already knows. An `Enum` property is a combo over `EnumTable::MembersOf`, so `Material = "Plsatic"` is impossible rather than caught
- [x] **the user interface, and Dear ImGui was vendored for this since v0.0.** `mono.engine/ui` at L12 `client` is the only target in the repository that links it. It is **not** in `render`, and the edge runs one way: `ui` implements `render::FrameOverlayHook` - two virtual calls and an opaque handle - so a shipped client links a renderer that knows an interface layer *could* exist and pays nothing for the one it does not have. The question "do we remove imgui now we are on Vulkan" had a short answer: this renderer is not on Vulkan, it is on **SDL's GPU API**, and `MonoVendor.cmake` has been building `imgui_impl_sdl3.cpp` and `imgui_impl_sdlgpu3.cpp` - the exact backend pair - since v0.0 with nothing linking them. **The hand-rolled `OverlayImage` panels stay**: `Overlay.hpp` says they have to work when the renderer is the thing being debugged, and an imgui panel is drawn by the pipeline it is reporting on
- [x] **`render::Viewport`, so the world is projected for the rectangle it lands in.** A studio puts panels down the sides, so the world occupies a rectangle that is neither the window's size nor its shape. Drawing full-window and letting the panels cover the edges would project for an aspect ratio nothing is displayed at, changing every time somebody drags a splitter. The rectangle sets the viewport, the scissor, **the projection and the cull frustum**, and those last two travelling together is the point - one aspect resolved once, because two copies is two chances to disagree and the symptom is geometry popping at the screen edge on one machine and not another. A sixth pass, `interface`, joins `render::PassOrder` and `graph::StandardPipeline` together, because `tests/Passes.cpp` compares the two and D00016 exists to keep them comparable
- [x] **the script editor**, as tabs over `script::SourceCache` with a save that files a path for a script that has none. Two imgui lessons are written into it in comments rather than left to be relearned: a widget's id comes from its label, so appending a modified marker made the tab a *different tab* and dropped keyboard focus after exactly one character; and `io.WantTextInput` is set during a frame, so the menu bar's Ctrl+S - drawn first - always saw `false` and fired alongside the editor's. Deferred and named: no syntax highlighting and no gutter, because a monospace font is a file this repository does not have and a licence somebody has to choose
- [x] **run (server-only) and play (server+client), with the restore people forget.** Pressing Play takes a snapshot, runs the game, and pressing Stop *restores it* - so an author's scene is not whatever their scripts left behind. `Universe::Save` and `Load` are exactly that operation and had existed since v0.2 with no caller that needed them; this is the first. **Script buffers are flushed into their worlds before the snapshot is taken**, and getting that order wrong deletes whatever the author had just typed - which is what it did, once. Trying to snapshot also found `client::DrawList` had never been registered at all: `SetResource` was minting it under the compiler's spelling of the type, rule 4's exact failure, invisible because nothing had ever saved a world that had one
- [x] **one game file, three programs.** `studio` writes it, `server --game FILE.agame` hosts every world in it and serves the first, and `client --game FILE.agame` plays it single-player with `HostRole::OfBoth`. What "running a game" means is one function - `game::StartWorldScripts` - because three copies would be three places for the heartbeat's delta to become wall time. **`mono.client` gained `Engine::game` and not `Mono::server`**: playing a game file needs the format and a VM, not a hosted server, so the escape `mono.client/CMakeLists.txt` reserves is *still* not declared. `mono.studio` is the second declared user of D00008's escape and the first that is a product rather than a diagnostic, and `expected_graph.json` is where both facts are visible
- [x] **importing and exporting one world**, as a `.aworld` beside the `.agame`. Different root elements and different extensions, and each reader refuses the other document rather than half-reading it - opening a game as a world gets you an empty world, which is a lost afternoon. `ImportWorld` takes a rename because importing the same world twice is a real thing an author does and two worlds cannot share a name

- [x] **a viewport panel, and the reason the obvious version does not work.** The world is rendered into a `render::SceneTarget` and the panel shows it with `ImGui::Image`. The cheap alternative - draw to the swapchain and put a background-less imgui window over it - works until that window is *docked*, which is the only way anybody uses it: `imgui.cpp`'s `central_node_hole` is only punched while the central node is **empty**, so docking a panel into it makes the dockspace fill its whole rectangle with `ImGuiCol_WindowBg` and paint over the frame. It was built the cheap way first and the bug was reported as "when viewport is docked, doesn't render", which is exactly what it was. imgui records its draw lists before the renderer runs, so the panel binds whatever texture exists at that moment - but the world pass and the interface pass share one command buffer with the world first, so a texture that keeps its **identity** across the frame is written before it is sampled. That identity is the whole game, and getting it wrong is what "the viewport lags while resizing" was: a target allocated to the panel's exact size is a *new* texture on every frame of a drag, and the bind imgui already recorded then points at the outgoing one - so the panel spent the drag showing a stale frame stretched to a shape it was never drawn for, at a flat 16.7 ms throughout. No profiler can see that; it costs nothing. Scene targets are allocated in **64-pixel blocks** with a 2x hysteresis before shrinking, so the texture survives a drag, the pass confines the world to its corner with `SDL_SetGPUViewport` - the renderer had never set one, relying on the default covering the whole target - and `SceneTextureExtent` hands the panel the UVs to sample only what was drawn. `render::Viewport` was added and deleted inside this version; it was the wrong primitive
- [x] **the studio's latency, which is not its frame rate.** Measured with `--profile-snapshot`: ~0.8 ms of CPU work in a 16.67 ms frame, and `--uncapped` runs the same scene at **0.212 ms p50**, so the editor was never CPU-bound and resizing never cost a millisecond. What it cost was frames of *delay*, from two places. SDL's GPU device defaults to **2 frames in flight** - its own header says higher values "increase throughput at the expense of visual latency" - and nothing had ever changed it, so the picture was up to two frames behind the mouse; the device now asks for 1. `--uncapped` joins the studio from the client, off by default, because a viewport spinning as fast as the GPU allows is a laptop with its fans up for a still picture
- [x] **the frame of input latency, and it was an ordering problem exactly as this line predicted.** `Editor::Run` pumped events and *then* blocked the better part of a frame inside `Renderer::Render` waiting on the swapchain, so every frame was built from input that was already a frame old - and no amount of speed between the two would have closed it, which is why the 0.8 ms measurement was consistent with a visibly laggy editor. `Renderer::WaitForFrame` is the wait, called first; `Render` records into the frame it already claimed. **The split is what makes it possible at all**: a swapchain image cannot be acquired without a command buffer and the wait *is* the acquisition, so there is no ordering inside one call that puts it after the caller's input. **The signature did not have to change** - the prediction here was that it would. `Render` claims the frame itself when nobody claimed it first, and the claim is idempotent within a frame, so an existing caller is correct without knowing this exists and two acquisitions in one frame are impossible. `mono.client` calls it too, because it had the same shape and the same frame of delay
- [x] **a frame that is waited for and never drawn is submitted rather than leaked.** Quitting during the event pump is the case, and SDL documents cancelling a command buffer *after* a swapchain texture has been acquired as an error - so `Shutdown` submits an empty frame instead. The loop's own shape makes it unreachable, which is precisely why it is handled: "unreachable" and "guaranteed" are different words, and a swapchain image held past the device's destruction is a crash inside the backend rather than an error anybody can read
- [x] **a Worlds panel - the scene manager.** Every world with its state and its instance count, a click to choose which one is being edited, and New / Duplicate / Rename / Export / Remove. Duplicate and rename go through the save format rather than through a new mechanism: `game::WriteWorldDocument` and `ReadWorldDocument` are `ExportWorld` and `ImportWorld` with the file taken out, so an editor action and a file on disk cannot disagree about what a world is. **Rename is refused while a game is running and `world::Universe` still has no rename**, because a world's name is what a bus envelope, a subscription and a teleport carry - renaming a live world strands its traffic and nothing would report it. In Edit mode there is none, and `Universe::Adopt` reuses the hole a destroyed world leaves, so the scene keeps its handle and its place in the list
- [x] **every panel is a panel.** Dockable, tear-off, resizable, closable, with a View menu to bring back what was closed and a Reset Layout for when it has gone wrong. The dockspace id carries a version, because imgui owns the layout once it has written its ini - which is right, and means a panel added later is a window the saved layout has never heard of and opens floating in a corner. Both new panels did exactly that before the version existed
- [x] **headless rendering, and it is a device with no window rather than a hidden one.** `Renderer::Initialise(nullptr)` claims nothing: no swapchain, nothing presented, no overlay and no interface pass - and the world still drawn, into the scene target. A hidden window still owns a swapchain, and whether one can be acquired for a window nobody can see is a per-platform answer nobody should have to know. The colour format then has two answers, so it gained exactly one asker - `Impl::ColourFormat` - because a second call to `SDL_GetGPUSwapchainTextureFormat` is a pipeline built for one target and bound to another
- [x] **`studio --headless --frames N --run play --capture PATH`**, which is the editor driven by something that is not a person. `Renderer::RequestSceneCapture` downloads the scene texture through a fence and writes a BMP. `just studio-smoke` loads a game, starts it, renders it and writes the result with **no display at all** - which is how the run cycle above was verified while the machine's screen was busy. Deliberately not part of `just check`: it needs a GPU, and a build container without one would fail a check about the editor for a reason that is not about the editor
- [x] **two scenes in two viewports, and the default template is what makes it the normal case.** `Editor::NewGame` opens *two* worlds - `SkyGrid` running `SkyGrid.luau` and `Mirrors` running `Mirrors-1-world.luau` - with a viewport pinned to each, the tree expanded and the Worlds panel in front. A one-world template was teaching the single-scene habit the rest of this engine is built against: the universe holds subworlds, `ExecutionMode::WorldParallel` ticks them alongside each other, and a viewport per world is what makes that visible rather than asserted. The two scenes are opposites on purpose - the skygrid is sparse geometry over empty sky, so most of the frustum is background and the cull has something to reject (`174 culled` of 196 blocks on the frame this was measured), while the mirror is a surface camera and a texture sampled back over a floor. **A viewport with no world of its own follows the active one**, so pinning is what stops two panels showing one scene twice; `ViewportState::World` is that pin. `SkyGrid.luau` is new and is the first example written for the *renderer's* sparse case rather than for a feature
- [x] **the Server-view and the Client-view, and `RunMode::Play` stopped being one half wearing both hats.** Play has claimed since it was written to be "both halves in one process, the arrangement `HostRole::OfBoth` describes" - and it ran one world with `IsServer()` and `IsClient()` both true and no replica anywhere, so nothing a client would actually *see* was ever produced. `studio::PlayLink` is the other half: an `Authority` over the world being played and a `Replica` of it in a second world, **with nothing in between them** - no datagram, no header, no cipher, no window, which is `mono.unified_server_client`'s arrangement reused rather than a second way to stand two halves up in one process. A viewport is pinned to it when Play starts, so the two are side by side by default; the Worlds panel marks it and carries the numbers a picture cannot give - entities each side, the last complete tick, bytes and the largest message. **A second world rather than a second view of one, and that is the whole design**: two views of one store show the same rows twice, and the only thing worth looking at here is where the two *differ* - a value that never crossed because nothing marked it dirty, a creation the client has not been told about
- [x] **and the ordering that makes it work is the opposite of a server's, which cost a failing test to find.** `mono.server` publishes straight after ticking and loses nothing, because on a server every write happens inside a system. **In a studio they do not** - dragging a part, typing into the properties panel, deleting an instance are all writes *between* two ticks, and a world clears its change bits at the start of one. Publishing after the tick therefore drops every edit an author makes, silently, and the symptom is the server view moving while the client view sits still: replication looking broken when the mistake is a line's position. The studio publishes **before** the tick, which catches the previous tick's system writes and everything done to the world since, and the tick then clears exactly what was sent
- [x] **a game file does not contain a replica, and the check for it is asked of the store.** `game::WriteGame` walked every world in the universe, which was right until a universe could hold a world that is a *view* of a game rather than part of one - the file would have carried a second copy of the scene under a second name, and loading it would give an author two scenes where they had made one. It skips a world whose store is `AdoptOnly`, which is already set on every replica for exactly this reason: the rows are not this process's to mint. Asked of the store rather than passed in by each caller, because a flag threaded through three programs is the same fact recorded three times - and `mono.client --game` gets it right for free
- [x] **`studio.playlink` - six cases, and the one that matters asserts the rows are on the other side.** The client half is deliberately the part of Play a headless test can reach: what a viewport shows needs a GPU, whether there is anything correct *to* show does not. It caught the publish ordering above, and it caught a claim in its own header - `Applied == 0` was documented as "the join never finished", and a run publishes before its world has ticked, so a correct join of an unchanging world leaves it at zero for ever. Positions are asserted against the wire's **documented** quantisation bound rather than for equality: `D00015` (a) puts a position on a fixed-point grid, so 1.0 arrives as 1.000030518, and a tolerance fitted to what a run happened to produce would stop being a check the moment the grid moved
- [x] **worlds open and close themselves, and "is anybody in it" had to be given a meaning before any of it could be written.** There is no player instance in this program, so occupancy is an editor-owned token - `PlayerWorld` - moved by the Worlds panel's `Teleport Player Here`. A world with nobody in it goes to `Suspended` after `IdleCloseSeconds`, defaulting to **300 s, which is Roblox's own place-shutdown grace** and long enough that an author who walked off to build in another subarea does not come back to a stopped clock; `AutoManageWorlds` turns the policy off, because a world that keeps closing under somebody debugging it is the one case whose right answer is a switch. Four rules are load-bearing and each was arrived at rather than assumed. **The wake condition is the destination's inbox, not the teleport call** - `TeleportPlayer` resumes a suspended world before addressing it, but a script's own teleport goes through the bus, and a suspended world is the one world whose inbox nothing drains, so anything sitting in it is by construction traffic waiting on a closed world. A running world replaces its inbox every barrier and a poll would race the tick; a suspended one holds what arrived, which is what makes this reliable rather than lucky. **Being looked at is activity, in either viewport** - and forgetting the second one was a real bug: with two panels open, the world that was not the active scene was closed under the panel showing it, which reads exactly like the second viewport being broken. **A scoped run's suspended worlds are not the lifecycle's to wake**, because a world outside the run scope is empty *because* somebody stopped it, and an idle timer restarting it is the editor arguing with the play button. And **never the last world**, since a universe with everything suspended is a game that has stopped without saying so. None of it runs in Edit mode, where no world ticks and suspending one only leaves a state the author has to put back by hand
- [x] **the simulation runs parallel and the drawing is sequential, and that is decided rather than defaulted.** `Universe::Tick` has run worlds under `ExecutionMode::WorldParallel` since v0.2; what was missing was anything that *showed* it, which the two-world template now is - `NewGame` sets the mode explicitly rather than inheriting it, because a universe that had loaded a game setting something else would keep that setting and the template would quietly stop demonstrating the thing it exists to demonstrate. **Drawing stays sequential: one `Renderer::Render` per viewport, one after another.** Parallel recording buys nothing here - the passes share one command buffer and one device, so the work would serialise at submit - and it would cost the thing that makes the viewport correct, which is that the world pass writes a scene target before the interface pass samples it in the same buffer. Revisit when a viewport is expensive enough that its *record* is measurable; at the sizes measured it is not
- [x] **the tree decides what renders, and before this nothing did.** `mono.engine/scene/Visibility.hpp`, and the hole it closes is embarrassing in the way only load-bearing defaults are: every query that built a draw list matched `<Transform, PreviousTransform, Bounds, Visual>` - a *component* test - so a part was drawn because of what it was made of and never because of where it sat. A template under `StarterGui`, a part in `ReplicatedStorage`, an orphan a script had created and not yet parented: all complete parts by that test, all on screen. **`Visual::Visible` was declared, bound, saved and reloaded, and no draw path read it either.** Roblox's rule is restored - a `BasePart` renders exactly when it descends from `Workspace`, and everything else in the tree is storage. It is a **tag component and not a branch**, for two reasons that agree: `client::CollectInstances` runs `EachBatchParallel` and writes `out[first + row]`, so a `continue` would leave holes and make the returned count a lie, and compacting instead wants a parallel prefix sum to avoid work the storage gives away free - and it is the ECS's own argument, the one `RigidBody` already makes, that you componentise what you iterate. **Derived by a scan and not maintained at the write**, because ancestry is not local: parenting one model moves everything beneath it, and the alternative is hooking `SetParent`, the explorer's drag, the game loader, `InstallServices` and `DestroyInstance` - where missing one gives a part that draws after being deleted from the scene, with the symptom appearing in the renderer a very long way from the reparent that caused it. Mark-and-sweep over a byte the sweep is already touching, rather than a `std::set` allocated and thrown away every tick, and **a mark rather than a monotonic stamp because the component reaches a snapshot**. It runs in `PreRender` rather than `PostSimulation`, which is the choice with a trap on the other side of it: **a world can present without ticking** - the studio suspends a world and edits it - so a gate maintained by the simulation would leave a part dragged into `Workspace` invisible until somebody pressed play. That makes it the one structural change in `PreRender`, which is safe only because nothing in the simulation reads `Rendered` and every host derives it identically from the same tree, so `just determinism` still compares equal. **A replica does not use it at all**: ancestry is the one thing the wire does not carry - `Hierarchy` holds `Entity` handles that mean nothing across two directories - and it does not need to, because the authority has already applied the filter, so `client::CollectReplicated` honours `Visual::Visible` directly, which is the half that does arrive
- [x] **`Visual::CastShadow`, and the draw-list arithmetic moved to where a test can reach it.** A part can be excluded from the shadow pass without being excluded from the frame - the property is bound in both languages, in the manifest and both declaration files, and it crosses a save. The interesting half is `scene::ScenePlan` and `OrderScene`: the renderer used to compute its own mirror partition and caster partition inline, and **those counts are `first_instance` and a draw count - the part of a pass easiest to get wrong by one and impossible to see in a screenshot.** A shadow range short by one loses a caster somewhere off screen and the frame looks fine. It is arithmetic over a `shared` type, so it belongs beside `OrderForDrawing` where a headless suite can assert on it, and the renderer is left with the part that genuinely needs a GPU. **The shadow pass draws two ranges rather than one, and that is forced rather than chosen**: the surface pass needs the non-mirrors contiguous from zero and the shadow pass needs the casters contiguous, one partition cannot give both, so the casters are partitioned *within* each run - the mirror half being zero in every scene with no mirror in it. Every partition is stable, for this file's standing reason: an opaque scene must come out exactly as it went in or a recording stops replaying
- [x] **two viewports were thrashing the driver's allocator, and it did not stay inside this process.** One shared depth texture is correct for one viewport and catastrophic for two: the panels are different sizes, so each `Render` found the other's dimensions and reallocated - a colour-sized D32 texture destroyed and created **twice per frame, every frame**. That is what "resizing makes my whole desktop lag" was, and it is not a frame-rate symptom, which is why nothing in `--profile-snapshot` had shown it. Each scene slot owns its depth buffer now, sized to the block-rounded allocation rather than the drawn rectangle because SDL requires colour and depth dimensions to match, and released outright rather than retired - nothing samples a depth buffer, so no recorded interface bind can be pointing at it and the grace period the colour target needs does not apply. **The depth format is chosen at start-up instead of hard-coded**, which was the second bug in the same file: `SDL_gpu.h` says outright that only D16_UNORM is guaranteed and to check which of D24/D32 exists, and D32_FLOAT was written in four places. On the first GPU without it the texture creation fails, the frame is dropped and nothing says why - a black window. Asked once, for both usages at once, because the shadow map is sampled as well as targeted and a second format would be a second thing to keep in step with a pipeline
- [x] **one table naming every key in the editor, and rebinding is why it exists rather than a `switch`.** `input::Actions` opens with "the one table in the engine that names a key; everything else asks for an action" and the editor had drifted from it - F5 was spelled out in `DrawShortcuts`, again in the Run menu's label and a third time in a comment. The editor cannot use that table: it is SDL keycodes through a polled `ActionState`, and the editor's keys are read through imgui, which owns the keyboard while a text field has focus and must go on owning it. So it is the same idea over `ImGuiKey`, deliberately a second table, with a Keybinds page that searches, rebinds, reports the conflict it is about to take and resets. **The part that could not have been left out is `Scope`** - Global, Viewport, Tree, Script - because without it the only available guard is `io.WantCaptureKeyboard`, which answers "is a text field focused" and not "does this key mean anything here": Delete belongs to the tree and the viewport and is a character in the script editor, so a binding on a plain letter could never have been added safely at all. `ShowStatistics` and `ShowFrameGraph` ship **unbound** rather than moved to a different pair of function keys, because F5 is Play in an editor and always will be, and inventing a second set is a decision for whoever wants them - which is what the page is for
- [x] **and now it is enforced rather than recorded.** The decision above was a paragraph in this file and nothing else: a second viewport recording from a worker would have produced a driver validation error or a frame of somebody else's geometry, neither of which points back at the thread that caused it. `Renderer::Render` and `Shutdown` abort unless they are on the thread that called `Initialise`, which is `ecs::Store::RequireOwningThread`'s shape and its argument - by the time a second thread is inside, it has already recorded into a buffer another thread is filling, so there is nothing left to decline and the stack at the violation is the whole value. **The owner is claimed by the constructor and re-claimed by `Initialise`, and that is what makes it testable**: `engine.render.passes` exercises the contract with no GPU, on a build machine that has none. Deliberately **no `BindToCallingThread`** beside the store's - a store is picked up by a different worker every tick and a device is not, so a public rebind would be a seam for exactly what this forbids. `render/AGENTS.md` says it too now, and says it as a contract rather than as a note
- [x] **the sixth pass had a half-updated check behind it, found while writing the one above.** `tests/Passes.cpp` compares `render::PassOrder` against `graph::StandardPipeline` by count and by order, and that comparison did hold when `interface` arrived - it is generic. The *names* test beside it is not: it spells out five passes by hand and `interface` was never added, so it was the one pass whose name nothing checked. The file's own header had gone stale the same way, still describing "five passes". Both fixed, and the count is now deliberately not written down in that file - it said "five" until v0.7 made it false, which is the failure the file exists to prevent happening one level up inside it

Found while building this, and fixed here rather than filed:

- **`scene::Visual` had a hand-written serialiser that two later fields never reached.** `Transparency` and `Surface` were added after `WriteVisuals` was written and neither crossed a snapshot, so a glass pane turned solid the first time a world was saved and reopened. Invisible for an opaque part, which is why it survived three versions. A field added to a type with a *generated* serialiser crosses for free; one added to a hand-written pair crosses only if somebody remembers, and nothing in the build checks - `mono.client/tests/Presentation.cpp` is the cheapest test for that whole class of bug and now covers it
- **WASD stopped working the moment you clicked on the picture, and the guard that killed it was the keyboard twin of a bug this same function had already fixed for the mouse.** The camera's fly keys were gated on `!io.WantCaptureKeyboard`, with a comment claiming imgui raises that "while any text field has focus". It does not - `imgui.cpp` raises it while *navigation* is active: `io.NavActive && (ConfigFlags & NavEnableKeyboard) && io.ConfigNavCaptureKeyboard`. `ui::Interface` sets `NavEnableKeyboard` and the second defaults to true, so **focusing a viewport set the very flag that switched its camera off**. Twelve lines above it sits the note about `hovered && !io.WantCaptureMouse` never being satisfiable, because hovering an imgui window is what raises `WantCaptureMouse` - the identical mistake, in the same function, on the other device, and the fix for one did not go looking for the other. The guard is `io.WantTextInput` now, which is the question that was meant and the one `DrawShortcuts` already asks. `F` to frame carried the same guard and was dead in the same circumstances
- **And the camera now follows focus as well as the pointer, which needed two facts where there had been one.** A viewport clicked into and then left by the mouse got nothing, because every gate was `hovered || active`. Routing WASD by `FocusedViewport` alone would have flown the camera while somebody typed into the properties panel - that field deliberately *keeps* naming the last viewport when focus moves elsewhere, so the transport readout does not blank on every click. `FocusedIsViewport` is the second fact, resolved from the same window in the same place, so the two cannot disagree: the mouse follows the pointer, the keyboard follows focus, and neither answers for a panel the keyboard is not in
- **Auto-pinning a client view per Play run was over-reach, and `--run play` is what showed it.** That flag starts every world in the game, so "one panel per run" opened a viewport per *world* - three or four pictures nobody asked for, each taking a slice of the centre pane and a turn in `PresentWorld`'s round robin, so every view also refreshed a third as often. One client view is the feature; the rest are reachable from the scene selector like any other world
- **The replica's draw path dropped the tail of `Visual` - the same silent loss as the serialiser above, arriving through a different door.** `CollectReplicated` copied the first few fields and left `Transparency`, `Surface` and `CastShadow` at their defaults, so a glass pane replicated as solid and a mirror replicated as a plain part. Two hand-written copies of one struct's fields is two places to forget, and neither forgetting is visible on an opaque part - which is why the pair of them survived together
- **`client::DrawList` was unregisterable, and the abort landed in a world that had never heard of it.** `ecs::Components::Of<T>` caches its answer per type per process, so **the first mention of a type anywhere decides its name** - and in the replicated world that first mention was a `SetResource` rather than `RegisterClientComponents`. The world built next then tried to register `client.DrawList` properly and aborted, naming a type its own function never mentions. Rule 4's failure mode exactly: the compiler's spelling wins by arriving first, and the diagnosis surfaces one caller away from the cause. `BuildScriptedWorld` and `InstallPresentation` had both opened with the registration call for this reason; this path was the gap
- **`game:GetService("Workspace")` found nothing, and an orphan claimed to be in the world.** The global is spelled `workspace` and the service lookup went through `lua_getglobal`, so asking for the service by its class name - which is what a Roblox script does - was refused for an object the engine plainly provides. It resolves from the registry now, so the global, `game.Workspace` and `GetService` are **one instance rather than three handles that compare equal**. Separately, a null `Parent` used to hand back `workspace`, which made every orphan look like it was in the scene - the answer is `nil`, and that is where "an orphan is not in the world" starts being true
- **`scene::CapturePreviousTransforms` moved into `scene`.** It was five lines inside `examples::LoadScene` and the studio needed the same five. Two copies of a *system* is worse than two copies of a helper: both can be installed into one world and the second wins silently every tick

Deferred, and named rather than discovered:

- **One VM per world, and it is Luau.** A world whose scripts are JavaScript runs nothing in the studio, the server's game-file path or single-player. `script::LanguageOf` picks per file and the runtime is per world; reconciling those is a design decision rather than an oversight
- **No undo.** Every editor action is applied straight to the store. Undo needs a command log, and a half-undo covering property edits but not deletions would be worse than none
- **No viewport picking, no gizmos, no reference picker.** Selection is the explorer's; a click in the world does nothing, and a `PropertyType::Reference` shows its target's name and cannot be changed
- **Headless runs no interface backends.** The imgui context starts and its platform and renderer backends do not, so panel *drawing* is skipped - a headless run exercises the universe, the scripts, the presentation phase and the render. Driving the panels themselves without a window needs the backends replaced rather than skipped, which is the shape a scripted or agent-driven editor will take
- **A capture is the world and not the editor.** The chrome is drawn onto the swapchain and SDL does not promise that is readable. Capturing the whole window means rendering the frame into an offscreen target and blitting it, which is a change to every program's frame for a benefit only the editor has
- **Replication is still one world per connection.** A game of several scenes simulates all of them and streams the first, which the server says in its log rather than leaving a player to wonder which world they joined. The studio's client view is per *run*, so a game with two scenes playing has two of them - which is more than a real client gets, and is the editor being a diagnostic rather than a preview of what shipping looks like
- **The client view has no loss, no reordering and no latency** beyond the one step the pipeline costs. `mono.unified_server_client --drop` is the harness for those and a socket between two processes is the real thing; what this catches is the class of bug that survives a *perfect* link, which is state that is never sent at all. Adding a fault model here would make it a second network test, and there is already one
- **`scene::SurfaceTable` is not written to a game file.** Materials are registered by scripts, so a loaded world rebuilds its table when its scripts run. The moment the studio lets somebody edit a material by hand that stops being true, and `game` gains a `<Surfaces>` section

### v0.8

**The scripted interface**, which is v0.7's last question answered rather than
deferred. The ask was: should the studio's own UI be authored in TypeScript,
with the Roblox `GuiObject` tree as the widget set and React as an option? The
answer is yes, and **not by writing fifty class registrations first**.

Those classes are shims until three things exist underneath them, and a shim
that nothing draws is an API with no caller - which is the thing this repository
refuses everywhere else. In order:

- [x] **`core` gained the value types the tree is made of.** `UDim`, `UDim2`,
  `Vector2` and `Rect` were already in `core/types` and **none of them was a
  `PropertyType`** - so a component holding a `UDim2` could not be a property,
  could not be saved, could not appear in the properties panel and could not be
  set from a script. `ecs::PropertyType` is a closed list that grows when
  userland gains a value, and this was that. Four cases in `game::Values`, four
  widgets in the properties panel, four in each binding, and the manifest
  regenerated. **The `UDim` widget is two controls rather than a `DragFloat2`**,
  which is the one part that was not mechanical: scale is a fraction and offset
  is pixels, so one drag step cannot serve both - a hundredth of a pixel is a
  drag that never arrives and a whole unit of scale crosses the parent in one
  mouse movement
- [x] **`mono.engine/gui` at L7 `shared` - what a 2D thing *is*.** Thirty-three
  classes as `ecs::Classes` registrations over twenty-three real components,
  exactly as `scene` does for `Part`: `GuiBase` → `GuiBase2d` → `GuiObject` →
  `Frame`, `CanvasGroup`, `GuiButton`(`TextButton`, `ImageButton`),
  `GuiLabel`(`TextLabel`, `ImageLabel`), `ScrollingFrame`, `TextBox`,
  `ViewportFrame`; `LayerCollector` → `ScreenGui`, `BillboardGui`, `SurfaceGui`,
  `PluginGui` → `DockWidgetPluginGui`; and the nine `UIComponent` modifiers.
  `shared`, so a server authors a `ScreenGui` and replicates it. **`core` and
  `ecs` are the whole dependency list**, and the one thing that looked like a
  reason to link `scene` - `NormalId` for a surface gui - is a six-member enum
  registered by name, pinned by a test at each end, which is the arrangement
  `DefaultMaterial`'s duplicated "Plastic" already uses.
  **`Instance`, `Name` and `Parent` moved down into `ecs`** as
  `Classes::RegisterInstanceRoot`: they project components `ecs` owns and were
  only in `scene` because `scene` had the first class tree, and a second tree
  that may not link the first made one declaration necessary rather than
  tidier. **`VideoFrame` is deliberately absent** - there is no decoder, and a
  class that draws nothing forever is what the paragraph below refuses
- [x] **Layout, which is arithmetic and belongs beside the tree.** `UDim2`
  against a parent's absolute rect, `AnchorPoint`, `SizeConstraint`, and the
  `UIListLayout`/`UIGridLayout`/`UIPadding`/`UIAspectRatioConstraint` modifiers,
  plus `UISizeConstraint`, `UITextSizeConstraint`, `UIScale`, `UICorner` and
  `UIStroke`. One pass, parent before child, producing a `Resolved` component
  the renderer reads with a query. **The order the constraints apply in is
  load-bearing and was arrived at rather than assumed**: scale multiplies what
  the `UDim2` said, the aspect ratio reshapes that, and the size limits clamp
  the result - clamping first produces a rectangle that obeys neither, which
  reads as "the constraint does not work". **`Measure` is split from `Place`**
  for one reason: a list or a grid has to know how big a child is before
  deciding where it goes, and that is the only thing a single top-down sweep
  cannot do. `AutomaticSize` is declared, saved and bound and is **not**
  implemented - it needs that second phase over the subtree, and `D00021`
  carries the argument rather than leaving it to be discovered
- [x] **The compiled draw list, which was not on this list and is the thing
  that makes the rest affordable.** `gui::Compiled` computes a rolling
  signature over every field the compile reads and keeps the flattened,
  sorted, clipped `DrawList` while it matches - `scene::QuickHash`'s pattern
  and `studio::HierarchyView`'s, for the same reason both give:
  `ecs::Hierarchy` and `gui::Element` are not observed components, so
  `Store::ChangeVersion` does not move when an element is reparented or
  resized, and it *does* move when a physics tick writes a transform, which
  would rebuild the UI sixty times a second for nothing. **The rule that a
  field added to a component must be added to the fold is checked rather than
  written down**: `gui/tests/Compile.cpp` walks every property the class tree
  declares, writes each one and asserts the signature moved
- [x] **Input routing.** Hit testing back to front, `ZIndex`, `Active`,
  `Selectable`, and `InputBegan`/`InputEnded`/`Activated` plus
  `MouseEnter`/`MouseLeave`/`MouseMoved`. **The hit test is a backwards walk of
  the compiled list rather than a tree walk**, because the list is already in
  paint order - a second traversal that re-derived that order would be a second
  answer to "what is on top", and the two would disagree the first time
  somebody changed a sort in one of them. Two rules inside it were arrived at:
  `InputEnded` goes to where the press *began* rather than where the release
  happened, which is what makes dragging off a button and back one interaction;
  and `MouseLeave` fires before `MouseEnter`, so a handler that moves something
  on leave runs before the one reacting to the arrival
- [x] **`gui` drawn on screen, through the atlas that already exists.**
  `ui::PaintGui` takes a `gui::DrawList` and an `ImDrawList` and nothing else -
  rectangles with rounded corners, outlines, nine-slice, tiling, fit, crop,
  text with both alignment axes, and clipping pushed with *intersection* so a
  panel's own clip survives. The atlas decision the plan flagged came out the
  way it stated: `ui` already owns four faces and a second rasteriser over the
  same four files would be two answers to what a glyph looks like, so the edge
  runs `ui` → `gui` and the painter is the only thing that knows both. The
  studio's viewport panels compile and paint the world's own `ScreenGui` over
  the world image and under the editor's chrome, one `gui::Compiled` and one
  `gui::Router` per panel - per panel because a panel *is* a canvas, and two
  viewports at different sizes resolve every `UDim2` differently
- [x] **`mono.engine/examples/Interface.luau`, and it is the proof rather than
  a demo.** A `ScreenGui` built entirely from a script, so it exercises
  `Instance.new` over the new class tree, `UDim2` and `Vector2` as real
  property types, the layout pass, the compile and the painter in one run. Every
  element in it exists because one specific thing looks correct without it:
  the centred card catches an anchor point applied backwards, the strip's
  content is four times its own width and catches a clip that replaces rather
  than intersects, and the grid's cells catch a wrap that counts cells without
  counting the padding. **Its clock writes at ten hertz rather than sixty**,
  which is both `D00020`'s workaround and what keeps the compiled-list cache
  measurable - a scene that changed every frame would rebuild every frame and
  prove nothing about it. It is installed into the skygrid world by
  `Editor::NewGame`, so a new game shows it
- [x] **`gui` rendering as a render pass at L12, which is the half a shipped
  client needs.** `mono.client` does not link `Engine::ui` and must not -
  `ui/Interface.hpp` gives the reason, and it is the same one that keeps imgui
  out of a game binary - so a shipped client draws no `ScreenGui` until there
  is a batched quad pipeline plus a glyph atlas behind `Pass::Interface`. **The
  seam is already the right one and that is what makes this a renderer rather
  than a feature**: the second backend is a new consumer of `gui::DrawList`,
  not a second compile, so neither half can drift from the other about where an
  element is.

  **The atlas decision is made: `render` owns the four faces.** `ui` owned them
  through imgui, which was right while the editor was the only thing drawing
  text and stops being right the moment a shipped client draws a `ScreenGui`.
  `ui` already depends on `render`, so the edge runs the way it needs to and
  there is one rasteriser rather than two answers to what a glyph looks like.
  **The dependency that seemed to block it was already in the tree.**
  `imstb_truetype.h` and `imstb_rectpack.h` are standalone public-domain single
  headers `mono.vendor/imgui` bundles rather than parts of imgui - a rasteriser
  and a bin packer - so `render::GlyphAtlas` includes them through
  `VENDOR_INCLUDES` and links no imgui symbol. Nothing new was vendored and
  nothing of the editor's toolkit reaches a game binary.
  `GlyphAtlas` is built and tested headlessly, which is why it came first: a
  wrong glyph would otherwise be found by looking at a screen. Coverage rather
  than colour, so one sheet serves every colour of text; grown by powers of two
  until the pack fits rather than sized by a formula that is either wasteful or
  occasionally too small; a pixel of padding checked *in the sheet* rather than
  trusted from the constant, because atlas bleed only shows at non-integer
  scales, which is to say on somebody else's machine. Latin-1 only, and the rest
  is filed rather than pretended at.
  Found while testing it: the font staging gate named `engine_ui` alone, so a
  test of the new rasteriser linked the module that reads .ttf files and got
  none staged - every case took its "no fonts" branch and reported green
  without asserting anything. The gate names both modules now and
  `mono_add_tests` stages them too; the suite went from 8 assertions to 574.

  **`render::InterfaceMesh` is the rest of it that a device is not needed
  for**, and it is a type rather than a loop inside a recording function for
  exactly that reason: quad generation, outline construction, batching and text
  layout are arithmetic, and arithmetic found wrong headlessly is arithmetic not
  found by looking at a screen. It produces vertices, 16-bit indices and the
  runs between scissor changes.
  The decisions inside it. **Batches split on the scissor and on the texture
  and merge otherwise**, because a clip is pipeline state rather than a vertex
  attribute - two elements clipped differently cannot be one draw however alike
  their pixels are. **An outline is four quads and never a line primitive**,
  because a line's width is a device setting with no portable guarantee and one
  that is a pixel on one driver and three on another is a difference nobody
  reproduces. **A solid quad samples a white texel packed into the atlas**, so a
  filled rectangle and a glyph share one pipeline - two would be two places for
  the blend state to be set differently, which shows as panels at subtly the
  wrong opacity and nowhere else. That texel takes a rect from the packer rather
  than a corner somebody picked: `stbrp` fills the whole sheet, so a hand-picked
  texel is one a large glyph can land on, putting a letter's coverage under
  every filled rectangle - a bug that appears only once the atlas is full enough,
  which is to say on the machine with the wider font.
  **The shaders are written and compiling.** `interface.vert` and
  `interface.frag` build to SPIR-V and stage into both the client and studio
  trees. Two decisions in them. Positions arrive in **canvas pixels** and become
  clip space in the vertex shader, so the only thing that knows the target's
  size is the shader - a panel at a different resolution needs no rebuild of the
  mesh. And Y is flipped where X is not, because SDL's GPU clip space is Y-up on
  every backend while a canvas has its origin at the top left: the one asymmetry
  in the file, and the one everybody writes symmetrically the first time.
  The fragment shader is `inColour * sampled` for all three kinds, not a branch.
  The atlas's white texel is (1,1,1,1), so a rectangle multiplies its tint by
  white and gets its tint while a glyph multiplies by (1,1,1,coverage) and gets
  its tint at that coverage - branching on which would be a divergent branch per
  fragment to avoid a multiply by one.
  **`render::InterfacePass` is the recording loop, and it is a
  `FrameOverlayHook` rather than a branch inside `Renderer`.** That is the same
  slot the editor's chrome occupies, so a client hands one of these where the
  studio hands its imgui recorder and the renderer knows the difference not at
  all - adding an interface path to the renderer's own recording would have put
  a `gui` concern inside three thousand lines of pipeline state, where the tier
  check would not have caught the edge and a reviewer would have had to.
  The decisions in it. **Straight alpha rather than premultiplied**, which is
  the opposite of the overlay pipeline beside it and is deliberate:
  `gui::DrawCommand` carries a tint and a transparency as separate numbers, so
  the vertex colour reaching the blend has never had alpha folded into it, and
  premultiplying would mean a per-vertex multiply on the CPU for no gain.
  **No depth and no culling**, because the interface is drawn back to front in
  compile order - a depth test would hide a panel behind a wall, and a quad
  wound the other way is invisible under culling, which looks exactly like an
  element that failed to lay out. **Buffers grow and never shrink**, so a panel
  opened once and closed does not reallocate the frame it opens again. **The
  transfer buffer is cycled**, because it was written last frame and may still
  be in flight - without that the copy overwrites bytes a queued frame has not
  read, drawing last frame's interface at this frame's positions, a tearing that
  only appears under load. The atlas uploads once rather than per frame; it
  changes only when the size it was baked at does, which is a settings change.
  **An unresolved image name falls back to the atlas's white texel**, drawing
  the bounds as a flat tinted rectangle - visible on purpose, for the reason the
  entry below gives.
  **And `mono.client` constructs one**, which is the half that makes the rest
  reachable: a pass nothing hands to the renderer is unreachable code that
  compiles. The client builds it from `Renderer::Backend()` after the renderer
  is up, compiles the rendered world's `ScreenGui` each frame, and passes it as
  the `interfaceHook` - the same slot the studio fills with imgui. A failure to
  initialise is warned and not fatal: a client that refused to start over a
  missing typeface would be worse than one that draws a world with no interface
  over it.
  **`mono.client` still links no imgui**, which was the point of the whole
  entry, and the architecture check pins it.
  **What is not verified is that it draws.** Every piece compiles, every piece
  above it is tested headlessly, and the pass itself needs a device and an eye -
  which is the one check in this version that a test cannot stand in for. Corner
  rounding and the image fit modes travel to that backend rather than being
  tessellated here - a radius belongs in a shader, and nine-slice needs the
  image's pixel size, which is precisely what a content name says this layer
  must not know
- [x] **Texture resolution for `ImageLabel` and `ImageButton`.** The runtime
