  format was added in v0.8 and the upload hook was connected in v0.10. The
  client and studio resolve `ui::ImageSource` through the content tables, while
  unresolved content keeps the visible fallback marker. Runtime code reads the
  baked texture format; PNG decoding remains in `bake` and is not carried by a
  shipped game.
- [x] **Connecting `gui::GuiEvent` to `script::Signals`.** The routing produced
  events and nothing turned them into a `.Activated`. **The join is in `script`
  itself, which is "above both" read correctly**: `gui` is L7 and `script` is
  L9, so the edge is legal by tier and it is the *same* edge `script` already
  has to `scene` - a script's vocabulary is the class tree and there are two of
  them, so depending on one and not the other would have put `.Activated` behind
  something higher up, and that something would have been the second path into a
  VM this line refuses. Six `SignalKind`s, both VMs, and one queue on `Runtime`
  holding nothing either VM owns.
  **Queued rather than fired on arrival**, for `Changes.hpp`'s reason one door
  along: the events are produced while a host walks a compiled draw list, and a
  handler that destroys the element it was called about would pull that list out
  from under the walk. They are delivered at the barrier, last within "what the
  previous barrier recorded" and before the tasks, so a click that defers
  belongs to the tick it happened on. **A dead element is skipped rather than
  dispatched to** - a close button destroying its own panel is the ordinary case,
  not an edge one, and the same delivery carries events about what went with it.
  **`Activated`, `InputBegan` and `InputEnded` are called with no arguments**,
  and that is the version of this decision the file's own rule produces: Roblox
  passes an `InputObject`, there is no such datatype here, and a different shape
  invented now would have to change the day one arrives - `VideoFrame`'s trade
  rather than `AutomaticSize`'s. `MouseEnter`/`MouseLeave`/`MouseMoved` get
  `(x, y)` in canvas pixels, which is Roblox's signature exactly.
  **The studio forwards and does not dispatch**, which is what keeps the editor
  out of it: `Overlay.cpp` hands the router's events to the running world's
  runtime and that is all it knows. Found while wiring it: `examples::LoadScene`
  registered the 3D class tree and not the 2D one, so `Interface.luau` - a
  shipped example built entirely from `ScreenGui` and `Frame` - could not load
  through the entry point every program shares. It worked in the editor, because
  `Editor::NewGame` registers them on its own path, which is the shape of gap
  that survives: it works everywhere somebody looked
- [x] **`GuiBase3d` and the adornments.** Eleven classes
  hung off `GuiBase`, which is the split that class was kept for: the 2D
  branch's own comment said `GuiBase3d` "and the adornments hang off `GuiBase`
  when they arrive, and a tree that had flattened the two would have to grow the
  split back at exactly the point somebody is adding a feature". This is that
  point, and `gui/tests/Adornments.cpp` asserts the promise was kept - a
  `SelectionBox` `:IsA` a `GuiBase` and deliberately **not** a `GuiBase2d`.
  **An adornment is a description and `gui` owns one half of it.** Turning
  "outline this, in this colour" into geometry needs the adornee's `CFrame` and
  stud extent, which are `scene::Transform` and `scene::Bounds`; this module
  links neither and the edge is refused. That is not a gap but `D00022`'s split
  arrived at again, word for word: **whoever draws an adornment has both
  operands and this module has one.** So what is here is the tree logic a drawer
  would otherwise each answer differently - `AdorneeOf`, `AdornmentDrawn`,
  `EachAdornment` - and the drawing is the render pass's.
  Two rules inside it were arrived at rather than assumed. **An unset `Adornee`
  means the parent**, which is what makes a `SelectionBox` usable by parenting
  it to the thing it outlines and setting nothing else; and **a dangling one
  does not fall back to the parent**, because quietly re-aiming an adornment at
  whatever it happens to be parented to would draw a box around the wrong
  object - a wrong answer to "what am I about to delete" is worse than no
  answer. Ordering is `ZIndex`, stable, decided here rather than by each drawer,
  because two drawers sorting independently is two answers to what covers what.
  **And the drawing half is `render::AdornmentGeometry`**, which is where
  `D00022` said it would be: this module links both `gui` and `scene`, so it is
  the first place with both operands. It resolves each adornment against its
  adornee's `CFrame` and `Bounds` into world-space lines - twelve edges of a
  box, not six faces, because filling one would hide what it is drawn around,
  which is the one thing a selection box exists to leave visible.
  Three decisions in it. **Corners go through the adornee's own frame** rather
  than being built in world space from a position and an extent, so a rotated
  part gets a rotated box - the axis-aligned version reads as the selection
  being wrong rather than the renderer. **The box is swollen a hair**, because
  edges coplanar with the surface z-fight along every side and a flickering
  selection box reads as a driver fault. **An adornee with no `Transform`
  produces nothing** rather than a box at the origin: a `Folder` can legally be
  an `Adornee`, and outlining it at (0,0,0) looks like a bug in the selection.
  Containment and the adornee fallback are not re-derived here - they are
  `gui::EachAdornment`'s, and a second answer would disagree with the first the
  day one was fixed
- [x] **Containment: where a collector has to live to draw at all.** Roblox's
  rule, and the engine was more permissive than it: a `ScreenGui` drew wherever
  it was found, including parented to a `Part` or to nothing. That let an author
  ship a game whose interface appears in the studio and is missing in the
  client - a difference running the worst way round, because the place it works
  is the place it is checked. A `ScreenGui` now draws from `StarterGui` or a
  player's `PlayerGui`; a `SurfaceGui` or `BillboardGui` from those and from
  `Workspace`, because each hangs off something in the world.
  **Tested by ancestor *name*, which is this module's existing arrangement
  rather than a shortcut**: those three are `scene`'s services and
  `gui/AGENTS.md` refuses the edge, the same refusal that made `gui::Face`
  re-declare `NormalId`'s six members. The names are exposed on both headers and
  pinned against each other from `examples/tests/Scene.cpp` - the lowest place
  both modules are linked - so a rename fails a test instead of making every
  interface silently stop drawing. `scene::AddPlayer` creates the `PlayerGui`,
  because a container each caller has to remember is one somebody will not.
  Found by it immediately: `Interface.luau` never parented its own `ScreenGui`
  and had been drawing on the permissive path since it was written

- [x] **`GuiService`** - and **not** `Path2D` or `GuidRegistryService`, which
  is this version's own rule applied to its own plan rather than to somebody
  else's. "Fifty registered classes with no layout, no rendering and no input
  ... draw nothing, forever, with no error" is what kept `VideoFrame` out, and
  it disqualifies two of these three: `gui::DrawKind` has four members and none
  of them is a stroked polyline, so a `Path2D` would be control points nothing
  ever looked at; and there is nothing here for a GUID registry to keep, so
  every method would return nothing. `Path2D` arrives with a fifth `DrawKind`
  and `ui::PaintGui`'s support for it, together.
  **`GuiService` is here because it has something to do.**
  `GuiObject::Selectable` had been a declared, saved, bound property with no
  reader since the tree was registered - the exact state the rule refuses - and
  the selection is that reader. `Select` refuses an element that cannot hold the
  selection rather than stranding a gamepad somewhere no direction can leave;
  `SelectNext` moves directionally over the **compiled draw list** rather than
  the tree, for `Pick`'s reason: the list is already what says an element is on
  screen, and a second traversal would be a second answer to that.
  **Scored rather than sorted**, because "is above" is not a total order - B can
  be above A while A is above C - and the across-axis term is weighted so it
  only ever breaks ties, never overrules distance. `GetGuiInset` reads the
  `Screen` rather than storing a copy that would drift on the first resize.
  Found while wiring it: the generated `Services` type was a hand-written list
  of five, so `game:GetService("StarterGui")` typechecked as an error against an
  engine that answers it perfectly well. It is derived from the class table now
- [x] **The studio's own panels, authored in TypeScript.** The point of all of
  it and deliberately the *last* step. `mono.studio/panels/Properties.ts` is a
  property grid - rows of key and value in a `ScrollingFrame`, stacked by a
  `UIListLayout`, inset by `UIPadding`, selecting on `.Activated`.
  **It is not a replacement for the imgui panels and must not be read as one.**
  `mono.studio` keeps Dear ImGui until the engine's tree can draw a property
  grid, because an editor half on each is two widget sets. This is the proof
  that it *can*, which is what has to be true before any of them move.
  **React is still an open decision and this is what it would be made
  against.** A reconciler's job is to produce this tree from a description, so
  the tree has to be known to work first - building it imperatively is how you
  find out, and `react-lua` or a hand-written one remain both reasonable.
  **Three things had to be fixed before a `.ts` panel could exist at all**, and
  each was a gap nothing failed over. `script/Runtime.hpp` had always said a
  `.ts` file "is expected to have been type-stripped already"; nothing emitted
  it, so every `.ts` in the tree was copied verbatim and QuickJS refused it at
  the first annotation - `Mirrors-1-world.ts` since the day it was written.
  Staging transpiles them now, through the `tsc` the repository already carries,
  with a missing one degrading to "no JavaScript twins" rather than a failed
  build. The generated `game.GetService` overloads were a hand-written four
  while the Luau half had been made to derive its list, so a panel asking for
  `StarterGui` typechecked as an error against an engine that answers it; both
  derive from the class table now. And **JavaScript's `GetService` never
  resolved from the tree** - the Luau half always had - so the two languages
  disagreed about what a service *is*, found by the first panel that tried to
  parent a `ScreenGui`

**Why not do the shims now, stated so nobody re-asks - and what it turned out
to buy.** Fifty registered classes with no layout, no rendering and no input
would put `TextLabel` in the insert palette, let somebody parent one under a
`Part`, save it into a game file - and then draw nothing, forever, with no
error. That is worse than not having it: it is a feature that looks present and
is not. The order above is the order in which each step has a caller.

**The rule was applied twice while doing it, both times against something that
would have been easy to ship.** `VideoFrame` is not registered, because there
is no decoder and never was going to be one this version. `AutomaticSize` *is*
declared, and that is the case where the trade came out the other way: it is a
property rather than a class, it crosses a save file and both bindings, so
adding it later is a format change and adding it now costs a byte - but a
property that is declared and does nothing is still a thing somebody will set
and wonder about, so it is filed as `D00021` rather than left to be found.

The rendering and camera work this version already carried, unchanged:

- [x] `mono.engine/examples/Mirrors-4-worlds.luau` and `.ts` - split-screen across worlds running in parallel. Four `ViewChannel` producers and one compositor, which v0.2 already built and `--worlds N` already drives with a synthetic scene. This demo is what proves the view path end to end. **Four rather than two**, because two of anything is the count at which a bug in the placement loop still looks like correct behaviour: two rooms side by side are symmetric, so swapping them or getting the sign of the offset wrong still reads as two rooms side by side. The four differ by an *ordered* palette running warm to cool and by caster count, so a wrong order reads as a wrong order and a view drawn twice reads as two rooms with the same population.

  **One file builds four different worlds, and `game.JobId` is what lets it.**
  `--worlds N` runs the same script in every world it creates; without an
  identity every view would be identical and the bug above would be invisible.
  Roblox's `JobId` names the server instance a script is standing on and a world
  here *is* that instance, so it answers `ecs::Store::Name()` - a name rather
  than a fresh GUID, because a name is already what a bus envelope, a snapshot
  and a view header carry. Both VMs, and pinned by a case in each.

  **The `.ts` twin is typechecked and not executed, and that is a gap rather
  than a choice.** `Runtime.hpp` says a `.ts` file "is expected to have been
  type-stripped already - nothing in the C++ build compiles TypeScript". Nothing
  in this repository emits it either: `tsconfig.json` sets `noEmit`, and
  `just typecheck` runs `tsc --noEmit`. So every `.ts` example, including
  `Mirrors-1-world.ts` since it was written, is a file QuickJS cannot parse - it
  fails on the first type annotation. Closing it is a staging step that emits
  stripped JavaScript beside the source, which is the toolchain's half rather
  than the engine's

### v0.9

- [x] local filesystem content delivery network (cdn) - `mono.cdn` and `cdn::ContentRoot` landed early, at v0.2
- [x] cdn content addressing - `Engine::assets` at L8 `shared`: `ContentHash` (BLAKE3-256, checked against the published vectors rather than against itself), `Chunker` (gear rolling hash, FastCDC normalised two-mask cut, table generated from a stated seed so 256 frozen constants are reviewable as an algorithm), `HashTree` (Merkle with a tagged interior and the leaf count sealed into the root, so neither a subtree nor a duplicated node can pass as a whole tree), `Signature` (Ed25519 over a domain-separated message, one signature at the root and none below it) and `Manifest` (the four-level tree, canonical order, byte-stable output, and a reader that recomputes every root rather than believing it). BLAKE3 vendored. `CDN.md` §2
- [x] cdn grants - `assets::Grant`: an HMAC-SHA256 token naming bundle roots, a session, an absolute expiry and a byte budget, plus `cdn::Gate` as the origin's whole authorisation surface. The MAC is checked before any field is acted on and compared in constant time; forged, expired and out-of-scope are counted apart so an alarm is not buried in ordinary events; a grant names content hashes and has no field a path could occupy; `nowSeconds` is passed in so neither end holds a clock of its own to drift. `CDN.md` §4
- [x] cdn group assembly - `cdn::Grouper`: self-sufficiency first (an affinity is never split, even past the ceiling, because half a scene makes nothing appear - and the oversized group is counted rather than hidden), then a size mix that falls out of largest-first packing rather than needing a rule of its own, then priority. Deterministic, because two origins that group the same content differently cache different bundles and nothing reports that they stopped sharing. `CDN.md` §5
- [x] cdn group compression - `cdn::GroupCodec` and `cdn::Dictionary` over vendored Zstd: per group and never per file or per manifest, chunks left uncompressed at rest so dedup works on them, dictionaries trained per build and content-addressed so they version like everything else. The content checksum is turned on because Zstd leaves it off and a flipped byte otherwise decompresses cleanly to the wrong content; a decompression buffer is sized from the signed manifest and never from the frame, which is what stops a kilobyte declaring a gigabyte. Zstd vendored under BSD-3-Clause, legacy frame support off as attack surface we cannot need. `CDN.md` §5
- [x] cdn prepared-group cache and cancellation - `cdn::PreparedCache`, keyed by bundle root **and** dictionary hash because a group compressed against one dictionary is a different artefact from the same group compressed against another. LRU, bounded, thread-safe, and frames handed out as shared ownership so an eviction cannot free bytes underneath a client still streaming them. Cancellation is load-bearing: a cancelled request costs no compression, and one cancelled mid-preparation has its result discarded rather than delivered to nobody
- [x] cdn as a cache server - `CDNSettings` is the whole of an origin's setup, and CDN.md §6's three sources are field combinations rather than three programs: local store (`AllowUpstream` off), cache server (local first, forward on a miss, keep what came back) and pure proxy (`LocalFirst` off, `CacheUpstream` off). Local content is checked before anything external, which is what makes a hit cost no network at all. What an upstream returns is verified against the **signed** manifest before it is cached or served - a length check, honestly not chunk-level, because the chunk layout inside a group is not designed yet; the client verifies end to end regardless, so this is defence in depth. Upstream attempts are bounded, and an upstream that is down is counted apart from one serving wrong content
- [x] **cdn wire streaming** - `Engine::net`'s `http/` sub-area landed and the origin serves over it. A strict HTTP/1.1 subset with the protocol split from the socket, so the whole wire format is exercised by a suite that opens no port: `GET` and `HEAD` only, `Content-Length` framing only, `Transfer-Encoding` refused outright and two `Content-Length` fields refused **even when they agree** - a body that can be framed two ways is request smuggling, and "even when they agree" is the check somebody eventually loosens. Polled rather than threaded, so a handler runs on the thread that pumped and every completion lands there; allowed to be asynchronous at all only because CDN.md §3's origin has no tick. Four routes and no more - `/health`, `/manifest`, `/dictionary`, `/bundle/<root>` - and **no route takes a name**, so there is nothing to walk out of a directory. Range serving answers `206` for the resumable case. `cdn` links `net` and `delivery` now, which this line predicted; every entry is still `shared`, so `just check-cdn-is-bare` still passes
- [x] cdn async pipeline - `cdn::Origin`: submit, pump, take, cancel. A publication is immutable and publishing is an **atomic swap**, so a request already admitted is served against what it was admitted against whatever is published underneath - pinned by a test that swaps mid-request. Concurrency is per request; `Jobs::For` appears in exactly one place, compressing a known set of groups, because that is CPU work with a known end and the pipeline around it waits on a filesystem. Cache lookups happen before the fan-out so a hit costs no compression. A bundle with no payload is refused rather than served empty. `CDN.md` §3
- [x] **the client half of the async pipeline** - `mono.engine/delivery` at L11 `shared`, and it is a new module rather than the `assets` addition this line predicted: fetching needs a transport, the transport is `net` at L11, and rule 1 forbids L8 seeing it. `assets/AGENTS.md` had already reached the same conclusion from the other side, so `assets` stays the format and the fetch path sits above both. **A completion becomes visible when the caller pumps and at no other moment** - no callback, no future, no background thread - which is what makes obeying rule 5 at a world's barrier the easy path. Sources are walked in priority order and a failure falls through to the next, so "local cache first, then the origin next door, then the one across the internet" is what a *list* means rather than a policy anything implements. Three checks in order: the manifest's signature, then a group's decompressed length against the **signed** manifest, then every asset against its root
- [x] **the chunk store, and the group layout** - `assets::ChunkStore` decides how chunks are laid out on a disk, which `CDN.md` §7 listed as not started and left `cdn::PayloadSource` as a seam for. It is in `assets` rather than in the origin for the reason the manifest is: **a publisher writes that tree and a client reads it**, so one implementation or the two acquire a dialect. A bundle's payload is its members concatenated in member order with no framing and no index - **derived from the signed manifest rather than transmitted**, so an origin cannot move an asset's boundary without breaking the signature
- [x] **asset kinds, and the root that binds them** - `AssetKind` in the manifest, decided once by the publisher from the extension and never re-derived downstream, which is what "connect and get assets of types" resolves to. Finding a home for it exposed a gap worth more than the feature: the manifest root covered bundle roots alone, so it bound every byte of content and nothing about what a byte was *called*. An origin serving a manifest with two names swapped handed a client content that verified perfectly and was the wrong asset. The root is now a two-leaf tree over a **descriptor root** - name, kind and content root per asset - and the bundle root. One signature, still at the top; the asset root is untouched and still addresses content alone, because folding a name into it would lose dedup across names. `Manifest::VERSION` 2, which cost nobody a re-publish because nothing had written a version-1 manifest to disk
- [x] **the origin is a program somebody can run** - `cdn --publish DIR --store DIR --signing-key HEX` builds a store, `cdn --store DIR --grant-key HEX --port N` serves one, and they are separate invocations because the signing key is separate: a key belongs to whoever publishes and **the origin holds none**, which is what makes it deployable on hardware nobody here owns. Upstreams, local-first, cache-upstream, compression level and cache size are all flags, so CDN.md §6's three deployments stay field combinations rather than three programs
- [x] **an origin attached to the server, and a client that can be pointed at one** - `server --content-store DIR --content-grant-key HEX` runs CDN.md §6's local store in-process; the grant is issued by one half and opened by the other **across a function call with both halves real**, which is §16.6's argument about the loopback transport applied to content. `client --cdn HOST:PORT --publisher-key HEX` points a game at content, repeatable and in priority order, `dir:PATH` for a local store. The studio gets the same list as a Preferences page with reorder arrows, because the order *is* the policy
- [x] **mesh importing and baking** - `assets::Mesh` is the baked format (`AMS1`: interleaved position, normal and texture coordinate, 32-bit indices because a PMX character is well past 65535 vertices, and one submesh per material because a mesh is not one material). **The bounds are derived on read and never stored**, because a stored bound is the one field of a mesh an attacker gets to choose - a zero box disappears from every frustum test and a huge one draws from everywhere, and both look like renderer bugs. `mono.engine/bake` at L9 `shared` is the only code in the engine that reads a `.glb`, a `.pmx` or an `.obj`: glTF with the node hierarchy folded into the vertices and a skinned node contributing the identity rather than its own transform (the specification's rule, and what stops a skinned model importing on its side), PMX with Z mirrored **and the winding reversed to match** - one without the other is a model you can only see from inside - and OBJ with negative indices resolved from where they are and `v` flipped once at import rather than in a shader that would flip the formats already right
- [x] **texture importing and baking** - PNG (every colour type, 8 and 16 bits, CRC-verified, interlaced refused by name), BMP (8/24/32-bit, and the signed height that means top-down), and a baseline JPEG decoder written for this - Huffman, restart intervals, greyscale and YCbCr, **libjpeg's triangle chroma upsampling weights verbatim**. The first version sampled nearest with a comment claiming that matched other decoders; measured against Pillow it was 28 levels out on a saturated chroma edge and the filtered version is 3, which is the inverse DCT's own rounding. Every decoder is checked byte-for-byte against an independent implementation on the real 2048-pixel sheets, not only on fixtures. Progressive JPEG and interlaced PNG are **refused by name** rather than half-read, because a recognisable wrong picture reads as a quality setting rather than as a bug
- [x] **the node pipeline** - `bake::Graph`, and the shape is `audio::Graph`'s deliberately: input nodes (`Source`, `Builtin`), processing nodes (`Import`, `Fit`, `Scale`, `Smooth`, `Resize`, `Opaque`) and export nodes (`Write`), run in topological order with **cycles refused at the wire**. **The graph touches no filesystem**, which is what `audio::NullDevice` is to the mixer: every importer, every processing step and every export is exercised by a suite that opens no file, and a studio baking into memory runs the identical code a CLI baking onto a disk does. A node has at most one input, so "what produced this asset" is a walk rather than a fold; fan-out is the useful direction and is allowed
- [x] **auto export to assets folder** - `assetc --input ART --output content`, which is `cdn --publish`'s input and knows nothing about it. A baked asset's name is its source's path with the extension replaced, and **that one rule is what makes a model's texture references rewritable without a lookup table**: the importer reports the path the model spells, and the same substitution names the baked texture. A file the baker does not understand is copied rather than dropped, because the output tree is what gets published and a baker that dropped every sound would produce a directory missing half the game. One unreadable file costs that file and not the run
- [x] **built-in engine meshes** - `assets::MakeBuiltin` generates a cube, plane, wedge, corner wedge, sphere and cylinder, and `render::MeshTable` registers all six at start-up so a scene may name any of them with nothing published. Generated rather than tabulated, because the property that matters is the **winding** and a hand-written sphere makes it unreadable: the suite checks that every triangle of every built-in points outwards, that every closed one is a manifold - every edge shared by exactly two triangles traversing it in opposite directions, which subsumes "no hole", "no duplicated face" and "no missing pole triangle" - and that each is the same geometry every call. `render::Primitives` held the cube and is deleted; there is one definition now
- [x] **MeshPart, SurfaceAppearance as components, and tagging** - `MeshPart` derives `BasePart` and adds no component, because `Visual::Mesh` and `SurfaceAppearance::ColourMap` already sit there; what the class is *for* is the vocabulary, so a script says `Instance.new("MeshPart")` and reads `.MeshId` and `.TextureID`. **Both new components are on `BasePart` rather than on `MeshPart`**, which costs sixteen bytes on every part in the world and buys a draw-list pass with no optional join - `client::CollectInstances` is a batched parallel walk over a fixed signature, and an optional column is exactly what that shape cannot express. `scene::Tags` is a 32-bit mask against a `TagTable` resource that maps a name to a bit: a name outside the process and a bit inside it, so a pass filtering by tag is an `and` per instance rather than a set lookup. **The thirty-third tag is refused rather than aliased**, because an alias puts one group's objects in another group's pass and is invisible until somebody notices the wrong thing reflected
- [x] **rendering a mesh, and its texture** - `render::MeshTable` packs every mesh into **one** vertex and index buffer pair, so a mesh is `(firstIndex, indexCount, vertexOffset)` and changing mesh costs three integers on a draw call rather than a rebind. `render::TextureTable` is its twin. `Renderer::DrawSlots` is the loop v0.9 exists to add: it splits each contiguous instance run wherever the mesh or the instance's texture override changes and issues a draw per piece - **consecutive-run splitting rather than grouping**, because the blended pass's order is back-to-front from the eye and reordering it is the transparency bug the sort exists to prevent. Three colour sources multiply rather than one winning: the texture, the submesh's base colour and the instance tint. Fixing the vertex layout exposed a bug that had been invisible while everything was a cube - the model matrix has a half-extent folded into it, so `mat3(model)` is not the normal matrix, and an axis-aligned normal survives the wrong matrix while a sphere's does not
- [x] **it works, end to end, on real assets** - `assetc` bakes a `fox_pet.glb` (ten material runs, no texture, recognisably a fox only because each run carries its own base colour) and a PMX character (twenty-seven submeshes, UTF-16 texture paths converted to UTF-8, six 2048-pixel sheets); `cdn --publish` signs the tree; `client --cdn` fetches, verifies against the signed manifest and registers into the renderer's tables at the pump barrier. **88,302 triangles in 136 draw calls with the origin attached and 1,796 in 28 without it** - the second number is every `MeshId` resolving to the fallback cube, which is the deliberate degraded state. `examples/Meshes.luau` is the scene, `client --capture` is the new flag that writes a BMP of it, and there is a mirror in front of the meshes: `ROADMAP.md` asked for exactly that check, and a frame where the reflection is right and the meshes are not says which of the two paths broke
- [x] scripts that create MeshPart and set mesh properties - through the same class table `Instance.new` has always used. `MeshId` and `TextureID` are declared properties projecting `Visual::Mesh` and `SurfaceAppearance::ColourMap`, so a script setting `.MeshId` and one reading `.Mesh` cannot disagree, and both languages get them from one declaration rather than from two bindings

- [x] **a material format** - closed at v0.10 rather than here, and the entry stays in v0.9 because that is where it was promised. `AssetKind::Material` writes something now: `assets::Material` is an `.amat`, `assetc` bakes a `.mat` into one, and `Submesh::Texture` is what it references. The v0.10 line below carries the whole change, including the enum it deleted
- [x] **tag-filtered surface passes** - `SurfaceCamera::TagFilter` is a mask an instance must intersect to be drawn into that surface, so a second camera pointed at the same scene draws a *group* rather than a second copy of it. **Applied per instance in the draw loop rather than by re-ordering the draw list**, because the order is shared by every view and a filter is per view: partitioning for one surface would partition for all of them and the screen pass would then draw the group instead of the world. The property is authored by name and stored as a bit - a script says `camera.TagFilter = "Imported, Reflective"` and the registration happens once, where the name is written, so the hot path never sees a string. **A list in a string**, because a mask holds thirty-two and a property holds one value; the alternative was a list type in `ecs::PropertyType` that exactly one property would use. A filter that would overflow the table is refused whole rather than applied part way, since a camera left with half its filter draws some of its group and that is harder to notice than none of it. `instance:AddTag`, `:RemoveTag` and `:HasTag` are the script side; they are methods rather than a `CollectionService` because the thing being tagged is already in hand and a service would add a lookup and nothing else. Measured on `examples/Meshes.luau`: **892 fewer triangles and 9 fewer draw calls** with the filter on, which is exactly the six built-ins, the baseplate and the two panes it excludes
- [x] **the v0.9 components replicate** - `scene.SurfaceAppearance` and `scene.Tags` are in all three authority lists, because a replica that received a mesh name and no texture name has half a model and it reads as a broken texture path rather than as a component nobody sent. `Signature` detection, since neither changes on a steady world. **The tag *names* do not cross and that is stated rather than hidden**: a `TagTable` is a resource and resources have no wire form, so a replica's table is empty and `HasTag(name)` there answers false - mask-against-mask filtering is unaffected, because a surface camera's filter and an instance's mask both come from one authority
- [x] **a priority score that reads a distance** - `replication::DistancePriority` fills in the `SetPriority` hook that had sat empty since v0.4, so a client is sent what it is standing next to before what is across the map rather than a plain round robin. **The arithmetic is `replication`'s and the lookup is the host's**, which is the division `SetPriority`'s own documentation asked for: two caller-supplied accessors, and the module still names no component. The ramp is linear rather than inverse-square - an inverse square collapses within a few metres, so everything past that sorts identically and the rotation decides anyway, which is the behaviour the score exists to improve on. Nothing a caller supplies can produce a non-finite score, because `std::sort` on a comparator that is not a weak ordering is undefined rather than merely wrong. `Server::SetClientViewpoint` is the host's half, keyed by slot *and generation* so a reused slot cannot hand a new client the previous one's viewpoint
- [x] **the handshake binds to a server identity** - `net::Handshake` has carried a `TODO(D00006)` since v0.3 saying an unauthenticated agreement is safe against a listener and not against a relay. It is closed, and **not in `net`**, which was always the plan: the fix binds the exchange to a *server identity* and that module has no idea what a server is. `Welcome` carries an Ed25519 signature over the same transcript its confirmation tag is computed on, signed with the key a publisher signs manifests with - one identity to distribute rather than two to pin wrongly - under its own domain tag so a manifest signature can never be replayed as a session one. `server --identity-key` and `client --server-key` are the two halves. **The tag and the signature are different checks**: the tag proves the sender reached these keys, which a relay does too by holding one exchange with each side; the signature proves which server reached them. The suite models a competent relay - two exchanges, so the tag passes and only the signature refuses - with a control beside it that removes the pin and watches the same bytes get in. Both ends default to the weaker mode and both log it
- [x] **lag compensation** - `replication::Rewind` keeps the last half second of placements and answers "where was this, then", so a server can judge a client's action against what that client actually saw. **A record and not a rollback**: nothing is re-simulated and no state is restored, because a hit test does not need one and rollback is a far larger blast radius. The lookup is the caller's, as the priority score's is, so the module still names no component. Two details decide whether it is right or merely present - the sample is **fractional**, because a client renders *between* two ticks and a whole-tick answer is a pose it never saw; and the compensation is **half** the round trip, because the input's journey back is already carried by the tick it names. The suite runs the whole loop at a speed where one tick of error is ten metres, and pins the fifty-metre gap between what the client aimed at and where the server thinks the target is
- [x] **the server applies inputs, authoritatively** - the line that said "nothing consumes inputs yet: an input has nowhere to be applied" is gone. `examples::Shooting` is the demo game's rule - a shot is a ray and a range, and **the ray crosses the wire rather than the hit**, because a client that decided what it hit is one nothing downstream can second-guess. The server decodes it, rewinds to the tick that client saw, tests it against the *history's* candidates rather than the world's - an entity destroyed since is still a legitimate thing to have shot at - and recolours what it struck, which replicates like any other `Visual`. Nearest wins and ties break by entity id, because candidates arrive in hash-map order and a server whose hit resolution depends on that is one whose recordings do not replay. The rule lives in a game module and not in `replication`, which is the whole reason `Input::Bytes` is documented as "the game's own encoding"
- [x] **a line of sight in the priority score** - `DistancePriority::Blocked`, wired on the server to `physics::Raycast`. Same split as the distance term: the arithmetic here, the query there, because occlusion needs a broad phase and a game's own idea of what occludes. **A hidden entity is scaled and never zeroed** - one scored at nothing is one only the rotation ever sends, so a player rounding a corner meets a wall of objects snapping into place; it should update *less*. And the cheap test gates the expensive one, so anything already scoring near nothing is never raycast about
- [x] **the client authenticates too** - `Identify`, signed over the same transcript, sent as the client's first act on the encrypted stream. It **sends** its key where a client **pins** the server's, and the asymmetry is the point: a server has one identity every client expects, and expects many clients it cannot list in a setting, so `Listener::SetClientPolicy` decides. A claim that does not verify is dropped without consulting the policy - "is this key real" is not a game's question. Adding the kind found a real trap: `ReadMessage`'s range check still named the previous last kind, so every `Identify` parsed as out of range and was dropped as malformed while every `-Wswitch` stayed quiet
- [x] **audio in cdn and running audio in-studio and in-game** - `mono.engine/audio` at L12 `client`: the DAW-like node pipeline `core-features.md` asks for, as `Player` inputs, `Fader`/`Emitter`/`Bus` processors and one `Output`, wired by directed edges and run in topological order. **Cycles are refused at the wire rather than found at the mix**, because a feedback loop is either infinite recursion inside a callback with a hard deadline or unbounded gain. The requirement `DATATYPES_LIBRARIES.md` §11.2 marks `!` is what shapes the whole module: a game ticks at frame rate and audio runs at sample rate, so **the tick queues a command with a sample deadline and the mixer splits its block at every deadline inside it** - a `Play` due at sample 200 of a 512-frame block produces two sub-blocks, not one applied at the top. A missed buffer is audible, so nothing on the render path allocates, blocks or locks: the mixer owns the graph, commands cross on a lock-free single-producer ring, scratch is sized when the topology changes, and a sound is a shared pointer copied on the tick side. RIFF/WAV decoding treats every length field as hostile - a chunk running past the end is a refusal and never a clamp, because a clamp turns a truncated file into a shorter sound that plays. Spatialisation is equal-power panning and an inverse-square falloff pinned to zero at its end. `NullDevice` renders on demand with no sound card, which is what makes **121 cases** of this testable at all; `client --sound FILE.wav` is the caller. The cdn half was already done: `AssetKind::Audio` classifies at publish and `RequestKind` fetches every sound in a game

### v0.10

- [x] **CurrentCamera** - `workspace.CurrentCamera`, a `Reference` property over the `ActiveCamera` resource rather than a component on `Workspace`. Reading resolves the resource and writing moves it, so there is exactly one place the live camera is recorded and no way for a second to disagree - which matters because `client::InstallDefaultCamera` writes the resource directly. **A write is refused for anything that is not a camera**, because assigning a part would leave `ResolveActiveCamera` looking for a component it will not find and the matrices would silently stay as they were: a frame drawn from where the camera used to be, which reads as a renderer fault. Removing the *hand-written* declaration was the other half - the generator had special-cased `CurrentCamera` because it was not a declared property, and an extern type carrying the member twice is not a warning, it is an assertion failure inside Luau's constraint generator that surfaces as `SIGILL` on every script in the tree. The same correction `Instance.Name` went through at v0.5, failing the same way
- [x] **basic character controls** - `scene::Humanoid` under a character model, which is Roblox's arrangement: the humanoid is a sibling of the parts it drives rather than a component on one. Deliberately small, because the instruction beside this item said to spend the time on the particles - a capsule, a walk speed, a jump and a ground flag, and no state machine, animator, ladder or swim. **`MoveDirection` is a direction and not a velocity**, which is the split that lets `WalkSpeed` mean something; the horizontal velocity is *replaced* each step and the vertical is kept, because adding a force leaves momentum that makes stopping take a second and replacing Y as well would cancel gravity. Input is normalised, so running diagonally is not forty per cent quicker - the oldest movement bug there is. **Ground detection is a downward ray and the query is the client's**: `scene` may not link `physics`, so `StepCharacters` reads `Grounded` and `client::GroundCharacters` writes it, which is `DistancePriority::Blocked`'s split exactly
- [x] **basic controls (enable/disable/shift-lock)** - `CameraController::Enabled` is separate from `CameraMode::Scriptable` on purpose: a disabled camera keeps its mode and stops responding, so re-enabling puts the player back where they were, where a script that switched to `Scriptable` and back would have lost the angles. Shift-lock is a *mode* rather than a flag, because it is the one where the character faces where the camera faces rather than where it is going - and the camera sits over a shoulder, which is a distinct offset and not only a facing rule
- [x] **basic camera and controls (zoom, pan, control camera via script)** - `scene::CameraController`, a resource, with the arithmetic in `shared` and the events outside it. **Turning needs the right button unless the pointer is locked**, which is Roblox's rule and what keeps a mouse usable for anything else. **Zooming all the way in *is* first person** rather than a separate key, because the transition a player expects is continuous. The pitch clamps a hundredth of a radian short of vertical, which is not fussiness - at exactly ninety degrees the `LookAt` has no way to choose a roll and the camera spins about its own axis. `CameraMode::Scriptable` is what makes `workspace.CurrentCamera.CFrame = ...` survive: without it the controller and the script would both write the transform every frame and the last one would win silently, which is the bug `MoveCamera` already records
- [x] **UserInputService and ContextActionService** - and the interesting half is the seam. **`input` is L12 `client` and `script` is L9 `shared`, so the service cannot read the module that produces its data.** `scene::InputState` is a resource both can see: `input::Translator` turns SDL events into named keys and the client copies the result onto every simulated world, `UserInputService` reads it, and `MouseBehavior` travels back the other way for the client to apply to the window. A headless world has no such resource and every query answers "nothing pressed" rather than raising, which is what lets one script poll input on both halves of a game. **What `ContextActionService` adds over polling is a priority stack** - two systems both wanting E is the question `IsKeyDown` cannot answer - sorted at bind time, stably, so ties break in bind order and a recording replays. Two real bugs came out of building it: the action list was a `thread_local`, so two VMs on one thread shared registry references and a *MemoryStore* test crashed; and `PumpInput` used `UpvalueContext` where it has no C closure on the stack, which turned every heartbeat into a failure. A third is a Luau constraint rather than a bug and is filed as D00030 - `luaL_sandbox` enables `safeenv`, so a property read as a bare global is resolved once and cached, and the service has to be reached the way every Roblox script already reaches it: `local UIS = game:GetService("UserInputService")`
- [x] **beams, trails** - `effects::Beam` and `effects::Trail` are **one ribbon builder with two sources of points**: a beam is a strip between two attachments with a cubic Bezier through it, a trail is a strip between two attachments sampled over time. Neither is a `DrawInstance` and neither has a mesh, because a ribbon's geometry is a function of where its endpoints are *this frame* - a mesh would be a mesh table entry churning at frame rate for four vertices a segment. **A trail's history is a fixed-capacity ring on its own row**, sixteen points, because a `std::vector` per trail is an allocation per trail and - the rule that actually forbids it - a component holding one cannot survive being memcpy'd across a process boundary. **Recording happens in `Simulation` and building in `PreRender`**, which is the one place this module departs from its own pattern: a trail is a record of where something has been, so sampling it at frame rate would make its length depend on the machine drawing it, and two players swinging one sword would leave different arcs. **The pass draws them**: a triangle strip over a real vertex buffer with a blended and an additive pipeline, one draw call per run - which is the opposite of the particle path's grouping and is right for the opposite reason, since a run is already a whole beam and two runs cannot merge without joining the end of one to the start of the next. **The geometry is built on the CPU in `shared`**, so a beam's curve, its width taper and its camera-facing turn are all checkable in a suite; the vertex shader is a matrix multiply and an unpack. Verified on screen: the arc, the colour sequence along it and the width taper all render
- [x] **pointlight, spotlight, surfacelight** - three classes over **one** `scene::Light` component, which is the trade `Collider::Extent` already makes across three shapes: the three differ by two fields, and three components would be three columns, three queries and three upload paths for something a renderer packs into one array either way. Each class sets its own `LightKind` as a *prototype default*, so `Instance.new("SpotLight")` is a spot light without a script saying so - and a `Kind` property is deliberately not declared, because the class is the answer and a second way to say it is the duplicate `AGENTS.md` warns about. `Angle` and `Face` sit on the base and therefore exist on a `PointLight` that ignores them, which is the honest cost of the single component and is stated rather than hidden. **They light now**: `client::CollectLights` resolves each one through its parent - a light carries no position, for `Sound`'s reason - orders them by distance from the eye and hands the nearest sixteen to the renderer, which adds them to the directional term in the opaque pass. Added rather than replacing, so a scene with no lamps looks exactly as it did before v0.10, which is what makes it safe to switch on for every existing world. The falloff is inverse-square *windowed to zero at the range*, because a bare inverse square never reaches zero and a light would pop off at its cutoff. The one gap is honest and filed as D00029: the light count is spelled in C++ and in GLSL and nothing checks that the two agree
- [x] **attachment** - the fence post the other three hang off, which is why it was built first: beams, trails and particle emitters are all parented to one, and any of them built earlier gets built twice. **The one exception to "every transform is world space and nothing propagates it", and it is narrower than it looks** - an `Attachment` is not a `PVInstance` and carries no `Transform`; it holds its own pair of frames and one flat pass over one component type derives the second from the first. There is still no transform hierarchy, no dirty cascade and no per-entity parent walk in the simulation. **Attachments do not nest, and that is enforced rather than assumed**: resolving against another attachment's *resolved* frame is what makes a pass order-dependent, and an order-dependent pass over an archetype walk answers differently depending on which rows moved last. `WorldCFrame` is a derived field rather than a getter because a beam reads four of them a frame - but the *property* resolves on the spot, so a script reading it on the frame it created the attachment gets the right answer rather than a stale identity
- [x] **NumberSequenceKeypoint + ColorSequenceKeypoint** - real userdata types, reversing a decision this engine had made deliberately. The note in `LuauDatatypes.cpp` said two more types for a value written inline once was surface nobody asked for, and while a sequence was only ever *constructed* it was right. What made it false is that a sequence became a **property**: an emitter's `Transparency` is read back, and `Keypoints` handed out `{time, value, envelope}` tables - three anonymous numbers with no `typeof`, indistinguishable from a colour ramp's stop, and nothing to compare against. Reading a value back in a shape its own constructor accepts is the round trip a property surface owes. **The table form still works everywhere it did** and both constructors take either. Writing the test for it found a real pre-existing bug: the table branch read its fields through a *relative* stack index that shifted as the stack grew, so the second field came out of the first one's value - it had survived because every sequence in the suite was built from the two-argument form
- [x] **Vector3.zero / Vector3.one** - lowercase, because Roblox's are, and a second spelling of one constant is the duplicate `AGENTS.md` calls the most expensive kind of debt. Members of the *library* rather than of a value, so they are fields on the global table and not cases in `Vector3Index` - putting them in the index would make `part.Position.zero` answer, which is a member nobody wrote. Pushed as ordinary userdata carrying the same metatable and tag as anything `Vector3.new` returns, because a constant that failed `CheckVector3` would be one no property could be assigned from
- [x] **hook assets manager to local cdn folder** - `cdn::LocalStore` over `~/Documents/atomic-game-engine/cdn`, flat, with `raw/`, `processed/` and `content.log`. **The two folders are split because the two halves have different identities**: a raw file is what somebody called it and changes when they edit it; a processed chunk is what its bytes are and never changes at all - putting both in one folder means either hashing the names or naming the hashes, and each loses the half that made the other useful. A raw import is named `<hash><extension>`, which is the roadmap's "hash-based naming between the folders": the hash makes the two line up and makes a re-import free, and the extension keeps the folder readable. **The original name is not on disk and is in the log**, because a flat folder of original names collides the first time two projects both have a `diffuse.png` - and a log is the only thing that can answer "where did this come from" six months later. The log is appended and never rewritten, so two processes interleave rather than losing each other's lines, and a line truncated by a crash is skipped rather than refusing the file. **The client defaults to it when nobody passed `--cdn`**, which is what "hook up all cdn behavior to this by default" means in practice: content resolves with no flag, where before it took `assetc`, `cdn --publish` and a path on every run. Six tests, every one of them in a temporary directory - a suite that used the default path would write into the developer's own store. **Not done from this line: the live renderer**, which is a preview of a selected asset and wants the studio's viewport to render something that is not a world
- [x] **upload current assets to that folder** - `mono.tools/contentimport`, and a tool rather than a shell loop because a loop run once leaves nothing anybody can run again on another machine or after the store is cleared. Directories are walked and files taken as they are, because a person dragging content in thinks in folders. Run over the roadmap's own list: **289 files, 702 MB** - 231 PNGs, 12 BMPs, 6 PMX models, four GIFs and the audio, with five already present and none failed
- [x] **assets manager with an upload button and a view of what is there** - a studio panel over `cdn::LocalStore`, finished at the end of v0.10 rather than at the item. **The `[~]` was the typed path, and the reason given for it was wrong**: the note said there is no portable picker in this stack, and there is one - `studio::FilePrompt`, an imgui browser that six other dialogs in this editor already use. The probe answered "SDL has no synchronous dialog" and the question was "can this editor browse for a file". So the field is gone and there are three ways in, all ending at `ImportFile`: **drag and drop, single or many** - SDL sends one `SDL_EVENT_DROP_FILE` per path, so a forty-file drop and a dropped folder need no special case - **Add files…**, and **Add folder…**, a new `FolderPrompt` that is beside `FilePrompt` rather than a flag on it because the two differ in what a click on a row *means*. **And the listing is now the store rather than the log.** It listed log subjects, which are the paths files came from - `~/Music/...`, `~/art/...` - so most of what it showed was somewhere else on the disk and some of it no longer existed. Now every row is a file in `~/Documents/atomic-game-engine/cdn` and nowhere else: `raw/` in one tab, the signed manifest's assets in the other. The log is still read, and only to *label* a row, because `raw/` is hash-named and nothing else can say what a file was called. A file dropped into `raw/` by hand is listed too, which a log-driven listing could not have done
- [x] **convert our luau/ts Enum_Material to Enum.Material** - both languages, and getting here took a correction worth recording. TypeScript was straightforward: an `interface Material` and a `const Material` inside `declare namespace Enum` are one name meaning both, because TypeScript keeps types and values in separate declaration spaces. **Luau was declared impossible and was not.** The first pass probed a definitions file, got the error "Unknown type Enum.Material", and concluded the language could not express it - the probe was right and the conclusion did not follow. A definitions file genuinely cannot declare a dotted type: `loadDefinitionFile` only ever writes `exportedTypeBindings[name]`. What resolves `Enum.Material` is `Scope::lookupImportedType("Enum", "Material")` - the `importedTypeBindings` map, which `require` populates and which a **host** may populate directly. That is exactly what Roblox does, and it is now what `mono.tools/scriptcheck` does: it walks the extern types the generator emitted under the `Enum_` prefix and aliases each under the `Enum` prefix before freezing. 35 enums, and `local m: Enum.Material` typechecks. **The declaration file keeps the flat names**, because the aliases are built *from* the types it creates and so cannot exist while it is loading - emitting the dotted form there made the file fail to load before a single script was checked. Both spellings name the same `TypeFun`. `DEFERRED.md` D00028 is closed with the correction written out; the editor half is open as D00031, because luau-lsp reads the same file and nothing registers the prefix for it
- [x] **Attributes and GetAttribute/SetAttribute** - and the storage question is the whole item. An attribute is a name an author invents at run time mapped to a value whose type they also pick, which is exactly the shape a component column cannot hold: a component holding a map would be a heap allocation per row on `Instance`, which is every row in the world, and rule 3 forbids it outright for anything that must survive a memcpy across a process boundary. **So it is a resource keyed by `(Entity, Name)`, and an entity with no attributes costs nothing at all** - not even a byte on its row, which is the case that matters because it is almost every entity. The value types are `ecs::PropertyType`'s rather than a new list, because an attribute and a property are one question asked at run time and at declaration time; the only refusal is `Reference`, because a handle means nothing outside the world holding it and an attribute crosses a save file. Signals go through the existing change queue, so an attribute written three times in one tick fires once with the value it ended at. Writing the test found a real bug: entity ids are reused, and the first version hooked `Store::Destroy` while a script's `:Destroy()` goes through `DestroyInstance` - so an attribute outlived its instance and surfaced on a freshly created part. The hook is now on `DestroyEntity`, which is the one point every row leaves by
- [x] **particle emitters (and hook to particle system)** - `Instance.new("ParticleEmitter")`, parented to a part or to an `Attachment`, simulated by `effects::StepParticles` and drawn by the renderer's own pass. The client installs the five systems that run it and turns the pool into `render::ParticleBatch`es - a span into the pool rather than a copy, so half a million particles reach the renderer without being moved
- [x] **flipbook particle animation** - `FlipbookLayout` of `Grid2x2`, `Grid4x4` or `Grid8x8`, with four playback modes. **The grid is square and a power of two on each side**, which is Roblox's set and is also what makes the cell arithmetic two shifts rather than two divides - this is evaluated per particle per frame, so the difference is half a million divides. `OneShot` is the default and stretches the sheet over the particle's whole lifetime, which is what almost every authored sheet is drawn for; `Random` holds one cell per particle, which is what a sheet of *variations* is for rather than a sheet of frames
- [x] **particle system**, at the stated scale and with the bottlenecks measured rather than guessed. New module `mono.engine/effects` at L8 `shared` - shared because a headless suite is the only way this gets checked at all, which is `scene::OrderScene`'s argument for living outside `render`. **The whole design is one number.** 100,000 emitters times five particles is half a million a frame, and that rules out a particle being an entity (create and destroy are what an archetype store is worst at), a `DrawInstance` (80 bytes of which none varies per particle), or a reader of its emitter's curves (four sequences, a kilobyte, scanned per particle). So: emitters are entities, particles are a pooled SoA in contiguous per-emitter blocks, retirement is a swap inside a block so `Jobs::For` needs no atomic, and each block holds a 16-sample table the step indexes instead of evaluating. The Roblox property surface is there in full - shape, style, in/out, partial, spread, drag, squash, rotation, velocity inheritance, locked-to-part, Z offset, time scale - and what is *not* implemented is absent rather than declared, which `effects/AGENTS.md` lists. **Measured, `release`, 24 threads: the two simulation passes cost 389 us at the full count**, and the stress scene runs at **170 FPS with 5 draw calls**. Three bottlenecks were found by measuring and every one of them contradicted a comment that had argued the other way: the refresh pass was re-sampling curves every frame and was 70 per cent of the frame (gated on `Store::Changed`, 522 us to 192); the render pass issued **one draw call per emitter** and would have been 100,000 of them (grouped by state, 1,608 to 6 on the same scene); and the upload memcpy'd into the transfer buffer and never issued the copy to the vertex buffer, which drew the *previous* frame and so took a screenshot rather than a crash to find. A fourth was in the scene rather than the engine - its host parts were authored `Transparency = 1`, which submits them into the sorted blended pass, so a hundred thousand invisible cubes were being sorted and drawn every frame at 4 FPS; `Visible = false` submits nothing, and the three-way distinction `Visual::CastShadow` documents is exactly that
- [x] **seed the store with the listed content** - the roadmap's own file list, brought in with `contentimport`: the three `~/Music` albums, `anime_girl_out`, `fox_pet`, `blender-dragon`, the six audio tracks, `fox_dance.gif` and the image. **289 files and 702 MB**, five of them already present because two of the folders shared bytes - which is content addressing doing its job on the very first import rather than in a benchmark
- [x] **add gif support** - a decoder in `bake`: LZW, interlacing, local and global palettes, transparency and the three disposal methods, into **one flipbook sheet rather than a list of frames**. That is the whole design decision and it is why the roadmap put GIFs and flipbook particles in one version: a GIF is a short looping animation, and the thing this engine can already *draw* animated is a flipbook - so the frames become a square power-of-two grid and an ordinary texture that every existing path handles. The alternative, an animated texture type, is a real feature and would have left a GIF unable to be a particle, which is the case that asked for it. What it costs is stated rather than hidden: a 12-frame GIF wastes four cells of a 4×4 and anything past 64 frames is truncated. **Verified against the roadmap's own `fox_dance.gif`** - 24 frames of a dancing fox at 420×420, composited correctly into a 3360×3360 8×8 sheet. The test builds its GIF byte by byte rather than reading one from disk, and hand-packing the LZW stream is what caught an off-by-one in the test's own encoding: the codes pack LSB-first at three bits and come out as two bytes, not three
- [x] **materials are content, and `Enum.Material` is deleted** - the enum held seventeen names, checked membership, and **no renderer sampled anything different for any of them**: a property that looked like it worked, on the most obvious control in the properties panel, for four versions. So the whole of it is gone - `Visual::Material`, `DefaultMaterial()`, the `EnumTable` registration, the generated `Enum_Material` - and what replaces it is an asset. **`assets::Material` is the format v0.9 listed as missing**: a magic, a version and one texture name, deliberately the dullest thing that could work, with the other four maps *absent* rather than declared and ignored - `SurfaceAppearance`'s own rule about `MetalnessMap`, applied to the type that would have carried it. `assetc` bakes a `.mat` source into it and rewrites the colour map through the **same `BakedName`** a model's texture reference goes through, because two spellings of that rule is a material resolving to nothing on a machine nobody tested. **A `Material` is an instance under a part**, which is Roblox's `SurfaceAppearance` arrangement and is what v0.11's node-based asset pipeline needs - a thing that will grow several texture references cannot be a `core::Name` field on `BasePart`. It resolves through `scene::MaterialCatalogue` and one flat `PreSimulation` pass onto the parent's `SurfaceAppearance::ColourMap`, which is `ResolveAttachments`' shape exactly and for its reason: the draw-list pass is a batched parallel walk over a fixed signature and cannot follow a child. **The default is `None`, and `None` now draws something** - `render::DefaultTexture`, ambientCG's Plastic 013 A as a compiled-in 64-pixel tile, lifted so its brightest texel is white because a default that multiplies every untextured part by 0.92 is a scene-wide darkening no author asked for. The one white texel it replaces was a stand-in for a *binding* and is still that; conflating the two is why every untextured part in the engine was flat white. One gap, filed as **D00032**: deleting a `Material` child leaves the texture it last resolved, because nothing visits a part that no longer has one
- [x] **seed the store with public-domain materials** - `scripts/fetch-materials.py`, stdlib-only, over **ambientCG, Poly Haven and cgbookcase**, all three CC0. **291 materials, 2.7 GB of source**, at 1K with the full map set - colour, normal, roughness, ambient occlusion and height - of which only the colour map is *named* by the `.mat`, because only a colour map is sampled today and a key nothing read would be half a feature. The other four are published beside it as ordinary textures and are waiting for v0.11's G-buffer, so what is missing then is a field and a pass rather than the pixels. **The map suffixes are normalised and all three sources disagree about every one of them** - `Color`/`BaseColor`/`Diffuse`, `NormalGL`/`nor_gl`/`Normal` - and a `.mat` naming whichever spelling its source used would make the material format's reference depend on where the pixels came from, which is a dialect in the one place this engine has been careful not to grow one. The script stops at a staging tree and `assetc` bakes it into `raw/`: three steps, each already having one implementation, and a downloader that grew a second would be the mistake `AGENTS.md` names. **`cdn::RawContents` is recursive now**, which it had to be - `cdn::Publish` has always walked `raw/` recursively and named assets by relative path, so a tree there was always publishable, and v0.10's material import is the first thing that writes one, because a material has to *name* its texture and `ImportFile`'s hash rename gives it no name to write. A store of six thousand files listed as empty, which reads as a broken panel
- [x] the other four maps - normal, roughness, ambient occlusion and height are published and **v0.11 samples them.** The G-buffer arrived, which was the stated condition, so `assets::MaterialData` gained the four names and `.mat` the four keys, and `assetc` resolves all five through the one `BakedName` rule. Format version 2, with version 1 still reading - a v1 file is a colour map and nothing else, which is exactly this material with four empty names, so the old format is read by not reading them rather than by a second parser. Ignoring unknown keys turned out to be exactly the right call: every `.mat` already on disk bakes unchanged. It was wider than it looked: a material reached a part as a *single* name, so `MaterialMaps` replaced the catalogue's one, `SurfaceAppearance` gained four, the codec and the demand path followed, `DrawInstance` carries the three the draw path reads, and `DrawSlots` binds them as slots 3 to 5 for the G-buffer alone. Every sampled map ends a batching run, or two instances of one mesh with different normals would draw with whichever came first. Normals are perturbed from a screen-space basis because this vertex format has no tangent. `height` is carried and not sampled - parallax needs a loop this pass has not got. **And the G-buffer's albedo was the shadow map until this change**: `DrawSlots` binds shadow, surface and colour at slots 0 to 2, `gbuffer.frag` declared one sampler at slot 0, so the pass wrote three targets, reported three, and put the shadow atlas in albedo - caught by rendering each target to the screen through the new `output` node and measuring it, which the earlier probe never did **Emissive is built too**, as a sixth name and format version 3: the strength lives in the material target's alpha and the hue comes from the albedo beside it, because storing emitted colour properly wants three more channels and a fourth G-buffer target costs bandwidth on every pixel of every frame for something almost nothing uses - a screen, a sign and a lamp filament all glow the colour they already are. Added after shading and unscaled by the light, since a sign is as bright at midnight as at noon. `fetch-materials.py` has no emissive entry on purpose: the CC0 sources publish one for almost nothing, because what glows is a decision about an object rather than a property of a substance. Verified against a hand-authored map on 29 appearances - the material target's hash moves while its RGB does not, the strength being in alpha, and the lit image goes from mean `(119, 102, 106)` to `(238, 220, 203)`
- [x] **material previews and a material picker that shows one** - the assets panel thumbnailed a texture and a `.amat` had no picture of its own, so every material row drew a dash for ever. It is the engine's own **sphere** wearing the material's colour map, drawn through the preview slot the mesh hover already owns - which is why this arrived as a small change rather than the deferred one this line predicted: "point the studio's viewport at something that is not a world" was built for meshes and a material is the same slot with a texture bound. A sphere because it shows a map at every angle at once - the top faces the light, the limb goes to grazing, and the silhouette is identical for every material, so two are told apart by their surface rather than by their outline. `MakeSphere` duplicates its seam column, which is what stops the map running backwards across one column of triangles. **Resampled to `PREVIEW_MATERIAL_SIDE`**, because this is the one preview that would otherwise grow without a bound: a mesh is capped by its triangle count and nothing evicts a preview, so hovering thirty 2K sheets at full size is half a gigabyte of device memory for pictures of spheres. **White under a texture and grey without one** - the grey stops an untextured mesh being a silhouette, and multiplying a material's own colour map by 0.92 would darken every preview against the part the same material draws on, which is the one thing a thing being looked at to judge a colour must not do. A material naming no map previews as a bare sphere and one naming a map this machine lacks is `Unavailable`, because a grey ball would be a picture of something the material is not. The routing - which kinds are a render rather than a bitmap - is `PreviewIsRendered`, one function rather than the same condition spelled in the hover panel and in the list row
- [x] **the world lifetime policy is somewhere both hosts can reach - `D00017`'s near half** - when a world stops (idle past a limit), when it starts again (something in its inbox) and three refusals lived entirely inside `mono.studio`, and `mono.server` has no `SetState` anywhere. `engine::world::DecideLifecycle` is the decision, at L4 where both programs already link it; **the gathering stays in the studio**, because "somebody is looking at it" is a question only an editor can ask and a scoped run means nothing to a server. **The dividend arrived before the de-duplication did**: `mono.server` is deliberately still not a caller - giving it suspension changes the behaviour of a program whose output `just determinism` compares byte for byte - so nothing is shared yet, and what changed is that a policy previously reachable only by opening the editor and waiting five minutes now has eight cases, two of which were pure comment before. Moving it also surfaced an ordering bug: the idle-clock lookup sat ahead of the suspended-world case, so a teleport waiting on a closed world would have been delayed a frame. Placement - which host a world runs on, and what happens when it dies - is untouched and still has no caller
- [x] **the local store bakes, and content is fetched because something names it** - two bugs with one shape: nothing checked what a store actually contained. `cdn::PublishLocal` published `raw/` **directly**, so a PNG imported through the assets panel reached a client as a PNG and `assets::Texture::Read` refused it - `Texture.hpp`'s whole argument is that a runtime does not decode. This repository's own store held **231 PNGs, 12 BMPs, 6 PMX models and a GLB, and the engine could read none of them**: an `ImageLabel` drew its missing-image marker, a part's `ColorMap` did nothing at all, a `MeshPart` drew the fallback cube, and the content picker offered every one of them as a choice. So the store has a **`baked/`** folder now and publishes that; **nothing in `mono.cdn` bakes**, because a baker is `Engine::bake`'s decoders and that is exactly the interpretation an origin must not do - the two programs that can link both do it instead. Publishing an unbaked store is **refused**, because an empty manifest written over a working one reads as a store somebody deleted. The second bug only appears once the first is fixed: `RequestKind` asked for **every** texture in the catalogue, `render::TextureTable`'s 512 MB went in manifest order, and 1,463 uploads were refused - so the sheets a scene named were usually among the refused. Textures are demand-driven now (`client::CollectWantedTextures`), meshes and materials still by kind because both are small and a material has to arrive before anything knows which texture it names. **A material's sheet is deliberately not requested when the material arrives**, which was the version that did not work: 295 materials arriving is 295 textures requested, which is requesting by kind again one indirection later. It reaches a part through `ResolveMaterials`, which writes it into a field the collector already reads. And `assets::IsRuntimeReadable` is the one place the two halves of the extension table are told apart, so a picker no longer offers a `.pmx` that can never draw
- [x] **a constant identity for the local store** - `cdn::DevelopmentSigningKey`, in the source and not a secret. A signature answers "did the publisher I trust produce this", and for a folder on somebody's own disk serving their own editor the answer is always yes; the key was pure friction, with the same sixty-four characters typed for `--publish` and again for `--publisher-key` and a client that refused every asset when one was wrong. **The client trusts it only when it is using the default local store and nobody named an origin** - naming any source leaves the key required, because a key everybody knows is not a trust boundary and must never become one for content somebody else served. `cdn --publish` still requires one outright
- [x] **the interface's image hook, connected** - `ui::ImageSource` and `render::InterfacePass::SetImageSource` are both hooks taking a resolver "from whoever owns the textures", and **nothing had ever supplied one**, so every `ImageLabel` in the engine drew the atlas's white texel whatever had loaded. The seam was right and one end of it was never connected - on both paths, the client's own GPU pass and the studio's imgui painter. The size travels with the handle, because a nine-sliced or tiled image is laid out in source pixels and a resolver returning a handle alone draws every slice at the wrong scale
- [x] **`.gif` as an animated texture** - the pixels, the grid, the frame count and the rate all arrived at v0.10 and **every pass sampled cell zero**, so a GIF was a still of its first frame - which reads as the decoder having dropped the animation rather than as nothing advancing it. `render::FlipbookCell` is the half that advances it, and it is arithmetic rather than a system because *three* passes need the same answer: the opaque pass for a part, the interface pass for an `ImageLabel`, and the studio's imgui painter for the same label in a viewport panel. **A scale and an offset rather than a cell index**, so a still image is the identity and no shader needs a branch per fragment. **`fract` before the transform**, because an imported mesh's UVs run past one and the sampler repeats them - right for a whole sheet and catastrophic for a cell, where 1.2 would land two frames along and show several at once. **Wrapped on the frame count and not on the grid**: a 24-frame GIF lands in an 8x8 with forty cells empty, and walking all sixty-four would spend five eighths of every loop blank. The clock is the caller's, for the standing rule every module here keeps - so a paused editor holds its frame with no second mechanism, and the modulo is taken in `double` because a `float` count stops resolving whole numbers after nineteen days and the animation would stutter and then stop. Verified against this store's own `fox_dance.gif`: two captures from a pinned camera, 50,880 pixels different and all of them inside the part
- [x] **a raw tab in the picker that bakes on demand** - `raw/` was invisible to the picker, so a file somebody had just dragged in could not be chosen until the whole store was republished, which on this repository's store is four minutes. Two tabs now, and picking a raw row bakes **that one source** through `assetc::Settings::Only` - a filter on the real walk rather than a second entry point, so a material picked on its own still has its colour map rewritten through `BakedName`; a shortcut path would be a second spelling of that rule failing only on the editor's side, which is the half nobody tests. The baked bytes go straight into this editor's renderer, so the asset appears in the viewport before any publish, and the tab says plainly that a publish is what a *client* needs. **The name written into the property is the baked one**, because a picker handing back a `.png` would put a source form on a `ColorMap` - the exact thing this store spent four versions doing
- [x] **an edited world is drawn where it is, not where it started** - `World::Present` runs `PreRender` alone and `capture-previous` is a `PreSimulation` system, so a *suspended* world never updates `PreviousTransform`: the draw list interpolated from wherever each part was created toward its real transform, at an alpha that also never advanced. A part dragged in the properties panel stayed at its birthplace while its selection outline moved away from it - two halves of one frame disagreeing about where something is, which reads as a renderer fault. A world that is not ticking is presented at **alpha one**, because there is no next tick to draw towards. **The obvious fix was the wrong one and is recorded rather than quietly dropped**: making an authored write clear `PreviousTransform` - "a teleport, not a simulation step" - reads well and breaks every scripted animation in the engine, because `Rings.luau` sets `CFrame` once a tick and relies on interpolation for smooth motion at 300 frames a second. `client.scene.tick` caught it
- [x] **the editor fetches content** - its delivery client existed and **nothing ever asked it for anything**, and its default source list was an HTTP origin nobody runs with no publisher key, which `DeliverySettings::IsValid` refuses - so `MakeAssetClient` was never called. A `MeshPart` drew the fallback cube however good its `MeshId` was, a `ColorMap` drew nothing, and the assets panel listed a store full of content none of which the viewport could show. Nothing said why: an unconfigured delivery client and one whose fetches fail look identical from a viewport.
