The default is the store on this machine plus `cdn::DevelopmentPublisher`, and the pump is `Client::PumpContent`'s policy one program over - meshes and materials by kind, textures on demand
- [x] **`BasePart` is what its subclasses share** - `Mesh` and `ColorMap` are `MeshPart`'s `MeshId` and `TextureID` now and nothing else's. A mesh reference on a `Part` was a picker that did nothing. The *storage* is unchanged and stays dense on `BasePart` for `CollectInstances`' reason; what moved is the vocabulary. One spelling each also ends the trap `studio/Assets.hpp` warned about, where an alias missing from the picker's table gave a plain text field on the name people use
- [x] **`PVInstance` pivots** - `Pivot::Offset`, `PivotOffset`, and `GetPivot`/`PivotTo` on the script surface. A placement says where a thing's centre is and almost nothing is placed by its centre: a door turns on its hinge, a lid sits on its rim. `PivotTo` is `target * Offset⁻¹` and the inverse is the whole of it - setting the transform to the target and hoping is what "PivotTo ignores the offset" bugs are. Identity is the default, so a part nobody has given a pivot behaves exactly as it did before the field existed
- [x] **the asset picker is clipped, and three bugs went with it** - it listed every texture in the store, 1,637 of them, submitting a `Selectable` and asking for a thumbnail for each on every frame the modal was open. That is a visible stall; worse, `ThumbnailFor` queues what it is asked for against a 256-entry cache, so asking for 1,637 a frame evicted each picture long before its turn to be built came round - the previews were not failing, they were being thrown away, and the list looked permanently broken after the first interaction. The picker was small when it was written and stopped being small when the store filled up
- [x] **the gallery scans once rather than every frame** - `DrawGallery` rebuilt when `Gallery.empty()`, and an empty result *is* a result: a place naming no assets rescanned every entity of every world on every frame the panel was open, which is what "the studio greys out for three seconds when I insert something" was. The gate is "have I scanned", and a structural change invalidates it
- [x] **previews follow the store's layout** - every preview read `raw/<name>`, defended by a comment that was true for exactly as long as the publisher walked `raw/`. The day it walked `baked/`, every published name resolved to a file that was not there and silently became "no local pixels". `cdn::FindInStore` is the one place that knows where an asset lives
- [x] **nothing is fetched by kind** - the editor froze for half a minute on start-up and the reason is one sentence from `delivery/Client.hpp`: *the unit that travels is a bundle*. Asking for every mesh and material by kind asks for essentially every bundle in the store, and `AssetClient::Pump` resolves, verifies and decompresses all of it **on the calling thread** - deliberately, because a completion arriving at a moment scheduling chose would be a desync. On this repository's own store that is 6.9 GB through one function on the frame the studio opens. A world names an asset or it is not fetched: **studio start-up went from about 29 seconds to 1.4**, and a scene naming one mesh fetches one mesh, in 26 ms. **Asynchrony here cannot mean a thread and does not pretend to** - what is spread is the *asking*, a bounded number of new requests per pump, so a large place arrives over a second or two instead of in a frame that never ends
- [x] **the picker wrote nothing, and one unset field is the whole of four bug reports** - "setting a mesh does nothing", "the cube never changes", "the texture never appears", "the mesh id stays blank". `game::PropertyValue` defaults to `PropertyType::Opaque` and the picker's confirm built one by hand, filling the name and never the type; `game::WriteProperty` refuses a type mismatch on its **first line**, and refuses silently, because a refusal there is ordinarily a script error somebody else reports. So the editor's most obvious control had been inert for five properties for a version, and every symptom pointed at content delivery rather than at the panel. **The fix is a constructor rather than an assignment** - `ChosenContentValue(type, chosen)` is the one place a chosen row becomes a value, so the type cannot be filled in by whoever remembers; the `...` button now carries `descriptor->Type` to it. Worth stating because the shape recurs: a struct whose default is a *refusal* is safe against the code that forgets to set it and useless against the code that half-sets it
- [x] **the mesh preview blanked the whole editor**, which is the same bug class one layer up and was reported as "hovering a row makes the engine go blank". `RenderPreviewSlot` passed `nullptr` where the interface goes, so the frame it drew wrote no imgui at all - and `Renderer::Render` owns the swapchain, so it cleared and presented an empty one. The panel was not failing to draw a mesh; it was drawing a mesh *instead of the editor*. **And the preview took the frame rather than a slot in it**: it returned early from `PresentWorld`, so a hovered row pre-empted every viewport for as long as the pointer sat there. It is a rotation candidate now, which is what the round-robin was already for. The mesh turns - `TURNTABLE_RADIANS_PER_SECOND` about the camera, not the mesh, so nothing writes a component to animate an icon - and built-ins resolve through `assets::MakeBuiltin` rather than through the store, because the six shapes the engine ships have no bundle to fetch
- [x] **a scene can ask what the store published**, which it could not, and the reason is the previous version's own rule biting. `GetMeshes()` reports what the catalogue *holds*, and since "nothing is fetched by kind" a catalogue holds only what something already named - so a scene reading it to decide what to name can never be the thing that asks, and the example grid filled only for a place that already named imports. `scene::PublishedCatalogue` is a derived resource holding `core::Name`s, and `ContentService:GetPublishedMeshes()` reads it. **Names rather than `cdn::PublishedEntry` because `scene` is L7 and cannot see `assets::AssetKind`** - the filtering by kind and by `IsRuntimeReadable` happens in the studio, which links both, and what crosses is the answer rather than the question. Offered per pump against a count, not once, because `Play` mints a server world and a client replica *after* the catalogue opens and a one-shot offer leaves both empty
- [x] **`MeshPart.Size` is a box and not a multiplier, which is what makes the cull correct** - reported as "the culling is deleting them". `graph::CullAndBound` tests `scene::Bounds` against the frustum, and nothing had ever tied the drawn geometry to that box: a PMX baked at authored scale extended ten times past the bounds describing it, so it vanished while still plainly on screen. `MeshEntry` now carries the mesh's own `Centre` and `Extent`, derived by `Mesh::Read` from the vertices so a file cannot lie about its own, and the renderer scales by `HalfExtent / Extent` - per axis, so a part's size *stretches* the mesh onto it rather than fitting it inside. **Geometry fills its bounds by construction**, which retires the mesh-aware cull this was going to need instead. Built-ins are a unit shape about their own origin, so `HalfExtent / 0.5` is `HalfExtent * 2` and every built-in draws exactly as it did before the field existed. The centre is subtracted before scaling, so a model authored off-origin sits where its part is
- [x] **swapping a mesh keeps the part's size, and that is a name rather than a flag** - selecting a `MeshId` fits the part to the mesh's natural bounds once, so a part resized afterwards stays resized and a *replacement* mesh does not resize it back. `Visual::Fitted` records which mesh the box was last shaped to, so `Fitted != Mesh` **is** the condition. A bool would need clearing in every path that writes `Mesh` - the properties panel, a script, a game file, a replication delta - and whichever one forgot would produce a part that silently stopped fitting. It is serialised, which is what makes reopening a place leave sizes alone, and it costs four bytes on every part including every plain `Part` that will never name a mesh - the trade `SurfaceAppearance` already makes to keep `CollectInstances` a flat walk. `sizeof(Visual)` is pinned by a padding test, which failed on the first build and now carries the reasoning
- [x] **a texture reference that resolves to nothing is now refused rather than baked** - reported as "textures aren't loading for a bunch of them". `assetc::Resolve` is purely lexical and never asked whether the file it named exists, and `ImportFile` flattens the store to `<hash><extension>` - so importing a model **destroys the directory relationship its texture references are written against**, and every one of them resolved to a plausible path that was not there. `cdn::StoreTextureResolver` walks the content log back the other way: model hash → original path → its directory → the reference → normalise → hash. What the bake gains beside it is the check that was always missing - the resolved file must be a regular file in the input tree - with `Report::DanglingTextures` counting what it dropped and a warning naming the model and the reference. **The pre-existing test would have passed on every day the bug was live**: it asserted the bitmap baked, not that the model still pointed at it
- [x] **custom models bake at unit scale** - `Settings::ModelSize = 1.0f` in both `contentimport` and the studio, so an imported mesh's natural bounds are a unit box whatever the authoring tool thought a metre was. This was load-bearing when it landed and is now merely conventional: the stretch above makes a mesh unable to escape its bounds at any bake scale, so what the unit bake buys is a library where two models are the same size until somebody says otherwise
- [x] **the headless smoke shot photographs a named world** - `--capture-world NAME` moves the active viewport before the first frame, because a capture photographs whichever world the drawing panel holds and only the panel that drew has anything in it. A name nothing answers to warns and falls back rather than aborting, since a capture is a diagnostic and one that killed a run over a renamed scene would be worse than one that shoots the default. `studio-smoke` takes a second shot of the mesh grid at **700 frames rather than twelve** - naming a mesh is what fetches it, the fetch spans frames, and the grid grows on `Heartbeat`, so an early capture photographs an empty plate and asserts nothing. **This is the gap that mattered**: every other check here is a count, and a count reads identically whether the models are right, squashed, inside-out, untextured or culled, all five of which happened at least once
- [x] **the light cap has one home - `D00029` closed** - `render::MAX_SCENE_LIGHTS` was 16 and `MAX_LIGHTS` in `opaque.frag` was 16, and the only thing keeping them equal was that somebody wrote both. `mono_add_library` takes `SHADER_DEFINES` now and the configure **reads** the value out of the header to pass `-DMAX_LIGHTS` to glslc, so the shader keeps no literal to drift. **The number stayed in C++ rather than moving into the build**, which is where the deferred entry's plan changed: `Renderer.hpp` is included by targets that do not link `render`, so a compile definition would have needed a fallback default - the second spelling again. **Demonstrated by mutation**: the header set to 8 changes the command line and the staged SPIR-V's hash. There is deliberately no test, because the disagreement is now unrepresentable rather than detectable - and a test could never have read the number anyway, which is what the first attempt at this discovered
- [x] **a part is cleared when its material goes away - `D00032` closed** - deleting a `Material` left the colour map it last resolved sitting on the parent for ever, because nothing walks a part that has no material. **Not closed with the destruction hook the entry proposed, which would have been harder and weaker**: `DropAttributes` is a hard-coded call inside `DestroyEntity` rather than a registration, and `scene` sits above `ecs` - but more to the point, a hook catches only destruction, and a `Material` **reparented** to another part destroys nothing while leaving exactly the same stale texture. What shipped is a difference between two passes: `ResolveMaterials` records the parents it wrote and the next pass clears any it did not write again, which is O(materials) rather than the O(parts) walk the entry rejected. **The entry named the symptom it had seen and wrote the fix for that symptom** - the trigger was "deleted" and the defect was "stopped being written". Handles rather than indices, because a freed index is reused immediately; the set is cleared on load, because a handle does not survive a directory replacement
- [x] **the mirror stopped flashing - `D00027` closed** - once per orbit the reflected camera whipped 180°, because `facing` flips sign as the viewer crosses the pane's plane and there is no continuous path between the two. **Fixed by deciding what a surface view means rather than by arithmetic**: a pane the viewer is level with covers no pixels, so within `EDGE_ON_MARGIN` it renders nothing and its pane comes off its slot. The sign still flips and no frame is shown while it does. Worst single-step turn over a 360-sample lap went from **exactly 2.0 to exactly 0**, and the zero is not a tuned bound - within one side of the plane the orientation does not change at all. Closing it forced the test to be rewritten, which surfaced the better question: continuity is only owed to frames that *draw*, and a second assertion now requires the blank band to actually be entered so the test cannot pass by no longer crossing the plane. **Two existing cases turned out to be pinning the degenerate arrangement without saying so** - one required a clamped frustum for a view nobody can see, and "a rotated pane reflects along the way it actually faces" had its eye exactly level with the rotated normal, so it passed for a reflection that was never computed

- [x] **`just docs-check`'s first pass is green, and the pass behind it has run for the first time - `D00035` closed** - the recipe had been failing on 19 lines, and **fixing the first one made the count go up**: the site pass had been stopping at a dangling `README` link, so the seven it then reported had never been seen, two of them live source defects where a new member's doc block had been inserted *inside* an existing one and left `bake::Graph::AddWrite` and `render::Renderer::TextureHandle` documented as the wrong function. The eighteen remaining had one cause, and it is a collision between a Doxygen setting and this repository's house style: `JAVADOC_AUTOBRIEF` ends the brief at the first sentence stop and does not care that the stop is inside emphasis, so `**A bold sentence.**` is split between the `**` and its partner. **Emphasis spanning a line break was the wrong suspect and was believed for an afternoon** - the first minimal reproduction held the stop inside the bold while dashes, quotes and wrapping were varied around it, so everything failed and wrapping took the blame. `docgen::Promote` now moves the stop outside the emphasis, which preserves the line count every source link depends on and leaves 355 bolds across 145 headers alone. The last one was in Markdown, which is not filtered: a code span containing apostrophes, found by neutralising each of its line's sixteen spans in turn. **What that uncovered is `D00036`** - the coverage pass behind it reports 307 undocumented public entities, and it had never completed once

- [x] **the asset that baked and never published has a name - `D00034` closed** - the store re-baked to 1974 raw and 1973 baked, and the gap was only ever visible by subtracting two numbers printed by two different tools. It was not the baker and not the publisher: `cdn::ImportFile` accepted a **zero-byte file**. Found by diffing the two folders by hash, which left exactly one - `af1349b9…`, BLAKE3's empty-input digest - and reading the content log, which named `blender-dragon/.venv/.lock`, a Python virtualenv lock file swept along by the folder import of a model. **The entry's diagnosis was right and its location was wrong**, which is the part worth keeping: "the reporting is the defect, not the missing asset" held exactly, but the missing report was three stages upstream of where it was looked for - `cdn::Publish` already names an empty file it skips, and by then the file has been in `raw/` for good. It is refused at import now, by name, with nothing written and nothing logged, because a refusal that left the file behind would move the silence rather than remove it

- [x] **a rendered preview outlives the cursor - `D00033` closed** - a mesh or a material had a picture only while it was hovered, because the preview slot is scratch: there are a handful, they are drawn into on rotation, and whatever is in one is gone within a few frames. `Renderer::CaptureSceneTexture` blits a slot into a new texture and publishes it through a new `TextureTable::Adopt`, **device to device** - reading a picture back to the host only to upload it again would be a round trip across the bus for bytes that never needed to leave the GPU. **`Adopt` transfers ownership, which is the whole contract**: the table releases on `Drop`, on replacement and on `Shutdown` exactly as for one it uploaded, so every refusal has to leave the caller still owning the texture, and a full table returns before releasing what was under the name rather than after. **The drawn rectangle is copied and not the allocation** - a target is rounded up to 64-pixel blocks, so copying it whole would keep an unwritten margin down two edges and force every consumer to carry `SceneTextureExtent` beside the handle. It lands in the studio's existing thumbnail cache rather than beside it, so `PumpThumbnails` evicts a capture by the same rule and the same call, and is taken once per asset rather than once per frame. **Every refusal is tested and none of the success is**: a capture needs a device, and standing a fake in front of that would test the fake

- [x] **every public entity is documented, and the check that says so has run to completion for the first time - `D00036` closed** - `docs-check`'s coverage pass had never finished, because the site pass in front of it had been red for two versions and the recipe stops at the first. Fixing that (`D00035`) let it report **307** gaps, all of them genuinely public. **About a third were unattached rather than unwritten**: `ecs::AttributeValue`'s fifteen payload fields are one idea, and `gui::Describe`'s nineteen overloads sit under one paragraph about all nineteen - Doxygen attaches a comment to the declaration beneath it, so the rest counted as missing. `DISTRIBUTE_GROUP_DOC` plus `//@{` documents each family once, which is not a lowered bar: the comment is still written and the group still marked by hand. The remainder was writing, and the entries worth having are the ones where a field's name hides the trap - `Delta::Baseline`, why `Forgotten` is never merged with `Destroyed`, and `Refused` against `Deferred`. It also turned up **three more orphaned doc blocks** of the kind that left `bake::Graph::AddWrite` documented as the wrong function, all three added by this version's own mesh work. `just docs-check` exits 0

### v0.11

* deferred rendering pipeline stuff, need prototype project

### v0.12

- [x] expose ECS underlying to luau and typescript (query engine for entities using direct and returns instances, create custom components and create custom entities and attach components, set component values, etc).
- [x] rojo folder syncing + tests, support multi-world by subfoldering (main.universe.json for universe mapping, main.default.json accepted beside Rojo's own default.project.json in each subfolder)
- [x] world => rojo sync, universe => multi-rojo sync (each world syncs separately, so one bad project file costs its own world and the rest still sync)
- [x] the rest of Rojo's file table: .meta.json and init.meta.json patch properties onto whatever the file of that stem built, .model.json builds a class with its properties and children, .json becomes a ModuleScript with generated source, .txt and .csv become a StringValue and a LocalizationTable over the new scene::TextContent, and a nested .project.json is followed with a cycle check.
- [x] room in scene::Visual again - Surface, CastShadow and Locked used the original three padding bytes, so it was widened once on purpose and the next four one-byte fields are free. engine.scene.components pins the size.
- [x] expanded ecs::Schemas from 256 to 2048 described components. The cap was never the real constraint: the generated hook bodies were inlinable, so every thunk carried a copy of the PropertyType switch - 113 MB of object at 4096 slots. Held out of line it is about 192 bytes of .text a slot, and the measurements are in Schema.cpp.
- [x] update built-in MCP integrations (component_list, entity_query, component_get, component_set over the v0.12 storage surface; first suite for `control`), add a studio ui button to enable/disable and information panel
- [x] walked the deferred register. Every open entry's reopen trigger is unfired: `D00102` wants the `bake` module split, `D00039` wants a world that ticks physics (v0.13), `D00038`/`D00046`/`D00103` want a render pass executor, `D00030`/`D00031` are tooling gaps with working workarounds, and `D00014`/`D00015`/`D00018`/`D00019` are net and replication decisions waiting on a deployment.
- [x] create a api for setting up configs in the studio and saving them to the ~/Documents/atomic-game-engine/studio folder. Save for preferences.json, cdn.json, recent.json (last 5 projects), keybinds.json. Also materials preview now renders without a hover and fetches a missing colour map through the delivery client.
- [x] studio editing tools; select, move, rotate, 3d scene interactable gimbals, grid step amount input, rotation amount input, anchor toggle, "lock" toggle, pivot editor mode, reset pivot button.
- [x] studio tooling tabs (Home, Model, Script, View)
- [x] improved physics pipeline with spatial optimisations - the broad phase's grids size themselves from the colliders they hold (spatial::SuggestCellSize), which lands on the hand-picked optimum at every density: 22% off the default at 4000 colliders, 17% at 1000. An author who names a size still gets it.
- [x] plugin system - a plugin is a folder in the studio config directory holding a plugin.json and a script, run as its own script::Runtime against the world being edited. It reaches everything a game script does, including World; the selection crosses as the studio.Selected component rather than as an API. One runtime each and one failure each: a plugin that will not start, or that throws three beats running, is switched off and named while the rest keep running.
- [x] plugin editor API - script::HostSurface is the seam (one virtual, a value tree, no lua_State crossing a module boundary) and script::HostValue carries an Instance and a Callback where ScriptValue carries neither. A plugin creates toolbar sections and buttons with real handlers, dock widgets that draw immediate-mode like every other panel, and reads or writes the source of scripts in the scene. The selection is `game:GetService("Selection")` with `:Get`, `:Set`, `:Add` and `:Remove`, matching Roblox - a dotted host name installs a service global, which `GetService` already resolved. The three writers take an array of Instance: the wrong shape is refused with a message saying what to write instead, an item that is the wrong type or has been destroyed is skipped with one warning naming which, and an empty array means "deselect everything". `D00105` is closed.
- [x] breakpoints in Luau, in the script editor and in a tab window - a clickable gutter beside the code toggles one, the Debugger panel is a capture list beside a Call Stack / Locals / Upvalues tab bar, and upvalues are captured per frame and kept apart from locals. `BreakpointService` is the same debugger reached from a script, high level over a script instance and low level over a chunk name, and it is a studio's alone.
- [x] asynchronous mesh loading - two halves, and the second was the one that interrupted. `scene::KeepLoaded` drops an instance naming a mesh the renderer does not hold while keeping one naming no mesh at all, so a `MeshPart` is invisible until its geometry lands instead of coming up as the default cube; `delivery::IntakeBudget` caps what one frame decodes and uploads at two megabytes, deferring the rest to the next frame rather than draining a forty-mesh burst in the frame that noticed it. The first arrival of a frame is always admitted, so an asset larger than the whole budget loads in one long frame instead of never. A part naming a texture the renderer does not hold now draws render::MissingTexture - a purple-and-black checkerboard, with the base colour neutralised so it cannot be tinted into looking deliberate - rather than the default plastic a part nobody textured gets. `D00107` records that the renderer cannot yet tell a streaming sheet from one that will never arrive.

### v0.13

- [x] create a unified networking system for LAN, Peer2Peer and remote connections - `mono.network`, a `shared`-tier member above `Engine::net` and below every program, imported by the client, the server, the studio and the cdn. **It ends at an `engine::net::Endpoint` and deliberately opens no connection**: `replication::Connector` already takes one, a `delivery::Source` already names one, and a second answer to "what is a connected session" would be the most expensive kind of debt here. What is unified is the table above it - a session heard on the subnet, one listed by a rendezvous point and one typed into a config file are three rows differing in a `Reach` and nothing else, so no caller has an "is discovery enabled" branch. A host announces once a second from an ephemeral port to a well-known one, rather than answering probes, so a machine can host as many sessions as it likes and a beacon answers nothing a stranger sends it. Peer-to-peer is a real punch: the point introduces two peers with a nonce it drew, both poke until one gets through, and there is **no relay** - a pair of routers that will not cooperate produces `ReachState::Failed` rather than a hidden bandwidth commitment. `Access::Private` is a pre-shared `SessionKey` - 64 hex characters or a passphrase, PBKDF2 over a fixed salt, and the vector is locked in a suite because changing the derivation silently invalidates every key anybody was given. It authenticates and does not hide: a private session on a subnet is visible to that subnet and joinable by nobody without the key, which is why a browser lists it as locked rather than dropping it. The rendezvous point holds no keys and must not, so a private registration is unlisted rather than gated and its 128-bit id is what stands in for a listing.
- [x] the engine's half - `server --advertise --session-name --session-key --rendezvous`, `client --browse --session-id --session-key --rendezvous`. **The punch happens on the socket the session uses**, because a NAT mapping belongs to a port: `replication::Listener::SetForeign` and `Connector::SetForeign` hand a datagram that is not a `net::Packet` back to the discovery layer, and the three formats route by magic (`ATN1`, `ATNA`, `ATNR`). `engine::net` gained `TransportSettings::Broadcast` and `ReuseAddress` and `Endpoint::BroadcastIPv4` for it - and the loopback honours a broadcast, which is what makes the whole discovery protocol a path a suite exercises with real encoding, no socket and no sleeping.
- [x] the cdn's half - `cdn::Stream` and `cdn::StreamFinder`. The four distribution streams are two settings rather than four code paths, because the origin cannot tell them apart: LAN is `--advertise`, peer-to-peer and public are `--rendezvous`, private is `--stream-key`, and `--rendezvous-listen` makes the origin the meeting place other people's sessions use rather than shipping a fifth executable. A discovered stream becomes a `delivery::Source` nearest-first, read-only - uploading to whatever answered a broadcast is how content reaches a machine nobody meant to publish from. `--stream-key` gates *discovery*; a `cdn::Gate` grant still gates delivery, and collapsing the two would make the key that finds a stream the key that draws from it.
- [x] the studio's half - `studio::TeamCreate` and a View → Team Create panel at `Purpose::Studio`, which is the same `network::Presence` the client browses servers with. It holds no socket until somebody opens the panel, and hosting produces a session id and a key to hand over. **Sessions only, and the panel says so**: two editors can find each other, and editing one place together needs a change model with an ordering that this repository has only for a server's world.
- [x] `ChangeHistoryService`, Roblox's shape method for method - `TryBeginRecording`, `FinishRecording` with `Commit`/`Cancel`/`Append`, `IsRecordingInProgress`, `GetCanUndo`/`GetCanRedo`, `Undo`/`Redo`, `SetWaypoint`, `ResetWaypoints`, `SetEnabled`, and the four events. It is backed by the waypoint layer added to `CommandLog` rather than by a history of its own, because a second undo stack beside the editor's would be two answers to what Ctrl+Z does. A recording is a **named, atomic group**: one undo reverses all of it, and one message carries all of it - which is the same primitive the shared document needs, and the reason these two arrived together. `Enum.FinishRecordingOperation` is registered, and an `EnumItem` now crosses the host seam as its member's name, which is the latitude `ReadEnumValue` already gives everywhere else. Two differences from Roblox and both are the seam: `GetCanUndo` returns a table because a host call answers one value, and the events take a handler because the seam has no `RBXScriptSignal`. `PluginSurface` has no unit suite, matching the note already in `tests/Plugins.cpp` - the semantics it wraps are covered by twelve cases over `CommandLog`.
- [x] studio replication for team create - `studio::EditStream`, and it is the third of three pieces rather than a thing of its own. The unit was settled by `CommandLog`: a committed waypoint, handed over whole, because a peer that applied half of a group would show a state the author never saw. The far end was settled by `ApplyForeign`: somebody else's edit lands without entering this author's undo stack. **The middle is an identity and a bus.** The identity is an *instance path* - a list of names from the world's root - because an `EditId` is one log's own name for an instance and both editors issue `1` for their first, so the collision is not a remote possibility but what happens immediately; the suite asserts that collision rather than describing it. Every id on the wire is the receiver's own, minted as it applies. Paths stay consistent because the stream is ordered: a rename is itself a replicated write, so everybody applies it at the same point, which is why the relay goes through the host rather than peer-to-peer among the guests. The bus is `MessageKind::User` on `replication` - a widening of the connected, admitted, encrypted, reliable session that already exists, because a fourth session type beside it is the thing to refuse. Conflicts are settled by taking turns - `studio::EditLocks`. A waypoint's author asks the host for the subtree it touches; the host grants it if it is free and **queues the request behind whoever has it**; the edit goes; the host applies it, relays it, gives the subtree back and grants the next in line. **Nobody is refused for being second**, which is the whole reason it is a queue: the second edit lands *on top of* the first, in the order the host granted, and both people keep what they did. The person never waits - their edit is applied at their own machine the moment they make it and what waits for the turn is the message - and an editor whose optimistic edit is ordered behind somebody else's replays it on top when theirs arrives, so every editor converges on the host's order. A grant carries a short guard rather than a lease on editing: it bounds one protocol step, so an editor handed a subtree that then dies costs the next person a pause rather than the session. A turn covers a subtree, since a turn over a model that let somebody else edit its parts would order nothing. `D00108` is closed.
- [x] two gaps `replication` had all along, found by the first caller that goes quiet. **Nothing ever called `Link::NeedsKeepAlive`** - every caller published a world every tick, so a packet always went out and carried the acknowledgement with it. A session that only carries occasional messages sends nothing between them, so the far side's reliable sender resent to its limit and gave up on a link that was up and healthy, with no symptom but sends beginning to return false. The keep-alive timer alone does not close it, because a sender reaches `MaximumResends` before a one-second keep-alive comes round - so an accepted-and-unacknowledged reliable payload now makes an acknowledgement due immediately. And an empty unreliable payload is that acknowledgement rather than a message: surfacing it as inbound handed every reader a zero-length message it could not parse, which each counted as a refusal.
- [x] a command bar - View → Command Bar, one line of Luau against the active scene, over the same `MakePluginSurface` a plugin gets. **The whole run is one waypoint**, because a line that moves forty parts is one action to the person who typed it and forty presses of Ctrl+Z would make the bar something nobody dares use: `RunCommand` opens a `ChangeHistoryService` recording before it runs and commits it after, which is the recording primitive `EditStream` already needed arriving at its second caller rather than a mechanism of its own. A run that changed nothing cancels instead of committing, so a query leaves no empty step in the Edit menu. The runtime is kept between commands so globals persist, and rebuilt when the active scene changes because a runtime is bound to one store - holding one against a world that has gone is the dangling reference the rebuild exists to avoid. It is the bar's own rather than a plugin's, so a command cannot spend a plugin's step budget or see its globals. Nothing beats it afterwards, so a widget it creates is never drawn: something that wants to be there next frame is a plugin, and a prompt that could quietly become one would be a plugin nobody can find the file for. No suite, for the reason already written in `tests/Plugins.cpp` - the panel draws an input box, and what Enter does is the recording bracket, which twelve cases over `CommandLog` cover.
- [x] full colour customisation - seven colours, three scopes, one ladder. **Seven and not fifty, and that is the design rather than a shortcut**: imgui has fifty-odd style slots and `ui::Theme` has always derived forty of them from five, because a button's face, an input's well, a border and a tab are shades of the surface on one ladder and a person setting them independently is a person putting a control at the wrong distance from the panel behind it. So what is exposed is the five a palette declares plus the semantic pair no palette declares - a warning and an error, which are here because somebody who cannot tell the default red from the default green needs to move it, and a theme that is customisable except for those is not customisable. **An override rides over the palette rather than replacing it**, sparsely: what was chosen is pinned and everything else still follows whichever theme is selected, so choosing a theme after customising still does something - the failure mode a full-copy override would have. The three scopes are the same seven applied in the order of how much thought went into them: palette, then editor-wide, then one panel's own. Per-panel is `engine::ui::ScopedColours`, pushed **around** `Begin` and `End` rather than inside them, because a window's background is read at `Begin` and colours pushed within tint everything except the window; `Editor::Skinned` wraps every panel's draw call in one place so no panel author can get that wrong, and only the slots that actually differ are pushed so a panel that moved one colour does not pay for the other thirty-nine. A plugin's dock widget takes `plugin.SetWidgetColour(id, "Accent", "#FF0080")` and `nil` clears one - the plugin owns the widget it created, and a settings page listing plugin widgets would be a page whose rows appear and vanish as plugins load. Colours cross every boundary as `RRGGBBAA` text, which is the one place the byte order is allowed to differ from `IM_COL32`: a preferences file nobody can read a colour out of is a file nobody can fix by hand, and the swap lives in two functions that are each other's inverse with a suite over the round trip. The editor's override rides in the layout ini beside the palette; the per-panel table is a document and rides in `preferences.json`, where an unreadable name, an unreadable colour and an entry left empty each cost only themselves.

### v0.13.5

- [x] deferred `D00102`'s decided half - `Engine::bakegraph`, the node vocabulary and the bake document format at `shared` tier, linking `Engine::core` and nothing else. **The point is what it does not link.** `bake` is the only thing in the engine that reads a `.glb`, a `.pmx`, a `.png` or a `.bmp`, and its build file states that nothing a shipped game links may link it - so a save format that named `bake` to parse a *text document* would have put every one of those decoders into `server`, which has no reason to decode a JPEG. The register weighed three ways out and settled on the module split as the honest fix; carrying pipelines as opaque text would have given up the load-time validation the render half has, and putting them on the universe rather than the world trades a dependency question for an ownership question nobody has answered. **It was cheaper than the register assumed**: `GraphDocument` needed only `Graph.hpp` and `core`, `Graph.hpp` needed `assets` - which is what a baked mesh *is* rather than a decoder - and only `Graph.cpp`, the evaluator, reaches an importer at all. `Build` is the one function needing both halves and stayed in `bake`; `IsBareNode` became public because a closed list of parameterless node kinds is precisely the thing that must not be copied. The suites split with the code, so `bakegraph`'s proves the format round-trips with no importer linked. **The `<AssetPipelines>` block was deliberately not written**: `Editor::DrawAssetsPipeline`, `WritePipelines`/`ReadPipelines` and `client::InstallWorldPipelines` are all `TODO(render-pipeline)` markers - the panel this entry described went out with the same revert that took `D00038`'s multi-view seam - and a save-format section with no producer and no consumer is a feature that looks present and is not. The format break is cheap to take later precisely because the module split was the expensive part.
- [x] deferred `D00039` closed - the physics module had a caller for the first time. It was complete, tested, benchmarked and connected to nothing: `RegisterPhysicsSystems` was called from `physics/tests/` and nowhere else, and every world this engine shipped was anchored throughout, so integrate, broad phase, narrow phase and solver had never run against a real scene. **Two things were missing and only one of them was the wiring.** `physics` deliberately has no gravity - its own suites say a top-down game should not have to switch one off, so `RigidBody` carries no gravity scale and the pipeline has no gravity step - which means wiring the pipeline alone integrates every body at zero acceleration for ever. That is not hypothetical: the new case failed exactly that way when it was first written against the pipeline alone, which is how the missing half was found. `scene::Gravity` is the second feature: a resource, so a world under water or on the moon authors its own vector, defaulting to 9.81 m/s² down - Earth's in metres, because this engine measures parts in metres and Roblox's 196.2 is the same acceleration in studs. The editor installs both on every world through one `PrepareWorld`, which also closed a smaller bug on the way: five call sites each wrote the same three lines and the one that wrote two left a world restored by Stop without services. A new **Slide** world is the demonstration - a curved ramp of overlapping boxes, blocks spawning at the top and destroyed ten seconds later - chosen over a stack because a stack tests the solver and nothing else, where a slide asks for all four steps at once and each fails visibly. A block was measured leaving the ramp at x 3.4 after 4.9 s and the earlier ones resting by tick 600. `just determinism` is unaffected: the server was deliberately not wired, because giving it suspension-like behaviour changes a program whose output that recipe compares byte for byte.
- [x] deferred `D00018` closed - what a game replicates is one table instead of three. It was written out in `mono.server`, `mono.unified_server_client` and `mono.studio`, and the entry said all three agreed, "which is the only reason this is an entry and not a bug". **Two of the three still did.** The server and the studio matched row for row; the diagnostic harness was missing `scene.Camera`, `scene.SurfaceCamera` and `ecs.Hierarchy` - a mirror, a lens and a parent link - so the copy whose own comment says it exists to make disagreement observable was the one that disagreed, and nothing observed it. That is the entry's prediction arriving in the least useful place to have it. **The table is `replication`'s now, against the entry's own objection**, and the objection is answered by what actually crosses: this names components the way the wire does, as *strings*, and `Authority::Replicate` already takes a `core::Name` - so the module gains no include of `scene`, no link against it, and no knowledge of what a `Transform` contains. What it is not is the real answer, and that has not changed: a `<Replicated>` section in the game document is a per-game declaration that deletes the table rather than moving it, and is the only version that also lets a game replicate a component `scene` does not own. It is now cheaper, because there is one caller to change instead of three. Four cases pin the pairing, the completeness, the absence of duplicates and the qualification of every name - where before, nothing in the build compared the copies at all.
- [x] deferred `D00021` closed - `AutomaticSize` grows a labelled element to its string, which was the half left open when the container half landed at v0.8. **The blocker turned out to be an argument rather than a dependency.** The entry said the text half needed metrics that are *shared* - a font table below L7 - because `gui` is L7 and `render` is L12, so injected metrics would make a client with an atlas lay a tree out differently from a headless server, a studio and a test. That is true of *replacing* the estimate and irrelevant to *using* it: `Layout.hpp` already states that the backend draws at `Resolved::TextSize` and does not second-guess it, so nothing downstream re-measures and within this engine `AVERAGE_ADVANCE` is not an approximation of the truth - it is the measurement, the one answer the hit test, a headless assertion and the renderer all agree on. A box grown to it therefore fits by the same definition of fitting the module uses everywhere else, and the old refusal's stated reason - "a box the text does not fit" - could not happen. `TextScaled` on a grown axis now returns exactly the size the author typed, by construction rather than by a special case: `FittedTextSize` divides the box by exactly the product the growth multiplies. The other failure the refusal named is still refused and now structurally - a `TextLabel` has no children, and a labelled element is sized from its text rather than from the children path that would have collapsed it to nothing. What remains genuinely open is unchanged and is *not* this entry: the estimate may be wrong about real glyphs, which is the risk `TextScaled` has carried since v0.8, and closing it means metrics shared below L7 rather than a second opinion at the point of use.
- [x] the replicated set is derived rather than listed - everything a world holds that is shared, less a named list of what is local. The allow-list of nine components was the wrong shape for the same reason the three copies were: a component added to `scene` did not cross until somebody remembered, and remembering is what had already failed. **The question a host answers is now which components are *local*, which is a much shorter list and one where every entry has a reason** - `ActiveCamera` and `CameraController` because the client mints its own viewpoint and a replicated one would be a second answer to which eye the world is seen through; `InputState` and `LocalPlayer` because they are the receiving machine's own; `PreviousTransform`, `Rendered` and `QuickHash` because they are rebuilt every frame by whichever machine draws, so sending them is wire spent on a value the far side overwrites; `Transient` because not being kept is its whole point. **`scene.Camera` itself crosses, and that distinction cost a test to find**: it is a *lens* rather than a viewpoint, a `SurfaceCamera` carries one, and excluding it left `AimSurfaceCameras` with nothing to aim - every mirror a flat grey rectangle on every client, caught by `studio.playlink`'s "a mirror arrives on the client whole". A type that is not trivially copyable is skipped too, because everything but the two tick-written components is signature-detected and a signature needs a hash: the three catalogues are resources holding maps, they hang off no entity, and they arrived only by sharing the prefix - declaring them was a warning per host per run. `Authority::Replicate` stays opt-in and unchanged; what moved is where the deciding is written down.
- [x] network ownership - who simulates a body, and who is allowed to say where it is. The question was whether server authority was enforced by anything, and the answer was that it was enforced by there being no other option: the server was the sole authority for everything because nothing else could write, and **the server was not simulating either**. That is the first half - `D00039` gave physics a caller in the studio and deliberately stopped there, so a game hosted headlessly integrated no body and resolved no contact. A part dropped in a hosted world hung in the air and every client watching it agreed, because they were watching an authority that was not simulating. `PrepareSimulation` is the server's half of `Editor::PrepareWorld`, on the two paths that host a real scene and deliberately not on the placeholder, which is a benchmark whose own `Integrate` and `Bounce` are what it exists to measure. Hosting `examples/Slide.luau` headlessly now prints that example's own launch report from a process with no renderer in it. **`scene::NetworkOwner` names a `Player` rather than a `ClientId`** - a client handle is `replication`'s at L11 and this is `scene` at L7, but the deeper reason is that a script is what assigns ownership and a script has a player, not a socket. Absent means the server, so ownership costs an entirely server-owned game one archetype nobody visits. **The wire needed no new message kind**: a delta going up is the same bytes as one coming down, so `Authority::Receive` stopped refusing `MessageKind::Delta` and `Connector::SubmitState` sends one - while a snapshot or a structure message from a client is still refused, because those are a client saying what the world *contains*. **The trust decision is written down as a decision.** Authorisation is ownership, checked per entity per message through a host-supplied predicate; owning an entity does not grant a component, because a client may only write what the server already sends it; and plausibility is deliberately *not* checked in the engine, which does not know whether this game has teleports, launch pads or a grappling hook - a speed limit invented at that layer is one every game works around and none can tune. No predicate means nothing may be written, so the insecure state is not the one you get by forgetting. **The filter runs at the write and that is mechanical rather than stylistic**: a component's values are one packed stream in entity order and only the descriptor knows how long one value is, so stripping entities from a `ComponentDelta` leaves their bytes behind and shifts every later value onto the wrong row - a case that fails with `700.0f == 42.0f`, the unowned client's position landing on the owned part, with every other assertion still passing. **`scene::AddPlayer` had no production caller**, which was the missing link: every world this engine ran had a `Players` service with nobody in it, so `SetNetworkOwner` had no argument obtainable at run time. A connection now becomes a player named for its slot rather than for anything it said, and dropping one destroys it - which is what makes the `scene.ownership` reclaim fire, so a body owned by somebody who left goes back to the server on the next `PreSimulation` instead of being owned for ever by a dead entity. Verified against three real clients over real UDP against a real server process. What did **not** land is `replication::Prediction`'s caller, and the reason is `D00109`: an owned entity is simulated by its owner and never corrected, so ownership describes a third category prediction deliberately does not cover. The larger finding is that nothing has ever called `Connector::Submit` at all - the server's whole rewind-and-hit-test path decodes an input no client has ever sent.
- [x] walked the deferred register against the tree rather than against itself, which is a different exercise and found three entries wrong. **`D00007`'s open half was built at v0.9 and nobody told the entry.** It asked for "a server-side history buffer of past ticks that `replication` deliberately does not keep" and a fairness policy that "is a game-design decision rather than an engine one" - `replication::Rewind` is the buffer, `RewindSettings::HistoryTicks` is the policy, and the entry's own sentence about fairness is *quoted in that header*, so it was built from this entry by somebody who did not come back to close it. Its reopen trigger, "the first hitscan weapon", had also fired: `examples::Shooting` is one and `Server::ApplyInputs` rewinds by the interpolation delay plus half the round trip before hit-testing against the history rather than the world. Retired. What is left of it - that nothing has ever *sent* a shot - is `D00109`'s and was found from the other end. **`D00046` names five things that no longer exist**: `ProfilePass::Elapsed`, `PIPELINE_NODES.md`, `graph::Execute`, `FrameRunner::Run`'s debug groups and `FrameResult::UploadedBytes`/`Uploads`. All went out with the render-pipeline revert, exactly as `D00038` and `D00103` already record for `render::View` and `VulkanTimestamps` - so the revert has now invalidated a claim in three separate entries, and the two GPU-timestamp entries are one item seen from two sides. Corrected in place rather than rewritten, and given the reopen trigger it never had. **`D00015`(c) says the client does not link `physics`**; it links it and has since v0.7, through `Engine::script` beneath `game` and `examples`. Measured on `release`: 8 of `libengine_physics.a`'s 15 members are in the binary - the query half, because a script raycasts - and the six that integrate and solve are dropped because nothing calls `RegisterPhysicsSystems`. The dependency is paid and the caller is not, which is `D00004`'s link-line-versus-call-graph distinction landing in a second entry. **And `D00109` was filed as `D00108`, a number retired at v0.13 by `studio::EditStream`** - picked by reading the front of the live file, which is the half of the register that does not contain the used numbers. Renumbered.
- [x] use tools and see what needs fixing
- [x] use tools and see what to improve
- [x] the ribbon, and the gizmo work that came with it. The Tools panel became the toolbar's second row - it floated over the viewport it was editing and carried a second copy of the manipulators the toolbar already had, so the same three buttons existed twice and were free to disagree. Four tool actions are registered commands now (`Select Tool`, `Move Tool`, `Rotate Tool`, `Scale Tool`), which makes the ribbon button, the palette and a binding one answer rather than three; they ship unbound and viewport-scoped, because `tests/Keybinds.cpp` holds the rule that a default nobody asked for is how one key came to mean two things in three files, and a viewport scope is what lets them be plain digits at all. **A move drag flickered between where the part was and where it was being dragged to**, and the cause is worth writing down: `Grabbed` is a distance along the axis from the selection's centre at grab time, and the centre was being read live - so once a frame applied the delta, the next frame measured from a centre that had already moved, got zero, and put everything back. Alternating. The fix is one captured `Centre` and every ray measurement taken from it; the handles are still drawn at the live centre, because a manipulator left behind is the next thing that looks broken. Move and Scale grew an arm on both ends of each axis, since one arm is a handle that cannot be reached from half the angles somebody orbits to. And a resize now says which faces it moves: `Side` holds the far face still (the default, and what "drag this face" means), `Both` moves both by the step, `Both Half` moves both by half of it - the last two differ in a way a checkbox could not say, which is why it is a list. `Side` is the only drag that writes a size *and* a placement, so the release opens a recording: one waypoint for the whole drag, which also fixed a multi-part drag taking one press of Ctrl+Z per part.
- [x] Select drags, and two buttons about what a drag does. **Select stops being "no tool"**: press on a part and pull, and it goes where the pointer does - resting on whatever the cursor is over, as far into it as the box's own corner reaches, which is `SupportAlong` and is why a turned part sits on a slope instead of sinking into it. Dragging something unselected selects it first; dragging something already selected carries the whole selection by one rigid transform taken from the part that was grabbed, because moving each member to the cursor would pile a built thing into one place. The raycast leaves the dragged parts out of the index rather than filtering them from the hit - the same argument locking already made, one door along: a part that swallowed its own ray would rest on itself and never reach the floor. **Align** turns a dropped part onto the surface it lands on, projecting the old facing onto the new plane rather than discarding it, so parts dropped on one wall do not all end up pointing the same way; `AlignedTo` is a named function with a suite over it because `LookVector` is `-Z` and a mirrored basis is invisible on anything symmetrical. **Facing** draws the look out of the front face to a ball with a ring round it and an arrow at the point that is up - a box says nothing about its orientation, and two parts sitting identically may be turned a quarter apart. Both toggles and the scale-side list ride in `preferences.json`.
- [x] surface effects - `Enum.SurfaceEffect` on a `SurfaceCamera`, and the mirrors example gives each of its four walls one: `NightVision`, `Thermal`, `Cctv`, `Swirl`. **A grade on the way out, not a second render**, which is the whole reason four of them are affordable: the surface pass draws the world exactly as it would have, and the effect is applied in `opaque.frag` where the pane samples the texture - no extra target, no extra pipeline, no extra bind, and switching one at runtime redraws nothing. It also means a reflection of a reflection is graded once, since the texture holds an ungraded picture and only the pane showing it grades. **A closed list rather than a shader name, and that is a limit rather than a stub**: this pipeline has one fragment program for opaque geometry, so a mirror naming an arbitrary one would need a pipeline per program, a compilation path and an answer for a file missing on somebody else's machine - the render graph below, not a field. `Thermal` reads luminance as temperature, which is a lie an engine with no thermal model cannot avoid and `scene::SurfaceEffect` says so out loud. `Swirl` is the one entry that moves texels, so it warps the coordinate before the fetch while the other three grade the colour after it - doing either at the other's moment is doing it to the wrong thing. The uniform got a field of its own rather than the spare lanes in `Surface` or `Flipbook`, because `DrawSlots` rewrites both wholesale per submesh and anything parked there would present as "the effect only works on some parts". The component's byte came out of `SurfaceCamera::Reserved`, which is what a named reserve is for, and `tests/Components.cpp` holds the sum so the day it runs out is a failing case rather than a hole in a snapshot.
- [x] deferred `D00107` closed - a streaming texture and one that will never arrive were the same picture. A sheet lands at least a frame after the mesh that names it, because the intake loop asks for a mesh's own sheets *while decoding the mesh*, and `delivery::IntakeBudget` may spread them over several more - so every imported model wore the purple marker on a scene load, indistinguishable from forty misspellings. **The marker now means only "nothing is coming"**, which is the only meaning that is useful: `render::ChooseTexture` is the rule and a name still in flight draws the default white plastic. Not a timer, and the deferred entry was right that this was the temptation - a grace period hides a genuinely missing texture for exactly as long as it hides a streaming one, and with a byte budget in the path there is no N right for both a small scene and a large one. The renderer is *told* rather than asking, because what is in flight belongs to the content pump and `render` must not reach up into it. **The load-bearing half is the unmark**, and the entry warned that skipping it would be worse than not doing this at all: a host that unmarked only on arrival leaves a misspelled sheet expected for ever and the marker never appears for the one case it exists for. A failed request carries no name - `Take` answers nothing - so `delivery::AssetClient::NameOf` was added for it, and both hosts read the name before taking because a take is what destroys the record. `MeshGrid.luau` and `Meshes.luau` each gained a part naming a sheet nobody published, so both scenes show all three answers side by side and the timing distinction is visible without reading a log.

### v0.14

- [x] rojo: `--rojo`/`$ATOMIC_ROJO_PROJECT` syncs any project at startup; checked against raceapet, which found and fixed two build bugs (a package's `default.project.json` was joined to its folder instead of replacing it, and a nested project's root `$path` was ignored)
- [x] the topbar is tab-based, and the plugins toolbars are a tab of their own rather than a panel
- [x] a Demo tab on the ribbon, and a Demo Nodes panel: a typed node graph with a cycle guard, cached evaluation, save/load and an imgui canvas - `studio/NodeGraph.hpp`
- [x] `PhysicsProperties` on BasePart: density, friction and elasticity as an override of the material, drag through `RigidBody`'s damping, `Mass` derived and read-only, all of it in the properties panel
- [x] `LuaSourceContainer` and `JavaScriptSourceContainer` as two components, with `CodeSourceContainerSelector` choosing which runs - the selector is scriptable and neither container is, which needed `ecs::PropertyDescriptor::Scriptable`
- [x] the full demo: fBm and ridged noise, warp, terrace, slope, threshold and combine; a colouriser and thumbnails on the nodes; erosion and staged tasks that run off the frame with progress, stages and concurrent branches
- [x] the Demo Nodes panel built out to the reference implementation: a picture and a description on the *data type* rather than the node, so an inspector can draw a node's inputs beside its output; per-type inspector handlers picked by name and otherwise inferred from what a node produced; Library, Inspector and Types tabs with live parameters and a port table; snapshot undo, copy/paste, save/load and image export; frames, collapsing, a context menu, insert-on-link and a filtered palette; and compression - a selection folds into one node whose ports are derived from how it was wired, with the fold as a *view over one graph* rather than a nested one, so the evaluator, the cycle guard and the content hash never learn it happened
- [x] the last of it: a 3-D view of any wire that can be read as elevation (`DataType::Heights`, a software rasteriser producing the same `PreviewImage` as everything else, so no render target is involved), a custom library - a fold files as a named type inside the document rather than outside it, and places independent copies through one `Graph::Absorb` that paste also uses - and a PNG encoder, so an exported picture is a picture
- [x] deferred `D00113`: one node graph implementation rather than two
- [x] default engine assets: the six shape meshes and a pink/grey checkerboard, under an "engine" tab, with a tab per cdn source and an "all" tab
- [x] raw folders bake on demand into the editor, memory-only by default, with a tab of their own
- [x] C++ node library suite via imgui in ~/Documents/GitHub/node-graph-template/cpp - model (graph, types, layout, hashing, evaluation, save/load) with no imgui and 93 checks, an imgui canvas over it, and an SDL3 demo
- [x] SETUP-CDN.md: folder-based, a store served from a folder, an origin on localhost and how to expose one; `cdn --ingest-key` added so the editor's Upload has an origin that can accept
- [x] investigated non-euclidean worlds: `NON-EUCLIDEAN.md`. cameras + portal parts is right, and `SurfaceCamera` is most of it - three small changes to how a surface view carries its projection, then traversal, which needs v0.15's character controller
- [x] **portals, and the non-Euclidean spaces they buy** - the first four rows of `NON-EUCLIDEAN.md`'s table, built in the order it argued for because each unblocks the next. `render::SurfaceView` carries a **projection** rather than a field of view; the frustum is fitted **off-axis** to the pane's four corners, which the surface camera already wanted since a symmetric fit spends half a mirror's texels on the far edge it cannot see; and the near plane is skewed onto the destination's plane with a real **oblique clip** - Lengyel's, in its `0..1` depth form, because `GLM_FORCE_DEPTH_ZERO_TO_ONE` is pinned engine-wide and the published `-1..1` derivation would land the near plane somewhere that reads as z-fighting. Then a `Portal` class deriving from `SurfaceCamera` with one `Destination` reference. **The four steps collapse into one code path**: take a placement transform, map the pane by it, fit the camera to the mapped rectangle - a mirror's transform is the reflection through its own plane, which *fixes* that plane, so the mapped rectangle is the pane itself and the old arithmetic falls out of the general rule. The fit must be to the mapped **source** pane and never the destination part, because `opaque.frag` shades a source fragment through the camera's matrix and the two only line up because they moved together. No second renderer, no exotic maths: the impossible space is that nothing constrains the pair of frames to describe one. **The one thing the engine cannot catch is authoring, and the demo made the mistake before the test did**: `Face` is resolved on the *destination* as well, so a hole aimed at a wall whose matching face points out of its room places a camera, fits a frustum, clips obliquely and shows the empty space behind that wall - the first `Portals-1-world.luau` paired north walls with south walls, which is what a corridor looks like on paper, and all four holes rendered nothing. The invariant that catches it is not where the camera stands but that the half-space the clip keeps contains the middle of the room the hole names. `Portals-1-world.luau` now has three rooms 300 apart and six holes - one pair an ordinary adjacency explains, and two that put the vault through both the hall's east and west walls - and it is the fifth world a new game opens with, beside the skygrid, the mirrors, the meshes and the slide. `scripts/demos/run-portals.sh`, `scene/SurfaceCameras.hpp`, `scene::Portal`, `scene::SurfaceLens`
- [x] deferred `D00112`'s traversal half closed below - what remains is the one-frame seam on crossing, which needs the surface pass to recurse in-frame
- [x] as many viewports as somebody wants: the fixed four became a grown list, panels own their imgui titles, and the asset preview's render slot is computed past the last panel rather than fixed - `--viewports N` and a New Viewport menu item
- [x] every shader moved to engine/resources/shaders, owned by a module of its own
- [x] add custom physical properties for BasePart but as a separate PhysicsProperties component. need things like friction, drag, density, etc. Make them visible in properties too.
- [x] create a shared script based but separate LuaSourceContainer and JavaScriptSourceContainer as two separate components.
- [x] add a way to select between luau and javascript for the code as properties for the Script/LocalScript/ModuleScript. Make it a separate component so we can keep both code containers but swap between them. Maybe CodeSourceContainerSelector? This way we can make CodeSourceContainerSelector scriptable but do not allow editing scripts from other scripts by LuaSourceContainer and JavaScriptSourceContainer.
- [x] **autocomplete for luau and js scripting, in the studio's own editor** - pulled forward from v0.15 because the external half was already done: `luau-lsp` and the generated `.d.luau`/`.d.ts` have completed in VS Code since v0.5, and the only place the engine's vocabulary could not be reached was the editor this repository ships. Ctrl+Space, or automatically after a `.`, a `:` or two characters; Enter accepts and Tab still indents, because `imgui_widgets.cpp` asserts that `AllowTabInput` and `CallbackCompletion` are never both set. **Nothing about the surface is written down**: classes, properties and enums come from `ecs::Classes` and `ecs::EnumTable`, and the globals and instance methods from a new `Runtime::Surface` that *walks a freshly built VM* - `_G` in Luau, `globalThis` and `__instanceMethods` in QuickJS. So a global added anywhere in `mono.engine/script` is offered with nothing else changing. That shape was chosen because this module has shipped the other one twice: `Values.cpp` records `Magnitude` and `Unit` promised by `engine.d.luau` for two versions while the run time answered "no member", and `JsSurface.cpp` records a hand-written `10` on a list of sixteen methods. The one surface a walk cannot reach is the fourteen instance signals, which are a branch chain - those are listed beside the chain and `engine.script.vocabulary` checks every offered member against a live VM in both languages. Also offers the tree beside the script, which is the one thing an external language server cannot know. `studio/Complete.hpp`, `script/Vocabulary.hpp`
- [x] **blocky character spawning, and the two engine holes it fell into.** A character is a `Model`, a `HumanoidRootPart` that is the whole capsule, and five anchored limbs carried by `scene::PoseCharacters` - Roblox welds six colliders with `Motor6D`s, and that needs joints, a solver that respects them and a mass distribution nobody authored, none of which changes what a player feels. **The first hole: a sleeping body has no `scene::Motion`.** `physics::Publish` takes it away - the archetype move *is* what sleeping is here - and until now the only thing that ever gave it back was a contact, so a character that stood still settled and could never be walked again: `StepCharacters` had a perfectly good move direction and nowhere to write it. `PhysicsWorld::Wake` is the verb that was missing. **The second: nothing marked an integrated transform changed.** `IntegrateMotion` has said since v0.4 that a consumer needing a replication delta out of an integrated world "marks it in its own publish step", and for three versions there was no such consumer - so a physics-driven body was invisible to `replication` and a character walked on the server and stood still on every client. `ecs::Store::MarkChanged` and one line in `physics::Publish`. **The third: the ground ray hit the character.** It starts *inside* the feet on purpose - a ray that begins exactly on a face is a coin flip about whether it hits it, and the coin lands differently on two machines - so with a root collider the full height of the character the nearest hit is always the character, and `hit->Owner != body` afterwards reads "not grounded" while standing on a floor, for ever. Testing the answer cannot recover it: the floor was never in it. `physics::Raycast` takes an entity to look through, which is what its own comment had said it would eventually want. `scene/Characters.hpp`, `physics/Characters.hpp`
- [x] **very basic character controls (WASD, SPACE), scoped to the body that is yours.** The arithmetic existed and drove *every* humanoid in the world, which is invisible with one player and is two machines fighting over one body with two. `scene::ReadMoveIntent` is the keyboard half split out so a connected client can send an intent without applying one, and `UpdateCharacterControl` now drives the `LocalPlayer`'s character alone - a world with no `LocalPlayer` still drives them all, because there the question has no answer
- [x] **local server: one server, several clients on one machine.** `scripts/demos/run-local-server.sh`, and two things had to exist first. **`scene::LocalPlayer` had no writer anywhere** - it has said since v0.10 that it holds this viewer's player, so `Players.LocalPlayer` was nil on every client that ever ran; it cannot be replicated, because a resource is one row and the answer differs per client, so it travels as a per-client message over `replication::MessageKind::User`. And **movement goes up as intent rather than as state**: `game::MoveInput` says which way a client is walking and the host decides where that puts it, which keeps one simulation and does not hand a client the ability to state where its own body is. `game/Play.hpp`, and `mono.server/tests/Replication.cpp` walks a character with two real clients over a real socket
- [x] **TeleportService, between worlds and natively in the studio - and the bus bug it uncovered.** The Teleport bus, `Postbox::Teleport` and the router's delivery have existed since v0.2, no script could reach any of it, and `PumpDeliveries` dropped every arrival on the floor. Now: a service in both VMs, and the engine admits the arriving player rather than a script, because who is in a game is the host's business. **A barrier is not a tick, and the router assumed it was.** A barrier runs once per host frame and a world's systems run at the world's own rate - the studio measured 200 barriers against 91 ticks - so replacing an inbox unconditionally took mail away before any system saw it, and a world owing catch-up ticks read the same mail once per tick. A teleport was delivered and lost by the first and admitted three times by the second. Two rules now: an empty barrier leaves a full inbox alone, and a world's catch-up ticks after the first see an empty one. The studio follows its player across worlds by name, because a name is the only thing a teleport carries
- [x] **the character through a portal - `D00112`'s traversal half.** It was deferred as blocked on a body to move, and the body arrived early. `scene::CrossPortals` tests the *segment* between `PreviousTransform` and `Transform` for a change of side through the pane, inside the pane's rectangle - a position test misses the tick a walking character was on either side of - and multiplies the body **and its velocity** by the same `destination · half-turn · source⁻¹` the camera goes through. Derived rather than read off `SurfaceLens::Mapping`, because that is presentation and a headless server never aims a surface at all. The seam on the frame you cross is still open and is a render-graph decision
- [x] **studio Play is both halves with somebody in them.** `PlayLink` has held an authority and a replica since v0.7 and nobody was in the replica. It now admits a `Player`, gives it a character and tells the replica which player it is; a viewport showing a client with a body is *played* rather than flown, and looks through that client's own camera. **Spawn Player and Remove Player act on the viewport you are in**, so a Run gains clients one at a time and becomes a Play - and `BeginRun` goes through the same `SpawnPlayer`, so there is one path that admits somebody. A new game opens with two more worlds, `Playground` and `Arena`, with a teleport pad between them
- [x] **a Live Instances panel, and the view that had no way back.** A run is not a scene, and the Worlds panel was being asked to be both: a client's replica had a row among the authored worlds - which is what made its viewport reopenable - while the *server's* view had no row anywhere, because the server is not a separate world at all. Closing that panel lost it. Live Instances lists the run and its clients with a `View` on each, opens itself when a run starts, and holds the counts that used to be a tooltip on a Worlds row. Two things fell out of it: the transport now scopes a **client** viewport to the run that owns it - a replica names no run, so `ModeOf` answered "Edit" and Play from a client panel would have started a *second* run inside somebody's view - and Stop while looking at a client removes that client rather than the scene. `studio/Viewports.hpp` is the panel choice, in a header a test can reach, because opening a second view of a world that is already on screen halves the refresh rate of both and reads as the editor being slow
- [x] **the overlay in a client viewport was aimed at the editor's camera.** `PresentWorld` reads a replica's `ActiveCamera` back after presenting it - that is what makes a client view a client's view - and `ProjectionFor` still built its matrix from the panel's free camera. So the grid slid across the floor as the player walked, a selection outline sat beside the part it outlined, and a click picked whatever was under a camera nobody was looking through. The grid is also gone from a client view entirely: it is authoring furniture, a replica carries no run record so `ModeOf` called it Edit, and the panel it was drawn over is the one picture in this editor that is exactly what a player sees
- [x] deferred `D00116`: a client cannot ask its server to teleport it - the binding refuses on a replica and there is no request channel

- [x] immersive portals (add a new ImmersivePortal of which does cross-world teleportation in real-time, can enter the next world immediately and shows the other world's side through a cross-world texture rendering). Also supports custom logic like "OnPlayerEnterNearby, OnPlayerLeavingNearby, OnPlayerEnterImage, OnPlayerLeftImage", etc. Also custom properties like the rate of which the image updates, range for nearby event, etc. Need a bus system for this.
- [x] also portals are NOT seamless, they are collidable so you cannot sit inbetween them. should be like this sample [Image #3] image attached that lets you sit in-between them. also, create a immersive-portals-demo-1 and immersive-portals-demo-2 which is a single portal block showing cross-world visualisation and immersive teleporting. force the worlds to NOT paused. add a floating colored brick in each world infront of the portal so you can see it from either side - make them different colored.
- [x] again to demonstrate the point of previous first seam issue, left side is the side the character is sitting on in the seam,
  right side is the other side of the portal looking at the character and seeing that its not rendering
  through (the entity is big enough to fit past the portal, but we don't project it or anytihng like that).
  this next image demonstrates the other side of the portal - the blue side has the character but
  its not projecting to the red side's. note: the camera works fine, it projects through the portal and
  renders the character, however, the character parts do not project through (neither does collisions).
  atomic-game-engine/temp/NonEuclidean will show how its done. Ensure both visually and physics-wise it
  propogates through - so even if you sit between the seam, the fact your body has collisions on either side
  will make it so you do not fall between the world (and in the seam you can do a binary threshold for which
  side to handle collisions on where >0.5 = B else A).
  **Built: `NON-EUCLIDEAN.md` Part V, all nine items.** A straddling body is **cut** at the plane and its far half drawn in the room beyond - carried by a seam plane on the row and a run break in `DrawSlots`, and discarded in `opaque.frag` *and* `shadow.frag`, so half a body no longer casts a whole body's shadow. The far half is lit by `R · L`, so one body reads as one body; the portal image stopped being multiplied by its pane's own grey, which was eating forty-five per cent of every far room. Local lamps cross a hole, particles cross a hole, and a caster beside one darkens the floor past it through a 2×2 beam-shadow atlas. The far room's colliders are copied *into* the near one under anything standing in a seam - the way round that needs no impulse mapped back. The pane's rim stopped showing the far room and the recursion terminus fades to ambient. `scene::Sun` is a resource a world can author. And a cross-world pane stopped deleting whoever walked into it: admitting a teleport happened inside the Luau runtime's delivery pump, so a destination that was not running scripts took the payload and left it there. Measured, not eyeballed - `examples/PortalSeam.luau` and `scripts/demos/capture-portal-seam.sh`.
- [x] **read CodeParade's demo back against our own portals, and took the four things it does better** - `NON-EUCLIDEAN.md`. The two engines agree on the maths almost exactly and disagree on the schedule: the demo resolves the portal chain *inside* one frame, depth-first, and we resolve it across frames, one bounce per frame. Nearly every other difference follows from that, and three of the four fixes were never blocked on it. **A visibility gate on the surface pass** - `graph::VisibleSurfaces`, which is the demo's per-portal occlusion query asked on the CPU against boxes the culler already derives; a signature says whether an image *changed* and nothing said whether it is *looked at*, so a room of eight mirrors redrew all eight whenever anything moved.
