It unions in the panes visible only *inside* another surface, because gating on the main camera alone freezes a mirror seen in a mirror. **`EDGE_ON_MARGIN` is a mirror's fix and was being applied to holes** - a portal drew nothing within 0.3 studs of its own plane, which is exactly where somebody walking through spends the crossing; a linked portal has no discontinuity to blank, because the frame the viewer's side flips is the frame the viewer is carried through the pane. **A landing clearance**, so nothing comes to rest on a plane it just crossed and gets sent back by a tick of jitter - and pointedly *only* the landing: the demo also offsets the plane the test is made against, which is hysteresis at 500 Hz and, at a quarter of a stud per tick, a band a body walks through without ever crossing. **And scale-carrying portals**, which is the one this document had been overselling: `SeamMapping` returned a `CFrame`, so a mismatched pair rendered a source-sized window onto a full-sized room and a body walked out the size it went in. It is a `scene::SeamTransform` now - the rigid map, the source pane's centre and the ratio of the two panes - with four *named* applications rather than one multiply, because a position, a velocity, a unit direction and a placement go through a hole differently and mixing two of them up is a portal that works and leads somewhere slightly wrong. A crossing resizes the crate's box and collider, the humanoid's height, radius, walk speed, jump speed and ground tolerance, and every limb's box and rest offset; mass follows from the box. Gravity does not scale, deliberately: there is one world and a small thing in it should fall the way a small thing falls. `scene/SurfaceCameras.hpp`, `graph/Cull.hpp`, `scene::SurfaceMapping`
- [x] **the portals, made usable** - a second pass over them after running the demo, and the reports were all one file deep. **Shadows cross a hole**: a ghost forced `CastShadow` off on the grounds that a second shadow would follow a body that is not there, which is true of a copy beside its original and false of one the map has taken to another room - a character in a doorway had half a shadow. **A crossing no longer snaps**: `CapturePreviousTransforms` runs at the top of a tick and `CrossPortals` in `PostSimulation`, so a teleported body was *interpolated across its own teleport* and drawn once or twice in the hundred studs between the panes; `PreviousTransform` goes through the same map now. **A body goes through the nearest hole rather than the first gathered** - the camera arm already took the nearest, and archetype order decided which one the body took, so on the frames they disagreed the eye and the body ended up in two rooms; one `NearestCrossing` serves both. **A viewpoint is never left standing in a pane**, which had no half-space to clip and no bounded fit and came out as a vertical smear. **Nothing larger than a hole is drawn through it**: every straddle check widens by the body's reach, so a floor fifty studs across was "inside" every rectangle in the building and the far room's floor was laid over the near one. **And one far-side copy instead of two** - `AppendPortalClones` and `AppendPortalGhosts` both ran on the client path, and the ghost pass then ghosted the clones. `scene::ClearOfPanes`, `scene::SeamStraddled`
- [x] **`SurfaceCamera.FPS`, and the surface pass stopped redrawing what nobody looks at** - a surface is a whole scene render and there was no reason it should keep the screen's rate. Three independent reasons to skip and a pass has to clear all three: the content signature says the image has not changed, `graph::VisibleSurfaces` says nothing can see the pane, and the rate says it drew recently enough. The visibility half unions in the panes visible only *inside* another surface, because gating on the main camera alone freezes a mirror seen in a mirror. `FPS` is 120 by default and zero is uncapped; the never-drawn case ignores the cap, or a pane walked up to shows its own tint before the picture arrives
- [x] **a portal is sharp up close** - it was not the texture being too small. The fit covers the whole pane, and against the glass almost all of the pane is off screen: a pane subtends nearly half a turn from a point on its own surface and a screen subtends seventy degrees, so nearly every texel went outside the frame. The fit is intersected with the viewer's own frustum carried through the same map, which is exact rather than a heuristic because the eye and the pane were mapped together. The clamp has to degrade *continuously* - a guard that switched it off when a frustum corner swung behind the camera moved the fit half a radian between two frames - and the coverage invariant had to change from "every corner of the pane is in the image" to "every part of the pane the viewer can see is in the image", which is what `opaque.frag` actually needs
- [x] **`D00112` closed: the portal chain resolves inside the frame** - the last row of `NON-EUCLIDEAN.md`, estimated large on the assumption that it meant a recursive pass with its own budget. It is a loop around the pass that already exists: bounce zero draws every surface sampling last frame's neighbours, the flip at the top of bounce one makes bounce zero's output the read side, and after `n` bounces a chain `n` deep is resolved deepest-first. The ping-pong pair each slot already carried is what makes it safe - a bounce writes one texture and reads the other. `Renderer::SetSurfaceBounces`, two by default rather than CodeParade's four because their portals are the whole scene and ours share a budget; one surface takes one bounce whatever the setting says, because nothing samples itself

### v0.15

- [x] rendering, physics and replication are unified under `mono.engine` - audited, and the verdict is that they already were. Three real faults found and fixed rather than a restructure: `mono.client` called `physics::RegisterCharacterSystems` while never naming `Engine::physics`, so the edge arrived transitively through `examples → script → physics` and nothing declared it; `client::CollectInstances` and `client::CollectReplicated` each wrote their own fourteen-member `DrawInstance` aggregate and had already drifted on which components they treat as optional, now both through `scene::MakeDrawInstance`; and `unified::Harness::DrawnPositionOf` re-derived a draw-list ordinal without the `Visible` skip its collector makes, so one hidden part anywhere ahead of the subject reported a different entity's position as a plain number
- [x] everything is components and not OOP under the hood - audited across every `mono.X`, and the pattern that keeps it that way is worth naming: a module needing a large private working set puts it in the ECS as a named `Store` resource and guards the storage with a `friend` struct in `src/` (`physics::PhysicsWorld`, `effects::ParticleSystem`, `replication::SnapshotBuffer`, the three `scene` catalogues). The one candidate raised - `Editor::ContentMeshFacts` - was examined and is **not** a violation: nothing reads that map but the editor, `script::ContentService` reads the per-world `scene::MeshCatalogue` the editor writes, and the map is what seeds a world opened after a mesh arrived. `input::Translator::Current` is a second `scene::InputState` by necessity, because there is one keyboard and N worlds
- [x] control over whether an explorer/properties edit is client-sided or server-sided - no new routing was needed, because a `Play` run already puts each client in a world of its own (`PlayLink::ReplicaWorld`), so the question is answered by which world the explorer is pointed at, for every client at once. What was missing was the studio saying so: `EditAuthority` and `Editor::AuthorityOf`, a `(client view)` tag on the explorer's world row, and a warning-coloured `[client - local only]` badge in properties whose tooltip says the next delta overwrites the edit
- [x] fix grid projection in run/play mode - the grid was gated on `RunMode::Edit`, deliberately. The argument for hiding it holds for a client replica, which is the one picture in the editor that is exactly what a player sees, and does not hold for an authored scene that happens to be running: `ShowGrid` is already the one-click answer for an author who wants the clean picture, so the mode does not need to decide it for them
- [x] gif rendering support (and cdn) - the decoder, the flipbook sheet, the resize path, both shaders and the studio painter had all existed since the flipbook work. `gif` was a source form in `IsRuntimeReadable` with no row in `assets::AssetKind`'s extension table, so `KindOfName` answered `Unknown`, `cdn::Publisher` recorded that kind into the manifest, and `client::Client` - which routes on kind - never handed the bytes to the texture path. Pinned by a test over the source forms rather than one more spot-check row, because that is the shape that catches `svg` next
- [x] `D00111` closed: `GET /catalogue` on `cdn::Service`, cursor-paged, name last on each line since it is the only unbounded field. Gated on the **existing** ingest key and the existing header rather than a second secret, off by default behind `cdn --list-contents`, which refuses to start without `--ingest-key`. Disabled answers `404` rather than `403`, so a switched-off origin and an older build are one outcome and the panel says so instead of claiming to know. `studio::OriginLister` fills the HTTP tab and carries a distinct sentence for each of not-asked, no-key-here, unreachable, not-offered, refused and unreadable - the deferred entry's rule was that the panel must never guess, and an empty list is a guess
- [x] asset names are not secret, decided. Closing `D00111` turned up that `GET /manifest` is already open and already enumerates, so anyone reaching an origin reads every name out of the signed manifest - the `/catalogue` key gate buys *convenient* enumeration being off and nothing more. `cdn/Service.hpp` argued the opposite in a comment, on a premise that was false here, and now says plainly that it is a convenience rather than a boundary - and that if names are ever meant to be secret, `/manifest` is the route that decides it and the two must not disagree. **Original text:** decide whether asset names are meant to be secret at all.
- [x] `D00110` closed, and built in the order the entry demanded: the consumer first, the files last. `ShaderScript` is now a real class with a `Source` property, `Material.Shader` selects one by name, `ResolveMaterials` carries it onto the part beside the texture maps, `render::ShaderLibrary` resolves a name to a `ShaderScript` in the world, else a staged built-in, else a diagnostic, and `Renderer` grew `AddShader`/`DropShader` with per-name opaque and transparent pipeline variants bound per run in `DrawSlots`. Only then did `unlit.frag` and `toon.frag` arrive, and they are reachable by name rather than being files nothing loads
- [x] the headless capture is now comparable with itself. `Options::FixedAnimationStep`, set for a headless capture only, replaces the measured frame delta so frame N lands on the same animation phase every run. Two captures of one unchanged world went from **12.4% of their bytes differing to 0.21%**, and `examples/Assets.luau` was counted off a frame index rather than `tick()` for the same reason
- [x] **the build was compiling one object in seven with no header dependencies at all, and it corrupts binaries silently.** Found by chasing a segfault: a change to `studio/Editor.hpp` rebuilt some translation units and not others, so the binary held two different layouts of `Editor` - `Settings.ControlPort` read as zero instead of minus one, walked past the guard that keeps the control socket shut, and died in `control::Server::Start` on a pimpl that was not there. **Nothing in 261 suites saw it and a full `cmake --build` did not fix it**, because ninja believed everything was up to date. It looked like three studio files; auditing the whole tree found **57 of 400** - all of `nodeview`, all of `graph`'s pipeline sources and tests, several `render` files, every benchmark object. The pattern is files added since the deps log was last rebuilt. Fixed by deleting every object with no dependency record and rebuilding: **0 of 1287 now**, and `studio/Editor.hpp` is recorded against `Control.cpp` where it was absent
- [x] and it cannot recur unnoticed: `just deps-check` walks every first-party object against the headers ninja recorded for it and fails naming each one that tracks none. It sits beside `test-architecture` and `bindings-check` for the reason those exist - rule 6 says a constraint the build does not check is documentation, and this was a constraint the build was silently breaking. It was red on the real defect (57 of 400) before it was green (0 of 1287), which is the only evidence that a check works
- [x] `--capture-world NAME` photographs the world it names. **The mechanism was a one-frame ordering, not a wrong lookup**, and everything obvious was right: `Active` was set correctly, `ViewportWorld` compared equal, and the copy read the drawing viewport's own slot. What was wrong is *when* the request is consumed - `Editor::Present` sets `CapturePath` after a viewport's `Render` returns, and the **next** `Render` consumes it, which with panels taking turns is a different panel showing a different world. `RequestSceneCapture` now carries the wanted slot and the renderer leaves the request pending until that panel's turn. Verified by eye: the capture went from the `Mirrors` room to the Assets bays, and the frame changed size because it is a different panel
- [x] a selected shader is confirmed to draw. Once `--capture-world` photographed the right panel, the first attempt still failed - the probe was on the Assets world's mesh bay, which sits off the left edge under the editor's default camera, so the object under test was not in the frame being measured. Moved onto the audio marker, which is visibly in shot: **0.69% and 0.64% against a 0.21% and 0.18% floor**, three times the noise across both pairs, and the crop shows it plainly - smooth falloff and a bright top face become flat quantised bands. `ShaderScript` works from a `Material.Shader` name through `ShaderLibrary` to a bound pipeline variant
- [x] the mirror bug: the half that was a defect is fixed. `graph::VisibleSurfaces` followed **one** level of surface-seen-in-surface while the pass drew `SurfaceBounces` of them, so a pane leaving the viewer's frustum dropped everything it had been revealing past the single level the sweep followed - which is why the symptom was a camera angle rather than a scene, and why the geometry looked untouched. The grant sweep now runs once per bounce with a fresh snapshot each round, so order-independence survives, and stops at a fixpoint. The renderer passes the same expression the pass itself uses, with a comment on both ends saying they must not drift. Measured at **0.64% and 0.80% of the frame against a 0.21% noise floor**, across two independent run pairs
- [x] the mirror bug: the half that was structural. **Mirrors compose now, because the surface pass below the first level is a recursion rather than a repeat.** The old shape could not be right at any depth: `AimSurfaceCameras` places every surface camera by reflecting the world's *active* camera, so a pane drawn inside another pane's picture was placed and sampled from the eye - several studs and a reflection from where it was actually being looked at. The coordinate left the texture's 0..1 rectangle and `opaque.frag` fell back to the plain lit pane, which is the flat slab, and which reads as culling rather than as a projection fault. Running the pass again could never touch it: iterating refreshes textures and never moves a camera
  - **The rule is one function now.** `scene::ReflectCamera(pane, viewer)` is where a mirror's camera comes from - pure, no `ecs::Store`, headless - and `AimSurfaceCameras` calls it for the top level, so a chain cannot drift from the screen by a sign. `scene::SurfacePane` and `GatherSurfacePanes` are `PortalSeam`'s and `GatherPortalSeams`' twins; `render::SurfaceView` carries the rectangle so the pass can place a camera for a viewer that is not the eye. `MAX_SURFACE_DEPTH` is a new ceiling, because a recursion costs `panes × (panes - 1) ^ (levels - 1)` where an iteration cost `panes × levels`
  - **The defect is proved as arithmetic before any device sees it.** Two panes facing each other, eye between them: through A the camera lands at `z = -4`; through B *from the eye* at `z = 16`; through B *from A's camera* at `z = 24`. Eight studs apart, and eight is what a reflection predicts of the gap. The renderer had the middle answer available to it and used it. `engine.scene.surfacecameras` asserts all three, plus that the aim pass's own answer is bitwise the eye's - 55 cases, 1629 assertions
  - **Measured on `examples/MirrorCorridor.luau`**, two panes facing across 26 studs, which counts depth by eye: each level adds a visible row of markers. In the deep box the largest single-colour run - the unresolved slab - is **49.1% of it at one level and 27.2% at two**, where at two the biggest run is the sky rather than a pane; distinct colours climb **258 → 432 → 560** across one, two and three levels. Its first camera stood *outside* the panes' width, where a pane's fitted frustum leans away from the axis and genuinely does not contain the other pane, so the corridor telescoped one way and looked like a recursion that stops. That is recorded in the scene
  - `examples/MirrorDepth.luau` is the angled case and now shows a picture in both inner panes where it showed two flat slabs. One of them is dark, and that is the honest answer: it faces sky and empty floor, and the floor edge and the cyan strip are visible inside it
  - Four hypotheses had been eliminated by measurement before this, and all four were correct eliminations. **The frustum fit**: a sweep over yaw and pitch, both signs, 0 to 80 degrees, passes 360 assertions. **The bounce count**: `--surface-bounces 5` against 2 was byte-for-byte identical - which is the defect stating itself, since more iterations of the wrong camera are the wrong camera. **Visibility and the refresh gate**: every surface reported `visible=1 ready=1 due=1`. **`cycle` on the ping-pong target**: switching it off changed zero bytes
- [x] `Renderer::SetSurfaceBounces` has a caller at last. It shipped with a comment calling the knob public - "a scene built around a corridor of holes can ask for more" - and **nothing anywhere set it**, so every scene resolved exactly two levels of surface-seen-in-surface whatever it was built from. `studio --surface-bounces N` is the first caller it has ever had. Measured on `Mirrors`: two bounces against four differ by **2.17% of the frame against a 0.21% noise floor**, ten times the floor, so the depth limit was visibly costing that scene a layer
- [x] the depth is the world's, and where the world says nothing the frame measures it. **The knob had the wrong grain twice over** - per process, and a number at all. `workspace.SurfaceBounces` is the per-world half: a computed property over a `scene::SurfaceBounces` resource, which is `workspace.CurrentCamera`'s arrangement exactly, so a script authors it, it round-trips through the world document, and there is one place the number lives. Zero is the default and means *measure it*: each viewport keeps what the frame it just drew reached - the deepest level that actually rendered a pane, and whether that level still had one in view it was not allowed to descend into - and `scene::NextSurfaceBounces` answers `resolved + (blocked ? 1 : 0)`, clamped to `MAX_SURFACE_DEPTH`. A corridor of facing panes therefore deepens itself one level a frame to the ceiling and a room with one mirror in it costs one level, with nobody having typed anything. `--surface-bounces` survives as an override rather than as a setting, which is what it was actually being used for
  - **The measurement asks whether the next level would *render*, not whether a pane is in the frustum**, and that distinction is the whole of why it cannot oscillate. A pane seen edge-on is visible and draws nothing, so the looser question asks for a level that comes back empty, measures one shallower for it, and asks again - two values alternating for ever with nobody moving. Asking the same question the descent itself asks makes `resolved` grow to meet it and stop
  - **The depth had to become an input to the surface signature**, which is the half that was found by running the thing rather than by reading it. A surface whose signature has not moved is deliberately not redrawn, so on a still scene the measurement said "one deeper", nothing else in the frame changed, no surface refreshed, the deeper level was never drawn, and the next frame measured the same shallow answer. `MirrorCorridor.luau` is static on purpose and sat at one level for ever. A level added or taken away changes what is inside every other pane's picture, so it belongs in that signature beside the draw list
  - **A view with no pane rectangle keeps the constant two**, deliberately: a cross-world pane and a camera parented to the world both resolve their chain by iterating rather than descending, so there is nothing about them to measure. That path costs `panes × levels` and is unchanged
  - Measured on `examples/MirrorCorridor.luau`: automatic reaches **792 triangles in 23 draw calls at the busiest frame**, which is byte-for-byte what `--surface-bounces 3` draws, against 552/17 at two and 312/11 at one - and it gets there unaided within three frames. That scene now states no depth and is the demonstration of the automatic half; `examples/MirrorDepth.luau` sets `workspace.SurfaceBounces = 3` and is the demonstration of the authored one, for the reason a measurement scene should state a depth - it draws every frame at the depth it settles on anyway (1164 triangles in 25 draw calls, against 732/17 at one level), so a capture is of the scene rather than of the ramp. `engine.scene.surfacecameras` is 58 cases and 1670 assertions, three of them new: the arithmetic including the ceiling, a model of the pass's own descent built out of `ReflectCamera` - one pane settles at one, two facing panes climb 1 → 2 → 3 and pin - and the property, which refuses a negative and lets an over-ambitious number through for the renderer to clamp
  - Rule 5 is untouched and checked: nothing on this path reaches an `ecs::Store`, the measurement lives in the renderer's per-viewport bank, and `just determinism` and `just replay-check` are byte-identical
- [x] `engine.core.framegraph`'s flaky timing case is fixed, and the fix went the harder way. Its ratio-against-wall-clock guard assumed the clock and the profiler stretch *together*, which holds only if the stretch is uniform - a scheduler preempts one span, so with four repeats a single stalled burn put the maximum past sixty percent of the loop and the case failed under this repository's own parallel sweep while the code was right. Sixteen repeats instead of four, and a third instead of six tenths: the gap the assertion measures gets **wider**, so the discrimination is stronger and the flake is gone. Raising the bound would have done the opposite
- [x] `render/AGENTS.md` no longer carries the two claims `ShaderScript` made stale. It now names `ShaderLibrary` as `ShaderCompiler`'s consumer - the compiler had existed for a case nothing could reach - and retires "a shader reaches the GPU only by being named in `CreatePipelines`", since `DrawSlots` binds a per-name variant per run. It also states the trap: a `.frag` added to `Engine::resources` without a `BuiltInShaderNames` row is a shader nothing can select. **Original text:** `render/AGENTS.md` carries two claims this made stale:
- [x] `D00114` closed, and not the way it was framed. The entry's headline case - `FindFirstChild` - is **still the union**, deliberately: resolving it to its receiver was the cheap option filed here, and it is simply wrong, because a child of a `Model` is not a `Model`. What was built instead is narrowing wherever the buffer *writes the class down* (`Instance.new`, `game:GetService`, the `OfClass`/`WhichIsA` pair, `:Clone()` carrying its receiver, and plain aliases, followed recursively), a fix for a real stale-fact bug where a local reassigned from something unreadable kept claiming the class of its first assignment, and - the part that answers the entry's actual warning - **every row now says which kind of list it is in**. A narrowed row's detail reads `bool on Part`; a union row's reads `bool on some class`. The entry's complaint was that an author had no way to tell a correct narrow from an incorrect one; both are labelled now, because a marker on the narrow alone is unreadable to anyone who has not seen the other kind
- [x] `D00115` closed: a character's limbs no longer pay wire for transforms the receiver overwrites. Two of the entry's own assumptions were wrong - it needed no wire-format decision, because a delta already carries only changed rows so an omitted row is indistinguishable from an unchanged one; and it needed no new tag, because `scene.CharacterLimb` already *means* an entity whose frame is derived from its root. `Authority::SuppressWhenTagged` skips a slot's delta rows for tagged entities, the pairing lives on `ReplicatedComponent` so both hosts apply it, and the tag crosses the L12-to-L7 tier gap as a string, which is rule 4
- [x] mip chains - all three halves. `TextureData::Mips` holds levels 1..N *beside* the base rather than concatenated with it, because a dozen call sites check `Pixels` against `Width * Height * BytesPerPixel` and one flat buffer would make every one of them read a third too much while still compiling. Level dimensions are derived by `MipExtent` and never stored: a stored per-level size is the one field of a level an attacker gets to choose. VERSION 3, with v1 and v2 still read. Built by `bake::ResizeImage` through a new `NodeKind::Mipmap`, which `assetc` places immediately before the write.
- [x] **and the sampler was the actual bug.** `mipmap_mode` has said LINEAR since v0.8, but the zero-initialised `max_lod` clamped every fetch to level 0 - so a chain could have been built, serialised and uploaded and still never sampled, with three modules each looking correct on their own. `min_lod = 0`, `max_lod = 32`
- [x] **a flipbook's chain stops before its frames bleed**, and that decision mattered more than the code. Halving a grid is safe only while every destination pixel falls inside one cell, so the chain ends at the last exact halving of the cell size. Padding a gutter was rejected because it changes what a flipbook *is* - the `FlipbookSide` division lives in `render::FlipbookCellAt` and in every UV that samples a sheet - and refusing the texture outright was rejected because it throws away an image that was fine without a chain. Bleeding is the failure worth refusing precisely because it is invisible in the file and reads as the cell arithmetic being wrong
- [x] built-in textures have mip chains, and the tier argument that blocked them was the bug rather than the reason. `assets::ResizeImage`, `MipChainLevels` and `BuildMipChain` are pure arithmetic over `assets::TextureData` and reference nothing from `bake` - they sat at L9 only because `bake` happened to be the first caller, while `MipExtent` and `MipLevelCount` were already at L8. So the filter moved **down** to join them and `bake`'s copies were deleted rather than wrapped, which is the rule about two ways to do one job. `MakeChecker`, `render::DefaultTexture` and `render::MissingTexture` now build their own chains and upload seven levels through the path `TextureTable::Upload` already had. No new link edge, so `expected_graph.json` is untouched
- [x] the mip sampler and the multi-level upload are verified **on a device**, and by measurement rather than by a screenshot. `--verbose` only proves nothing complained, which cannot tell a sampled level from one clamped away - `max_lod` sat at zero for four versions while `mipmap_mode` said LINEAR, and no log line ever mentioned it.
  - `examples/MipProbe.luau` is the fixture: 810 small tiles each wearing the whole checker, camera down the grid, so the far tiles are a few pixels and the sheet is minified two orders of magnitude. The scene is static, so two captures of one build are **byte-identical** - a zero noise floor, and every digit of a difference is the change.
  - The metric is mean absolute difference between neighbouring pixels in the minified band, against the same in a near band as a control. **far 9.841 to 2.943, a 70% drop. near 3.114 to 3.103, unmoved.** A blur or a broken texture fetch would have smoothed both; only a chain smooths the minified half alone. The crops show it as moiré becoming flat.
  - The first version of this probe measured nothing: a `MeshPart` maps its texture 0..1 across a face, so one part stretched 900 units wears a single checker with no tiling and nothing to minify. Both builds would have scored near zero and it would have read as "no change". Caught by looking at the picture instead of the number
- [x] auto complete as you type. The machinery already fired on every keystroke; what was wrong was that the popup waited for **two** characters before opening, and that the highlighted row was *clamped* across a re-query rather than reset - so an author who arrowed down and kept typing was left highlighting the sixth entry of a different list, and Enter inserted it with nothing on screen looking wrong. One character now, and the highlight returns to the top whenever the list itself changed
- [x] svg rendering support (and cdn) - a hand-written rasteriser in `bake`, no new vendor dependency, which is the house style every other decoder here follows. **Dispatched by name and not by a text sniff**: `ImageFormatOfName` is consulted only after `ImageFormatOfBytes` returns `Unknown`, because a `<svg`/`<?xml` prefix is a claim over all text and would capture the next XML-shaped format the module learns - and a `.svg` holding a PNG still decodes as a PNG. **The raster size is a node parameter**, `NodeKind::Rasterize` appended after `Write` since ordinals are on-wire, because an SVG has no intrinsic pixel size and a post-hoc `Resize` would be rasterising twice. `assetc` applies `--max-texture` by re-rasterising at the cap. An honest subset is drawn and everything else is **refused by name** - gradients, filters, text, masks, `use`, dashes, arcs and quadratics among them - rather than silently mis-rendered
- [x] and it turned up a denial of service that was not on anyone's list: a hostile 4000-rect file took **over two minutes** to bake. Each fill is now charged its bounding box plus edge crossings *before* it runs, so the same file refuses in 0.78 seconds while a legitimate 900-stroked-circle illustration bakes in 0.28
- [x] the "Meshes" demo is now an "Assets" demo world - `examples/Assets.luau`, six labelled bays rather than a grid, because every bay is a *different* path and the useful frame says which pipeline broke rather than only that one did. Mesh through `MeshTable`, a still sheet and an animated one through `TextureTable` side by side so the pair is the comparison, the same bytes again through `InterfacePass` on a `SurfaceGui`, a bobbing marker for audio because a still marker is a sound that did not load, and a bay that says "no decoder" for video rather than showing a stand-in - `AssetKind::Video` is a routing label with nothing behind it, and a bay pretending otherwise would be the one lie in the scene. `ContentService:GetFlipbook` is what sorts a baked GIF from a baked PNG, since both are one `.atex` and the difference is the triple inside. `MeshGrid.luau` stays in `examples/` for the narrow question.
- [x] the Assets world's frame is confirmed by eye - grey pedestals, the green audio marker, the dark video pane. It could not be seen until `--capture-world` was fixed, because every capture naming it photographed `Mirrors` instead. **Original text:** visually confirm the Assets world's play-mode frame.
- [x] portal and surfacecamera rendering share what they should and keep their own entries. The two passes had hand-rolled the same four blocks of device setup side by side, so every bug in one was available to the other: opening a scene pass - target infos, `BeginGPURenderPass`, the light and beam pushes, the pipeline and buffer binds - the world-minus-panes draw, the blended tail minus the panes in it, and the shadow texture/sampler pair derived twice under two names. All four are one lambda each now, used by the surface pass, the mirror recursion and the portal recursion alike. **Proved byte-identical**: capture, revert `Renderer.cpp` alone, rebuild, capture, `cmp` - `MirrorDepth`, `MirrorCorridor` and `PortalProbe` all match, and those three are the only example scenes deterministic enough across two runs of one build to support the claim
  - **The entries stay three, and what separates them is no longer the recursion.** Both a hole and a mirror derive each level's camera from the level above; what differs is the map - a mirror reflects its viewer through one plane, a hole carries it through `destination · half-turn · source⁻¹` onto a second pane that may be turned, moved and resized anywhere - and the lookup that follows from it. A hole's sub-render is the screen's own frustum skewed, so its pane reads the texel it is standing on; a mirror's is fitted to its own rectangle, so its pane reads by projecting its world position. Neither lookup is expressible in the other's target, which is why merging the two entries would mean one of them carrying the other's fields empty. `PortalView`'s header comment, `Renderer.hpp`'s `SurfaceView` and the two pass headers all said the old reason - that a surface camera cannot compose - and all three now say this one
- [x] common services with scripting binds. `CollectionService` (tag-first: `GetTagged` is the question nothing could answer), `HttpService` (`JSONEncode`/`JSONDecode`/`GenerateGUID`/`UrlEncode`, and **no `RequestAsync`** - what a game script may talk to is a decision nobody has taken, and an absent method refuses at the call site), `TweenService` + `Debris` (both stepping the fixed tick delta at the barrier, both deterministic under `just replay-check`), `SoundService`, `CrossWorldService`. Every one is a row in `ServiceCatalogue.cpp`
- [x] **the reason there was drift to fix: which services exist was two lists.** Luau installed nine surface services and JavaScript five, four were reachable from one language and not the other, and nothing in the build said so - the TypeScript declarations claimed two of the four anyway. `ServiceCatalogue.hpp` is the language-neutral contract (name, `ServiceAvailability`, `ServiceLanguages` mask); `ServiceCatalogue.cpp` is the one file that has met both VMs and names every installer, which is also what stops a static archive dropping one. Self-registration was refused for that exact reason - `scene::RegisterSceneComponents` is explicit for the same one. Both runtimes walk the table, and both `GetService` refusals consult it: `ContentService is not bound for JavaScript in this engine` where it used to say the engine had never heard of it
  - Getting one row per service meant splitting installers that built several: `OpenServices` built four and `OpenInputServices` two, and on the JavaScript side five services were **anonymous blocks inside install functions** - a list written as control flow, which nothing outside could enumerate. That is how the two lists came to differ
  - Moving `RunService` into one installer found it was being assembled across two files: `OpenJsBindings` built a `Heartbeat` with a hand-made `Connect` that `JsSurface.cpp` then **overwrote** before any script could reach it. Dead the whole time
  - `engine.script.servicecatalogue` pins the table against live VMs of both languages, including a deliberate landmine: the JavaScript half asserts it found at least one unbound service, so the day that drift closes the case fails and is deleted rather than passing vacuously
- [x] extra functions like `PlayerGui:...`. `Player.PlayerGui` is a computed property - the container has existed since v0.8, `AddPlayer` makes one beside every player because `gui::Layout` only draws a `ScreenGui` from `StarterGui` or a `PlayerGui`, and **nothing could name it**. `Players:GetPlayerFromCharacter` and `Player:LoadCharacter` are bound, and `scene::PlayerOf` is `CharacterOf`'s inverse - one component read, because `Character::Owner` already held the answer
  - **A property was free in both languages and a method was not**, which is the distinction the next item closes: `PlayerGui` is a `PropertyDescriptor`, so it arrived in Luau, in JavaScript and in the properties panel with nothing else changing
  - `GetPlayers`/`PlayerAdded`/`PlayerRemoving` are **not** redeclared on `Players`. Every instance method lives on one shared table, so they are declared on `Instance` and inherited; spelling them again would be a second declaration of one member. What an author was missing is not the type but knowing the member exists and which instance to call it on, so the generator emits a sentence above the class
- [x] user input system with scripting binds, and finishing it turned up two defects rather than a gap. **`InputChanged` had never fired** - connectable and silent since v0.10. **Mouse buttons produced no signal at all**, though `InputState::WasButtonPressed` had existed since v0.10 with nothing asking it. And `PumpInput` passed a bare `Enum.KeyCode` where `engine.d.luau` declared it passed **nothing**: three answers to one question, none of them Roblox's. `InputObject` exists now (`KeyCode`, `UserInputType`, `UserInputState`, `Position`, `Delta`) and all five signals plus the bound-action handler carry one. `WindowFocused`/`WindowFocusReleased` needed a flag taken out of `InputState::Reserved`, so `sizeof` stayed 64 and **no save format moved**
- [x] code cleanup: systems under services, internal and exposed. Five hand-rolled copies of the same ten lines of `lua_newtable`/push-closure/`lua_setglobal` became one `ServiceSurface` - and it had to carry a thing that looked like style and was not: **`UserInputService` is a userdata rather than a table**, because `luaL_sandbox` enables `safeenv` and a property read off a constant global table compiles to a `GETIMPORT` resolved once and cached, so a live value reads as a frozen one. A contract only seven of nine services could use would not have been one
  - **And methods stopped being bound twice.** `ScriptCall.hpp` is the neutral method layer: a method is written once and each VM adapts. `ScriptValue` could not be its currency - `ValueTag` has no instance and `CodecStatus::Unsupported` documents that as rule 3, because a `ScriptValue` crosses a *world* and a method call crosses nothing. Luau's instance table had thirty entries and JavaScript's twenty-one; all nine missing migrated, including `GetAttributeChangedSignal`, since `PushSignal` and `MakeJsSignal` hand back the same `SignalTable` entry and only the wrapper differs. `Instances.cpp` lost 338 lines
  - Four divergences the parity tests caught, three of them older than the layer: Luau stored `SetAttribute("n", "5")` as a **number** where JavaScript stored a string, because `lua_isnumber` is true for a numeric string; `AddTag({})` would have tagged a part `"[object Object]"` in JavaScript where Luau raises; **JavaScript had no way to compare two instances at all** - `===` is identity and `MakeJsInstance` builds a fresh object per call, while `Vector3`, `Color3`, `Vector2`, `Signal` and `EnumItem` all carry `Equals`, so `Instance:Equals` is a neutral method now; and one of mine, `ReturnInstance` using the raw pusher so a null entity came back as a handle to nothing and `if player then` was true for a player who does not exist
- [x] proper channels for cross-world communication. The gap was in `world` and not in the bindings: `Publish` is a topic fan-out with no destination and `Teleport` names a world but carries a **person**, so a game saying one thing to one world had to broadcast it to everybody or send a player carrying it. `BusKind::Channel` is **appended** beside `Teleport` - these are wire ordinals - and is deliberately not a flag on it: the two look identical on the wire and mean entirely different things to a receiver, and `script::AdmitTeleports` runs on every world whether or not it has scripts, so a channel message arriving as a teleport would have it building a `Player` out of a chat line. A test sends both to one world in one tick and the receiver tells them apart
  - **No `GetWorlds`, stated rather than faked.** A world's storage cannot see its siblings - the directory belongs to the barrier's router - so answering it would mean stamping a world list into every store every tick. `Send` answering false for a world that is not running is the honest half of the same question, and it is the half `Publish` cannot answer at all: a publish with no subscribers is a quiet afternoon and indistinguishable from one nobody wanted
- [x] **Luau and JavaScript are at parity, and closing it took the same move twice.** Seven services were reachable from one language and not the other; every one crosses now. `BreakpointService` is the single exception and states its reason in the row: it arms `lua_callbacks(State)->debugstep` and switches the VM into single-step mode, so a JavaScript debugger is a feature rather than a binding
  - **A service method became neutral, exactly as an instance method had.** `ServiceSurface` carried `lua_CFunction`s, so it could only build a Luau service and every JavaScript one was hand-written - that is mechanism, not effort, and it is why the two lists drifted in the first place. A `ServiceMethod` is a `ScriptMethod` now and `ServiceCatalogue.cpp` installs both languages from one surface. `ServiceSurface::LuauMethods` is the not-yet-migrated span, so a half-migrated service is one description with a visible remainder rather than two descriptions
  - **A live property needed the opposite shape in each language, which is what was left.** Luau needs a userdata and a catch-all `__index`, because `safeenv` compiles a property read off a constant global *table* into a `GETIMPORT` resolved once and cached - a live value reads as frozen. JavaScript has native accessors and none of that problem, but registers one *per name*, which a catch-all cannot supply. So `ServiceSurface` carries a `ServiceProperty` **list**: Luau walks it from one generic metamethod and JavaScript defines one accessor per row. That also replaced `InputServiceIndex`'s chain of string comparisons
  - **A binding is not a pump, and two services proved it.** JavaScript had no input pump at all, so a JS `BindAction` would have taken a handler and forgotten it - bound, silent, forever. `PumpJsDeliveries` never handled `BusKind::Channel`, so `CrossWorldService.MessageReceived` could be connected and never fire. Both built, at the barrier positions their Luau twins use
  - Divergences the parity cases exposed, none of them the thing being ported: `AsNumber` took a numeric string in Luau and refused one in JavaScript, so `BindActionAtPriority(..., "5", ...)` bound at five in one language and threw in the other; an unknown-member **write** was silently kept forever as a new property in JavaScript where Luau raises; `MouseDeltaSensitivity` did not round-trip, because `Sensitivity / 0.0035` promoted a float and divided by an inexact literal, so a fresh world answered `1.000000030866691`; and a null-data `string_view` traps `lua_pushlstring` **even at length zero**, which an invalid `core::Name::Text()` is
  - Two kept as genuine language, each with a test asserting the *difference* rather than papering it: `GenerateGUID(0)` differs because `0` is truthy in Lua and falsy in JavaScript, and a strict reader would refuse the `if x then` shape every Lua author writes; and an unknown-member **read** raises in Luau and answers `undefined` in JavaScript, because intercepting it needs a `Proxy`, which `JsBindings.cpp` excludes deliberately
  - Two per-method gaps inside services that otherwise cross, declared rather than discovered: `TeleportService::GetTeleportData` and `ContextActionService::GetBoundActionInfo`. The second answers a record holding `Enum.KeyCode` members and `ScriptValue` has no tag for an `EnumItem` - **and must not gain one, because it is a wire format**
  - **`:Once` on an input signal never retired, and it fired for ever.** `SignalTable::Fire` deliberately retires nothing itself - only the VM knows how to release a callable, and a disconnect can arrive from inside a fire about a value still on the stack - so every direct caller of `Fire` owes the retirement. `FireSignal` and `FireJsSignal` have always paid it; both input pumps were written later and neither did, so a `UserInputService` handler connected with `:Once` answered every edge for ever - one a script cannot get rid of and did not ask to keep. Fixed in both, and the test takes **two** edges and counts, because one edge and a flag would have passed against the broken code: reverting the fix reads `"2" == "1"` in both languages
  - **The landmine did its job.** `engine.script.servicecatalogue` asserted the JavaScript half found at least one unbound service, so the day that count reached zero the case would fail rather than pass vacuously. Closing five of seven left two, which would still have passed - so it became `checked == 2` **and a named set**, and then failed properly at zero. It is now "every surface service is in both languages", asserting the unbound set is empty and named, so un-binding one fails saying which

- [x] client-only and server-only, which `ServiceComponent::Scope` had described since v0.7 with **nothing reading it** - it round-tripped through save files and showed in the properties panel while `ServerScriptService` and `ServerStorage` went down the wire to every client that joined. `scene::VisibleToClients` and `scene::PlayerOwning` are the rules, hung on `replication::Authority::SetInterest` by the server, because the wire moves components and has no business knowing what a service is. `SetInterest` did not hand its predicate the world where `SetOwnership` does - a predicate looking up its own world from inside a walk of that world - so the signature widened
  - A test caught the first version being too broad: it hid every player's row from every other client, which reads as a lobby that never fills. The `Player` row is public and what is *under* it is private, which is where Roblox draws it and why
  - **`StarterGui` was not a template at all.** `Layout` drew a `ScreenGui` from `StarterGui` *or* from a `PlayerGui`, so every client drew the same instances - a script hiding one player's health bar hid everybody's. `gui::ResetPlayerGui` does Roblox's rule: collectors with `ResetOnSpawn` true are destroyed and re-cloned, ones set false survive with their state, and a survivor of a name is not cloned beside it. That field had been on `gui::Layer` since v0.8 with nothing reading it. `mono.server` links `Engine::gui` for it - that module depends on `core` and `ecs` and nothing else, and `just check-server-is-headless` still reports no graphics stack
- [x] common services implemented through the contract rather than spread out - seven stopped being written twice (`RunService`, `TweenService`, `Debris`, `MessagingService`, `TeleportService`, `MemoryStoreService`, `DataStoreService`), and every `Always` row in `ServiceCatalogue` is now one `ServiceSurface` with the per-language function pointer deleted from the row. `BreakpointService` keeps the only one and has the argument written down. Two members the declarations had been lying about: `TeleportService.GetTeleportData` was declared in `engine.d.ts` since v0.15 and the JavaScript runtime did not have it, and `ContextActionService`'s two reporters were Luau-only and undeclared. That second pair crossed on `BoundActionReport` - the fact is one struct and the record wrapper is per VM, which is how a record holding `Enum.KeyCode` members got across **without** widening `ScriptValue`, which must not gain an enum tag because it is a wire format
  - The parity test now checks every member is reachable *and callable*, from a list written separately from the surfaces with a set-equality guard so a new service cannot quietly skip it. Its first version passed against a deliberately broken `TeleportService`, because `Runtime::Run` reports whether a chunk ran and not what it returned
- [x] user input system with scripting binds - three defects that only showed in a shipped binary. **`Enum.ContextActionResult` was unimplementable**: the handler's return value was thrown away by `lua_pcall(..., 3, 0, 0)` and by a `JS_Call` result freed unread, so `Sink` and `Pass` were words in the documentation. **`gameProcessedEvent` was never passed at all**, so a Roblox-shaped `function(input, gameProcessed)` read nil on every edge; it is decided once per beat from the router's pending events and filtered so it cannot leak onto a key. **A shipped client never routed gui input**: `Client::InterfaceRouter` was constructed and read for `Hovered()`/`Pressed()` and never `Update`d, so no `TextButton` ever lit and no `Activated` ever fired in a game binary - it worked in the editor because studio drives its own router, which is the worst version, invisible to exactly the person authoring the UI
  - New: `GetLastInputType`, `MouseIconEnabled`, `LastInputTypeChanged`. Three bytes taken out of `InputState::Reserved[7]` so `sizeof` did not move, and a `static_assert` pins 56 so the next field is a decision rather than a silent save-format move. Gamepad, touch and `TextBox` focus stay absent with the reason where the members would be. `input::Translate.hpp` was the one public header in its module with no suite and has one now
- [x] proper channels for cross-world communication - a channel is `(destination world, channel name)`, where v0.15 had one unnamed pipe per world pair, so two subsystems talking between the same two worlds read each other's traffic and told it apart by inspecting the payload. **The barrier sorted senders by `Name::Id()`**, which is handed out in interning order: a universe restored from a snapshot interns in file order where the run that wrote it interned in creation order, so the same two envelopes applied in the opposite order. `BusRouter::SortedKeys` had already been given exactly this argument for the snapshot codec and the barrier had not. It is `(From.Text(), Sequence)` now, and the test builds two universes in opposite creation order
  - `Send(world, message)` was **removed** rather than extended: it answered a boolean about its own send budget and threw the bus's verdict away, so `NoSuchWorld` was decided, delivered, matched against nothing and dropped. `OpenChannel`/`CloseChannel`/`SendAsync` replace it and every failure is a status a script reads - `NoSuchWorld`, `NoSuchChannel`, `WorldNotReady`, `Overflow`, the last bounded at 256 per destination per barrier and reset with the fanout so it is backpressure and not a dead channel. A *suspended* world is deliberately not refused, because its inbox is what wakes it. Also: `Buses::Teleports` had no reader anywhere since v0.2, and `AdmitTeleports` was treating a teleport *reply* as an arrival - erasing it from the inbox so nothing could ever await it
  - **A world's channel *table* is bounded now too, closing `D00118`** - `ChannelQueueLimit` bounded the queue between two worlds and nothing bounded what one world could *hold*, so a world opening a distinct channel every tick for an hour left two hundred thousand live entries that every snapshot carried. `UniverseSettings::ChannelsPerWorld` is 256, which is `ChannelQueueLimit`'s number deliberately: past that a world cannot take one delivery on each of its channels in a single barrier, so the next channel buys a name and no more traffic. The refusal is `TooManyChannels` and not `OverBudget`, because a budget is a rate that comes back next tick and this is a total that only the world closing something clears - a world that retried it as a budget would loop for ever. **`Postbox::OpenChannel` hands back a ticket where it used to hand back a boolean**, since the count is the router's and a world cannot answer it from its own store without keeping a second copy of that table: the barrier decides and the verdict comes back as a reply, which is where every other verdict on this bus already arrives. The script member stays synchronous - `OpenChannel` and the `Connect` on its result are one expression, and suspending it would make it a promise in JavaScript rather than a stricter member - so a world at the cap is named in the log and its senders read `NoSuchChannel`. Closing *idle* channels is deliberately not built: a channel that goes away while somebody holds its signal is a failure the opener never asked for
- [x] players, respawning and the gui systems, with `StarterPack` → `StarterGear` on join and `StarterGear` → `Backpack` on spawn - the per-player tree is `PlayerGui`, `PlayerScripts`, `Backpack`, `StarterGear`, and `StarterPack` and `StarterCharacterScripts` did not exist as services at all. Join happens once (`StarterPlayerScripts` → `PlayerScripts`, `StarterPack` → `StarterGear`); spawn happens every life (`StarterCharacterScripts` → the model, `Backpack` emptied and refilled from `StarterGear`, `ResetPlayerGui`). Gear a game grants at run time survives dying because it is added to `StarterGear` and never to `Backpack`
  - **The Workspace-rename landmine is closed.** Every fixture lookup was `FindFirstRoot("Workspace")`, including the one `InstallServices` uses to decide what a world is missing - so renaming the workspace made `WorkspaceOf` answer nothing *and* made the next install mint a second `Workspace` beside the one holding the scene. Lookups are by class now, and the suite renames three services and requires all three still resolve with no duplicates
  - **`CharacterRemoving` cannot be queued**, and the first version queued it beside `CharacterAdded` for symmetry: a handler read `Name is not a valid member of this instance`, because by the time a queued signal drains the model is gone. It rides `Store::OnDescendantRemoving` while the model is whole and `RemoveCharacter` stops, destroys, then releases. The asymmetry is written into `scene/AGENTS.md` so nobody tidies it back
  - Scoping proven over a real socket: each client holds four containers of its own and **zero** of the other's, while both `Player` rows stay public
- [x] a character dies at zero health instead of by being destroyed, closing `D00121` - `scene::Humanoid` gained a `Health` and a `MaxHealth`, and `UpdateRespawns` now measures `Player.RespawnTime` from the tick the humanoid reached zero rather than from the tick a player was first seen with no model. That was Roblox's delay hung off the wrong event: here the body stays where it fell for the whole delay and is then removed and replaced, which is what `LoadCharacter` has done since v0.14 without anything new destroying anything. A destroyed model still schedules the same deadline, because a teleport and a host dropping a client remove a character without setting anything to zero first
  - **Who may write it is the authority, and it is one rule with two doors rather than a new mechanism.** `ecs::Store::SetProperty` has refused every property write in a replica since v0.3, so `Humanoid.Health` is an ordinary writable property and a client script asking for more is refused by name - a `Writable = false` would have stopped the *server* script that wants to kill somebody, which is the ordinary way a game does it. `scene::TakeDamage` makes the same refusal on `Store::AdoptOnly` for the C++ door, because a rule that only covered scripts is one a hit-resolution loop walks straight past. Proved over a real socket: the shooter's replica is told no by name and its victim's health is still the server's
  - **The reopen trigger fired in the same commit**, which is why the entry is closed rather than extended: it was "anything in the engine that damages a character", and `Server::ApplyInputs` was the hit test with no consequence it named. That path subtracts a constant now - never a roll, for `FindSpawn`'s reason about picking a pad in tree order, since a random draw inside a tick is a recording that does not replay
  - `MaxHealth` has a reader inside the engine rather than only being the denominator of somebody's bar: `Health` clamps against it and lowering it pulls the health down. That is the bar `D00119` set, and it is why both are written conversions rather than `ClampedProperty` - each bound is the other field, and `ClampedProperty` bakes its bounds in as template arguments so its setter stays captureless
  - **No `Humanoid.Died` and no `ForceField`, both decided rather than skipped.** `scene` is L7 and a signal is L9, and `humanoid:GetPropertyChangedSignal("Health")` already delivers the transition once - `TakeDamage` takes nothing from a humanoid already dead, so two hits in one tick are one death with no flag on the row. A forcefield is one branch inside that same door and a whole spawn-protection feature around it
- [x] `PlayerGui` and the rest of the instance functions - the neutral migration is finished and **no per-VM instance method table is left in either language**. All twenty remaining methods are one `ScriptMethod` row each, including the three named as not-straight-lifts: `Destroy`/`ClearAllChildren` through `ScriptCall::Forget` (the walk is shared now, so a grandchild's connections cannot outlive its row in one language and not the other), `SetNetworkOwner` (both languages refuse `SetNetworkOwner(5)`), and `GetPropertyChangedSignal` - which exposed a live drift, because JavaScript compared the descriptor name and **ignored `Scriptable`**, so JS could watch a property Luau refuses
  - **Six `Instance` methods were bound but undeclared**, so an author calling `model:FindFirstChildOfClass(...)` got a typecheck error against a method the engine answers. Worse, `FindFirstChild`'s `recursive` argument was undeclared, so the call typechecked and silently answered the non-recursive question
  - `PlayerGui:GetGuiObjectsAtPosition` reads `Resolved::Order`, which the layout pass already writes back, rather than re-deriving paint order - `gui/AGENTS.md` refuses a second answer to what is on top. The test uses two overlapping objects and asserts the order *flips* when `ZIndex` moves. The tween trio reaches `TweenService` rather than growing a second interpolator. `WaitForChild` was held back here for being a yield and not a lookup, and landed later in this version with the second resume source it needed
- [x] `Instance:WaitForChild`, the last member of Roblox's tree surface this engine did not have and the only one that is a **yield rather than a lookup** - every other method crossed to `ScriptCall` at v0.18 and each answers from the store on the tick it was called. What it needed was a second resume source at the barrier: `ScriptCall::Await` is keyed on a `world::Ticket` because a bus reply is the only thing that had ever resumed a script here, and a wait resumes on the *tree*. `ChildWaiters` is the shared table - a parent, a name and a deadline in **ticks** through the same `TicksFor` `task.wait` and `Debris` use, so half a second is thirty beats on every machine and in every replay - and `PumpChildWaiters` runs immediately after `PumpTree` in both runtimes, so a `ChildAdded` handler and a woken script see one world. Two members on the interface and one branch in the Luau instance trampoline, which is the whole of what a suspending instance method costs
  - **`WaitForChild(name)` with no timeout raises, and that is the decision rather than an omission.** Roblox's form waits for ever and warns after five seconds; a script that waits for ever is a script that never finishes its tick, which is rule 5 met from the inside. Both approximations are worse than a refusal - a default timeout hands a cleanly-ported script a nil it never checks for, and returning `FindFirstChild`'s answer typechecks, works in every scene where the child is already there, and answers nil in exactly the case the method exists for. The timeout is declared **required** in `engine.d.luau` and `engine.d.ts`, so a ported script fails `just typecheck` before it fails at run time, and `script/AGENTS.md` carries the divergence where a porting author will meet it
  - The match is a `FindFirstChild` against the store rather than a filter over the arrival list, for three refusals rather than a preference: `TakeTreeChanges` is a *take* and the tree pump is per language; a reparent is only recorded once something has called the irreversible `ObserveTree`, which a world that merely waits should not have to; and a child **renamed** into the awaited name is an arrival as far as the author is concerned and is no reparent at all
  - `examples/MagicTests.luau` keeps the one line it changed to run here, and now says why rather than only that: the 215 specs run inline inside a single `LoadScene`, a wait suspends until a barrier, and there is no beat inside a scene load - so one miss would suspend the chunk and the scene would report success having run nothing

- [x] the internal / scripting-exposed split, and it is a rule the build checks rather than a convention - a file in `script/src/` that reaches `<lua.h>` or `<quickjs.h>`, however many include hops away, has a name beginning `Luau`, `Js` or `JavaScript`. `ServiceCatalogue.cpp` is the one exemption, named with its reason. `InputServices.cpp` was three jobs in 986 lines and is now two neutral service descriptions plus a Luau adapter; `RunService`, `BusServices`, `ServiceSurface`, `Instances`, `Values` and `Bindings.hpp` split or were renamed the same way. `TicksFor` existed three times - `script/AGENTS.md` claimed a debris deadline uses the same arithmetic `task.wait` does, and that was true only by coincidence
  - `JsBindings.hpp` was not self-contained: it used `ServiceProperty` and `ScriptMethod` and compiled only because every consumer included one of the right headers first. All 326 first-party headers were then compiled standalone and it was the only one
  - The **`ci` preset was already broken** and nothing in the loop ran it: `-Werror` on a dead file-local function, two `-Wswitch` gaps on `MessageKind::User`, and an unused parameter. `just preset=ci build` exits 0 now. The hand-written `BusStatus` union in the bindings tool is generated from `DescribeStatus`'s switch, so a status that has not reached the checked-in declarations fails `bindings-check` by name

- [x] setup players joining with ids and character spawning, which the server assigns on join - `scene::AddPlayer` had **no production caller at all**, so every world that ever ran had a `Players` service with nobody in it, and the consequence was not cosmetic: ownership is assigned to a player, so a server with no players had nobody to assign it to and `SetNetworkOwner` had no argument a script could obtain. A `UserId` comes from a counter on the world rather than a clock or a random draw, which is what makes two runs of one recording hand out the same identities. The name is `PlayerN` for the connection's slot and not `"Guest #####"` - a name a client chose is a field of an inbound message and this one ends up in the tree where scripts index by it, so rename the prefix if the spelling matters
- [x] demo playerlist with TAB - `examples/PlayerList.luau`, and the decision that shaped it is **where the rows are written**. Not `StarterGui`: that is a *template* `gui::ResetPlayerGui` clones at spawn, so a row added after somebody joined never reaches the copy they are looking at, and a list built there is correct for exactly one instant per player. Each player's own `PlayerGui` is both live and correctly scoped - `scene::PlayerOwning` makes it private on the wire - and it is the only place both are true at once, so a dedicated server keeps every client's list up to date and no client sees another's panel
  - **TAB is the viewer's question and the rows are the host's, and only one of the two crosses today.** `RunService:IsClient()` decides: a studio Play and a single-player client have a keyboard and hold the panel open; a dedicated server has none and leaves it *shown*, because a list that is permanently hidden is indistinguishable from one that is broken. Closing the gap needs a script running in the replica and `mono.client` runs none - filed against `D00122`, which now has a second consumer waiting on the same missing piece
  - The suite stands two players up, loads the scene and asserts each player's own panel names **both** of them. Red on two mutations before it was green: building into `StarterGui` finds no panel at all, and a list that holds only its own player counts one row where it wants two
- [x] finish replication for server/client, server authoritative. The mechanisms were in place and connecting them found three defects, none of them in the code being connected and every one invisible from either side
  - **`examples::EncodeShot` has a caller.** `Client::SubmitMove` sends a `game::MoveInput` every tick and an `examples::Shot` built from the live camera's ray on a click - through `scene::ReadAimIntent`, which sits beside `ReadMoveIntent` for that function's stated reason: the studio applies the same aim from a `PlayLink` with no socket in the middle, and two copies of "which way is the player looking" is the shape that drifts and drifts first in the editor. `mono.server/tests/Replication.cpp` stands two clients on a real socket, fires from one, and asserts the colour reaches the other - the shooter never says what it struck
  - **`scene::InputState::LatchPresses` latched keys and not buttons**, so a click that began and ended between two ticks was lost about two times in three. The frame/tick argument is written out in full above `InputState::Pressed` and had been applied to one of the two input devices, because jump was the only thing a simulation had ever acted on. `PressedButtons` came out of `Reserved`, so `sizeof` is still 56 and no save format moved
  - **The rewind history walked `Transform` *and* `Motion`, and `physics` takes a row's `Motion` away when it puts the body to sleep** - deliberately, so the solver's query never visits a resting row. A player standing still was therefore unhittable, and it presented as an ordinary miss because a hit test against an empty candidate list is one. It walks `RigidBody` now, which is the question that was meant: an anchored part never gets one, so the static geometry the old predicate was aiming at is still excluded and nothing else is
  - **A client's input tick goes stale in a quiet world.** A client stamps its input with the newest tick it has *applied*, and a tick reaches it only when something changed - so in a still scene its idea of the server's clock stops while the server's does not, and the rewind targeted a tick that had fallen out of the 32-tick ring. Resolved at the present rather than at the oldest frame held, which follows from *why* it goes stale, and cannot be gamed: rewinding is the favourable answer, so claiming a tick this server no longer remembers buys the least favourable resolution there is
  - **`SetClientViewpoint` has a caller**, three versions after it and `DistancePriority` were both written. The comment beside it said there was no per-client avatar and the placeholder world was cubes with nobody in it, and it was right - so the score fell back to the round robin it exists to replace, on every connection. `UpdateClientViewpoints` points each client at its own character's root once a tick, before the delta is built; a client between a join and a spawn is *forgotten* rather than left where it fell, because a round robin is a better answer than a stale one
  - **And `Struck`/`Dropped` are reported.** Two counters incremented since v0.9 and printed nowhere - the file's own comment says they are two rather than one so that a payload which did not decode is not buried in a hit count, and neither could be noticed at all while neither was reported
- [x] engine-level and cdn-level fast-flags for enable/disable types of content - **two verbs over one vocabulary**, because a form can be refused in two places and they mean different things: `content.<form>` is *this process will not decode, route or hand it over*, and `cdn.publish.<form>` is *this form does not enter a publication*. There is deliberately no serve-time gate, and that is a fact about an origin rather than an omission - after a publish there are only hashes and a hash cannot be walked back to a name, so publish time is the one moment the name is real
  - **The vocabulary is generated from the extension table rather than written out**, so a format added there arrives with its flags and no second list. Building it merged two lists that already had to agree: `AssetKind.cpp` carried `EXTENSIONS` for routing and a separate `SOURCES` for "does this still need a bake", with a comment on the second saying it was the one that goes stale. They are one table with three columns now and `IsRuntimeReadable` is a single negation over it
  - Gated at four doors, all before anything decodes: `assetc`'s dispatch, `cdn::Publisher`'s classification, the client's request and the studio's picker. A refused form is **refused by name with a reason** and counted - `PublishReport::Refused`, `assetc::Report::Refused` - because a run that quietly produced fewer assets than its input holds is the mystery the whole layer exists to end. The `cdn` suite asserts the store is *smaller*, which a gate applied after the chunker would fail while passing every other assertion
  - Everything is allowed by default, so `just determinism` and `just replay-check` are unaffected by construction rather than by luck. Both still pass byte-identical
- [x] engine, cdn, client and server configs - `core::Flags` is the store and `core::Config` is the adapter, and keeping them apart is what lets a program add a source without the store learning a fourth format. Precedence is a property of the *source* - built-in < config file < environment < command line - so a program may apply them in whichever order suits it and a file still cannot overwrite what somebody typed
  - **Frozen before the loop starts, and that is rule 5 rather than tidiness.** A value that can move between two ticks is a value two machines can disagree about, and the disagreement arrives as a desync a long way from the flag that caused it
  - **A flag is the *default* for the matching option and not a second copy of it**, and the built-in values are read off a default-constructed `Options` - so `Client.hpp` and `Server.hpp` stay the one statement of what each program does with nothing configured. The subtle half is `||` rather than `=` for every bare flag: an absent `--stats` is silence and not "off", and assigning would let a command line with no panel flags on it overrule a config file that asked for one
  - `--config PATH`, `ATOMIC_*`, `--flag NAME=VALUE` and `--flags` on all six programs. A key naming no declared flag is an **error** - `core::Arguments` already takes that position for a command line and a typo that produces silence fails at the behaviour, days later, somewhere unrelated. `engine.log-level`, `engine.jobs.workers` and `engine.serial-compute` were each reachable from one program's flag and are now settings anywhere
- [x] the deferred register worked rather than added to. **Four entries retired, and three of them were doing work no code was doing**
  - **`D00126` - the formatter was not pinned and "21 or newer" was the bug.** Two majors do not agree on the same file, so a box carrying an unversioned 21 *and* a versioned 23 formatted with 21 because the bare name was tried first, while a box with only 23 formatted with 23 - and the diff landed on files nobody had touched, which is what everybody was reverting by hand. One number in one place, `CLANG_FORMAT=` as the deliberate escape, and it warns when it is used
  - **`D00125` - `just client-smoke` presses a button in a shipped client with no display.** It needed two things the client did not have: `--headless`, and `--click NAME`, which resolves the element through `gui::Resolved` and synthesises the press into `input::Translator` as an ordinary SDL event - so it travels the path a real click travels rather than a shortcut past it. **It found a live bug on its first run**: `examples::LoadScene` kept the only reference to the VM it created, so `RuntimeOf` answered null for a `--script` world and *every* gui event the router produced was delivered nowhere. The router was right, the events were right, and the last hop was missing - which is the same shape as the bug the entry was originally filed for. The recipe is red on that regression and green on the fix
  - **`D00124` - `ServiceComponent::Fixture` refuses something at last.** It had said since v0.7 that an author may not delete or reparent a service and nothing read it, so a script could `Destroy()` `Lighting`. `ecs::Store::Protect` is the seam and **`InstallServices` is its only filler** - the entry's own warning was that a per-world seam is one somebody forgets, and that door is idempotent and runs on a loaded world as well as a fresh one, so protection arrives by construction. The four authored doors refuse; everything else keeps `DestroyInstance`, because `Debris` draining its queue is the engine moving its own furniture
  - **`D00127` was filed and then deleted rather than carried.** A settings list had no shape in the config format, which is a thing to build and not a thing to record: `core::FlagKind::List` appends on a repeated key and is replaced by a source that outranks it, so `client.content-sources` and `cdn.upstreams` stopped being comma-split and a path may now contain anything
  - `D00109` is *mostly* closed and stays open honestly - two of its three bullets are gone and the third turned out to be a different thing. Nothing reads `Connector::Unconfirmed()`, and the reason is a decision one module along rather than an oversight here: `game/Play.hpp` has a client send *intent* and never result, so there is nothing to reconcile and `Prediction`'s premise is one this engine does not currently share
- [x] `D00122` closed: a join is two blobs now, because `ReplicatedFirst` was first in the delta stream and a client's first view of a world is not the delta stream. `Authority::SetPreface` is the seam - a predicate over entities, no `ClientId`, the same shape `SetInterest` has - and `BeginSnapshot` captures what it claims as its own snapshot, finished before the world's first chunk is built. The entry called two blobs the honest shape and said it doubles the join state machine, and it did: two cursors, two completions, a `SnapshotStage` on every chunk, and `PROTOCOL_VERSION` 6
  - **The preface merges and the world sweeps, and swapping the two is the bug the shape invites.** A preface is a slice of a world, so a receiver applying it authoritatively would destroy everything it fails to mention - which on a *re-snapshot* is the whole world the client already holds, wiped a moment before being sent it again. So `Replica` applies the preface as an `Overlay` and the world blob authoritatively, and the world blob still carries the preface's entities: that one is the complete picture and the sweep behind it is what retires a stale row. The duplication is the size of what a host puts in front, not the size of the world
  - **A tick's chunks come from one blob, which is what makes the ordering a rule rather than a likelihood.** `AGENTS.md`'s refused chunk - `applied=184 refused=17865`, a cursor that read like a protocol error - has a second way to be reached once there are two cursors, and a preface chunk the link turned away beside a world chunk it took would be resent *behind* bytes it was meant to precede. Two blobs never share an outgoing list, so no refusal can reorder them, and the cost is one tick at the seam of a join that already spans many. `Carried::Stage` is the other half: a refusal rewinds its own cursor
  - **Red before green, and on the thing that matters rather than on arrival.** `engine.replication.stream` asserts that at the tick the preface lands the replica holds it and holds *one* row; `server.replication` stands the real server binary up over a real UDP socket with a nine-stud part under `ReplicatedFirst` and a fifteen-hundred-part world, and catches the replica holding exactly two entities while `Joined()` is still false. Each was checked against three separate mutations - no preface blob, the two blobs streamed in the other order, and `Unsent` rewinding the wrong cursor - and every one of them is red
  - **The script half is deliberately not built, and it is not a gap.** Roblox also runs `ReplicatedFirst`'s scripts before the rest of the tree arrives; `mono.client` runs no replicated scripts at all, so an ordering guarantee about script execution would be a guarantee about something that does not happen. That is also what `examples/PlayerList.luau`'s TAB toggle is still waiting on - a script running where the viewer is - and it is unchanged by this
- [x] `D00015(b)` closed: the replicated world has an audit. The server hashes groups of the state a client has already *acknowledged*, sends the digests, and takes back the groups that disagreed; the repair is the recovery walk that already existed, so a disputed group is put back into `Unconfirmed` and nothing new resends anything. It is the only thing in the module that can notice a client quietly holding the wrong value - the class of bug this version chased one cause at a time - and it is the one message that speaks on a world at rest, which is why it is off by default and `mono.server` turns it on. `PROTOCOL_VERSION` 7, `assets::HashTree` rather than a third hash
  - **The entry's open question was whose hash it is, and the answer is per-client over a rotating slice.** A cell hash is shareable only if a client sees a whole cell or none of it, which turns interest management from per-entity into per-cell - a real architectural change and a larger one than a bandwidth entry authorises. The rotation is what bounds the per-client cost anyway: 32 entities in one datagram once every eight ticks, so a world twice the size is swept half as often rather than costing twice as much. **The cadence argument was the strongest part of the proposal and it is what shipped**
  - **Membership is on the wire, and that one decision made the rest fall out.** The audit lists the entities it hashed rather than letting the receiver derive them, so the sender can leave out three sets the receiver knows nothing about: anything still unconfirmed (the delta path is already correcting it, so the server is merely ahead), anything the client owns (v0.13 ownership makes its copy the newer one), and anything carrying a `SuppressWhenTagged` tag (the far side derives that row on purpose). Nothing about interest, ownership or suppression had to cross
  - **Both ends hash the value a *replica* holds, and assuming the quantiser is idempotent would have been wrong.** The authority puts its own value through the same encode-and-decode a join snapshot already goes through, so the two ends compute one expression over one buffer. Measured on the real smallest-three rotation: **1666 of 2 million** uniformly random orientations re-encode to different bytes, which is one part in twelve hundred reported as a mismatch for ever, every sweep
