  - **The limit on the answer is the server's and none of it is read off the message**, which the entry called part of the security argument rather than a tuning knob: the tick must be the audit this server issued, the labels must be groups it hashed, strictly ascending so one cannot be named twice, and an audit may be answered once. An audit the link refused is struck off by `Unsent`. The most a client claiming everything mismatches can buy is the repair of the slice the server had already chosen to look at. Ten cases, ten mutations, all red - the two that matter are a replica corrupted on a still world coming back right, and every group label there is being refused and counted
- [x] `D00015(c)` closed, and with it the whole of `D00015`: a client dead-reckons the bodies nobody owns. `replication/AGENTS.md`'s prediction invariant was **amended on purpose** rather than read narrowly - *prediction is the local player and nothing else* still holds word for word about what it was written about, and beside it now sits the distinction it never drew: predicting an input-driven agent is guessing at a human, where dead-reckoning a body is evaluating a function the authority already sent, because `scene::Motion` *is* the derivative of the pose. `SnapshotBuffer::DeadReckonSeconds` measures the guess, `client::CollectReplicated` decides who gets one, and `physics::Advanced` is the arithmetic - the same function `IntegrateMotion` is now written out of, so a client cannot integrate what the server does not
  - **`D00010` is unchanged and is now scoped, which is the reconciliation the entry called the actual design question.** A dry buffer still stops: the render clock halts at the newest sample, `Sample` holds the last pose the authority described, and the original case asserts it verbatim. What is new sits outside the buffer and only on rows carrying the one thing D00010 did not have - a velocity the server sent, which is a right answer to extrapolate *toward*. A player has none and a body with no `Motion` has none, and for both the freeze stands for D00010's own reason
  - **The bound nobody had measured, measured.** Per axis, worst case over the whole of both grids: **0.977 mm at zero elapsed, 1.042 mm after one 60 Hz tick, 1.367 mm at 100 ms, 1.953 mm at 250 ms, 4.883 mm at one second** - a straight 3.906 mm per second on the position grid's own 0.977 mm intercept, every figure within 0.003% of the analytic line. So the horizon is **derived rather than chosen**: a quarter of a second is where the integrated velocity error equals the position error it started from, which is 64 m over 256 m/s with the step counts cancelling
  - **Bounded twice, because the second bound is what stands in for the collision nothing runs.** No broad phase, no narrow phase, no solver and no gravity - a replica holds whichever colliders interest let it see, so a swept stop would be confidently wrong about the geometry that did not arrive. Instead a body is never carried further than its own smallest half-extent, which also scales the feature the right way round with no second rule: a walking body gets the full quarter second and a twenty-metre-a-second one gets 25 ms
  - **The correction is the guess *unwound*, which is neither a blend nor a snap.** The offset is `velocity * seconds`, so easing the seconds to zero eases the offset to zero - one number for the whole world, no per-entity pose kept, continuous by construction. Given back at half real time, which is the fastest rate at which a corrected body still moves forward; at one it stands still and above one it reverses, and reversing is the one artefact more visible than the snap. Thirteen mutations, thirteen red, and the one worth naming is dropping the guess in a single frame - invisible to every counter, caught only by asserting that no drawn step is backwards
  - **It cost a caller and no tick order, which is less than the entry predicted.** No system, phase or registration was added; `physics::Advanced` is `inline` in a public header, so measured on `release` after the change `IntegrateMotion.cpp.o` is still out of the client binary along with `BroadPhase`, `NarrowPhase`, `Solve` and `SyncBroadphase`
- [x] `D00102` closed: a world carries its bake pipelines. The dependency half shipped at v0.13 - `Engine::bakegraph` is the node vocabulary and the document format at `shared`, linking `Engine::core` and nothing else - and what was left was that nothing carried a document. `game` names `Engine::bakegraph` now and writes `<AssetPipelines>` beside `<Sources>`; `bake::PipelineSet` is the named collection a world holds, in the ECS as a resource registered beside the classes so its component id cannot be minted under the compiler's spelling of the type. The block is **one CDATA block of `bakegraph`'s own line format** rather than XML restating it, which is the shape the render half's `<Pipelines>` used and is why there is one grammar for a pipeline instead of two that can disagree while both parsing. `server` gained `bakegraph` in `expected_graph.json` and still does not name `bake` - a dedicated server parses a pipeline document with no PNG, JPEG, GIF, BMP, OBJ, glTF or PMX decoder linked, which is the property the whole entry was about
  - **The decision the entry left open was what a world does with a node kind this build does not have, and it matches the render half: refuse the pipelines, keep the world, warn.** The vocabulary is a closed list, so `node bevel` written by a newer editor is indistinguishable from a typo, and a world whose parts all loaded and whose recipes did not is recoverable - refusing the document loses somebody's level over a bake chain they can re-author. The *whole* set goes rather than the readable half, because part of a saved set is not a set, it is a file from a build somebody should be told to upgrade from. Both directions are pinned: the tests were checked against three mutations - the writer removed, the refusal ignored (the world then holds the half that parsed), and the refusal escalated to a failed load - and each is red
  - **No `FORMAT_VERSION` move, and that is a decision rather than an omission.** A world with no pipelines writes no element, so every file this build produces for existing content is byte-identical to the last one's, and an older build skips a child it does not recognise. Bumping would turn a file it could otherwise read into a refusal and buy nothing; the version is for a change that makes an old *reading* wrong, which is what `2` was. `just determinism` and `just replay-check` are unchanged
  - The entry's own v0.13 note had argued the block should wait for a panel, since a save-format section with no producer looks like a feature and is not. It is written now because the wiring is the half carrying the dependency risk, and it is worth more proved against a live round trip than remembered: an Assets Pipeline panel becomes a UI change against a format that already round trips, rather than a format change made beside one
- [x] `D00119`'s `Team` half closed, and closed in the order the entry named: the reopen trigger was *a `SpawnLocation` class*, because a team whose only effect is a coloured name is a field rather than a feature. So the class came first - deriving from `Part`, carrying `TeamColor`, `Neutral` and `Enabled`, and reached through `PartDesc::Class` so `MakePart` is still the only constructor - then `Teams` and `Team` through `InstallServices` and the `ServiceComponent`/`ServiceScope` pattern (`Shared`, for `Players`' reason: a server decides who is on which side and a client asks which side it is on, so both halves need the list), and only then `Player.Team`. **`scene::FindSpawn` resolves the class *and* the name**, because every scene in `mono.engine/examples` builds its pad as `block("SpawnLocation", ...)` and a plain part wearing the name is read as an enabled, neutral spawn - dropping that half would have put all of them at the origin with nothing in the file saying why. The filter prefers a pad of the player's own colour, falls back to a neutral one and never takes another team's, and it takes the **first in tree order** rather than Roblox's random draw: a respawn drawn from a random number is a recording that does not replay. `CharacterAppearanceId` stays open with its own trigger - an avatar format - because `CharacterDesc` is still three colours and six boxes
- [x] `D00113` closed: there is one node graph and it is vendored. The entry named the obstacle to the option it preferred - the template repository had no remote whose commits this one could name - and it has one now, so the editor's copy went upstream and comes back as `mono.vendor/nodegraph`. The direction is the part worth writing down: the further-along implementation was *ours*, and everything general in it went out - folding a selection into a node whose interface is derived from how it was wired, frames, a graph-owned template library, an async evaluator with a worker pool, previews that belong to a wire rather than to a node, a software surface rasteriser, a PNG writer, the inspector registry and the whole suite, ported off Catch2 and now 415 checks in that repository rather than 295 assertions in this one. What stayed is what only an engine has: a texture for a picture, a theme, an undo stack and a dockable window. Two seams came out of the split as free functions so a test can reach them - `NodePreviewTexture`, which is a `memcpy` exactly as long as both sides mean red first and the top row first, and `ApplyNodeChrome`, which hands the library the four theme values it draws chrome with, every frame rather than at start-up because the palette and the interface scale are both settings somebody can change while the panel is open. `VENDOR_PUBLIC` is the cost: the `Editor` holds the graph, the canvas and the evaluator by value. And the third implementation the entry warned about is now a build failure rather than a paragraph - `just check-one-node-graph` refuses any first-party file that opens `namespace nodegraph`
- [x] `mono.studio/src/Pipelines.cpp` deleted. It has been a zero-byte file since the render-pipeline revert at `ec79c88` and was still being compiled into `studio_lib`; the `mono.studio/CMakeLists.txt` comment that pointed at it named a dependency the same revert removed. That revert's own convention is a `TODO(render-pipeline)` marker in the files it touched - fifteen of them in this program - and an empty translation unit carries none, so it recorded nothing and read as a placeholder for something
- [x] `D00104`'s `.rbxm` half closed: the Rojo sync builds Roblox's binary model, and the reader is `bake`'s rather than the editor's - a parser for a foreign binary format is the largest attack surface a content pipeline has, and `bake/AGENTS.md` is where that rule already lived. `bake::ReadRobloxModel` hands back a tree of class names, names and values and `studio::RojoSync` maps it onto instances, which is the same split that will take `.rbxmx` when something can parse XML. **A referent becomes the shape of the tree and nothing else**: the format numbers its instances so its `PRNT` chunk can say which is inside which, those numbers are whatever wrote the file chose, and nothing that leaves the reader is numbered - the tree is nested rather than a flat list with indices, and a `Ref`-typed property is refused rather than turned into one. An **enum** is refused for the same reason from the other side: it is a number naming a member of Roblox's table, and the only ways to honour one are to ship Roblox's numbering or to use this engine's declaration order, which is precisely what rule 4 forbids
  - **The subset is decided and written down rather than discovered by whoever hits it.** `String`, `ProtectedString`, `Bool`, `Int32`, `Int64`, `Float32`, `Float64`, `UDim`, `UDim2`, `Vector2`, `Vector3`, `Color3`, `Color3uint8`, `Rect`, `NumberRange`, `CFrame` and `SharedString` - every type this engine's own `ecs::PropertyType` can carry without a table it does not have - and **everything else is refused by name**, per class and per property. That is safe rather than lucky because of a property of the format: a `PROP` chunk holds one property of one class and nothing after it depends on its bytes, so an undecoded type is a chunk skipped whole and the list can grow one row at a time. The suite puts the refused chunk *between* two that must arrive, because a refusal consuming the wrong number of bytes would take the rest of the file with it and a case with the refusal last could not see that
  - **`ProtectedString` is the row nobody would have added**, and it was found by reading real files rather than by reasoning about the format: a script's `Source` is written under a type number of its own carrying identical bytes to a `String`, so a reader written from the type list alone imports every script in the file with no program in it. That mattered twice over, because Roblox keeps the text on the instance and this engine keeps a key into the world's `SourceCache` - an import that made only the instance would produce a `Script` that sits in the tree and never runs, which looks exactly like a script with a bug in it
  - **The `CFrame` rotation survives, unlike the `.model.json` path's.** A model file writes twelve numbers and this module reads only the three of its position; a `.rbxm` states an orientation as one byte naming one of twenty-four axis-aligned rotations, which is `6 * right + up + 1` over `[+X, +Y, +Z, -X, -Y, -Z]` with the third axis their cross product. That is a formula rather than a checked-in table because only one of the two can be got wrong in a way review would not see, and it was verified against a real place file: every rotation id occurring across a hundred and forty thousand instances resolves to a perpendicular pair, and identity comes out at the `0x02` Studio writes for it
  - Three bounds are on numbers rather than on the buffer, which is where a binary parser is actually attacked: a chunk's declared inflated size, checked before the allocation it causes because LZ4 expands by up to 255 times; the instance count, checked against a ceiling before a referent map is built from it; and the parent table's depth, which is also the cycle check, since a chain that has not reached a root within it is either too deep or a loop and both refuse the file. The LZ4 block decompressor is written here - the one departure from `mono.vendor`'s preference for a submodule, because a block has no framing, no checksum and no dictionary and the submodule would be larger than the decompressor - while Zstandard, which recent Studio writes and which is entropy coding, is the vendored one `delivery` already links. Proved under ASAN and UBSAN over a real Studio-written model, every truncation of it, and four hundred thousand mutations of it, plus a 6 MB Zstandard-framed place of 140,301 instances that reads clean
  - The editor's three decisions are the editor's and are stated where somebody will meet them: one instance named after the *file* (the container allows any number and Rojo's table maps one, so a file with several is refused rather than wrapped in a folder nobody wrote), an unknown class becoming a `Folder` and saying so - the same answer `$className` already gets - and a property this engine has no declaration for being **counted rather than listed**, because Studio writes every property of every class and a note each would be a hundred lines saying this engine is smaller than Roblox
- [x] `D00001`'s macOS bullet examined, and the sentence it had carried since v0.1 was false in the half that mattered: **there is no cross-compile step**. Nothing in the root `CMakeLists.txt`, `mono.build/` or any module's CMake names MSL, SPIRV-Cross or `shadercross`, and `mono.vendor` holds no translator - `shaderc` pins glslang, SPIRV-Tools and SPIRV-Headers and carries no `spvc`. "Wired and untested" is a worse thing to record than "absent", because it reads as a step somebody has only to run. The examination also found the item is in **two** places rather than one: `mono.engine/render` links `shaderc` into the shipped client and `render::ShaderCompiler` compiles runtime-authored GLSL while the engine runs, so a build-time translation would cover the 49 built-in modules and leave every `ShaderScript` broken on the platform
  - **The half a Linux machine can hold up is now held up.** `mono.tools/shadercheck` and `just shader-check`, in `just check`, reflect every `.spv` the build produces and hold it to the resource contract `SDL_CreateGPUShader` documents: one entry point named what the renderer asks for at the stage its filename claims, every resource carrying an explicit `layout(set, binding)`, every resource in the descriptor set SDL names for its stage, bindings contiguous within a set because every other shader format numbers them by counting and a gap shifts everything after it, and no SPIR-V capability outside an allowlist of what MSL can express - `Float64` being the one that compiles happily for Vulkan and cannot be translated at all. MSL, DXIL and DXBC all derive their bindings from those sets, which is why this was never the part that needed a Mac. 49 modules pass, and the suites build their SPIR-V word by word so the negative cases are inputs rather than sentences
  - **It translates nothing and proves nothing about a Metal device, deliberately** - a check asserting something this machine cannot observe would be the same untested claim the entry was corrected for, arriving a second time. What it does derive is where each resource would land in Metal's index space, printed rather than asserted
  - A macOS client stops at the **configure** now, naming the reason, instead of building for four minutes and dying inside `SDL_CreateGPUDevice` - `render::Renderer` asks for `SDL_GPU_SHADERFORMAT_SPIRV` and Metal offers `MSL` and `METALLIB` and never that. That `message(FATAL_ERROR)` is the entry's new reopen trigger, made of the same material as the item: it fires for the first person who *tries* rather than for the first person who owns the hardware, and it cannot fire on Linux
- [x] `D00001`'s macOS bullet, again, and this time the translation runs. SPIRV-Cross is vendored on its `vulkan-sdk-1.4.357` branch - Apache-2.0, three of its eight libraries built - and `Engine::msl` wraps it: SPIR-V in, MSL out, and no SPIRV-Cross type in a public header. It is a **module and not code inside `render`** because there are two callers at two times: `mono.tools/shadercross` translates the built-in shaders during the build, and `Renderer::AddShaderVariant` translates a `ShaderScript` while the engine runs, which a build step cannot do because the script does not exist yet. Two implementations would have disagreed about which texture is `[[texture(0)]]` the first time somebody edited one, invisibly on Linux
  - **The check found a real defect before any Mac could.** SPIRV-Cross numbers resources in the order it walks the module's ids, and `SDL_CreateGPUShader` numbers them by kind then descriptor order - for `opaque.frag` that put `beamMap`, the *last* texture in set 2, at `[[texture(0)]]`. Every surface would have sampled its neighbour's map on macOS with nothing on Linux to say so. `Engine::msl` tells SPIRV-Cross every index now, and `shadercheck` reads the emitted `.msl` back and derives the same indices independently - the tool that emits does not grade what it emitted, which is the whole reason they are two programs
  - The format literals are gone. `SDL_CreateGPUDevice` asks for SPIR-V **and** MSL because the build produces both, and `render/src/ShaderBinary.hpp` asks the device which it took and answers the three things that move together: the format, which of the two staged files to open, and whether the entry point is `main` or `main0`. SPIR-V wins where a device offers both, so MoltenVK stays on the verified path. The `FATAL_ERROR` became a `WARNING`, because a refusal guarantees the one thing left - somebody running this - can never happen
  - **`just shader-check` reported 49 modules and there are 14.** The shader stage was written to and never reconciled, so the 35 `.spv` orphaned when the built-in GLSL moved from `render` to `resources` in v0.14 were still on disk, still staged into `client/`, `studio/` and `unified_server_client/`, and still being checked. Three fixes for three causes: `_mono_add_shaders` prunes a module's stage against what it compiled, `mono_add_program` clears a staged directory before copying into it, and `mono_prune_shader_stage` removes the directory of a module that stopped owning shaders at all - the case neither of the other two can see, because a module with no `shaders/` is one nothing looks at again
- [x] `D00117`'s keyboard half, up to but not including a character reaching the text. **`gameProcessedEvent` is true for a key now, and it is true for exactly Roblox's reason**: a press on a `TextBox` takes the keyboard, a press anywhere else gives it back, and both pumps ask `InterfaceHasKeyboard` where they already asked `InterfaceHasPointer` - `IsPointerReport` stopped being a veto that kept `true` off a key and became the selector between two independent answers, which is what makes a place that moves its character on `InputBegan` finally able to tell typing from walking
  - **The focus lives in the ECS and the router only decides it**, which is rule 2 applied rather than dodged: `UserInputService:GetFocusedTextBox` at L9 has no route to a `gui::Router`, so a handle on the router would have been the second statement of one fact. `GuiServiceState::FocusedTextBox` is where it rests, beside `SelectedObject` and for that field's reason, and `Router::Update` takes a mutable store for this and nothing else. Both existing call sites already passed one
  - **A destroyed focused box is a question rather than a crash, and rule 3 is why.** The handle carries a generation, so `FocusedTextBox` validates with `Store::Alive` on the way out and answers null - nothing had to hook `DestroyInstance`, which is the same argument `gui/AGENTS.md` makes for `Rendered` being swept. Reparenting deliberately does *not* release it: a handle does not change when its instance moves, and nothing the person did says they stopped typing. No `FocusReleased` is emitted for a box that died, because the event names an element and a dead one has nothing to fire at
  - **A `TextBox` was not clickable at all, which is what the keyboard half turned up.** It is not a `GuiButton` and its `Active` is false by default, so `Pick` walked past it and a click landed on whatever was behind - a text field that ignores the mouse, in the shipped build, since the tree went in. `TakesInput` now tests `Entry` beside the button class, for the reason it already tested a class rather than a field
  - **`SDL_EVENT_TEXT_INPUT` reaches `input::Translator` and is UTF-8 all the way**, appended whole rather than a byte at a time and cleared as a frame delta beside `MouseDelta`. `Entry::CursorPosition` is Roblox's one-based *character* index and `gui::Focus` counts continuation bytes to place it, so a box holding `héllo` reads 6 and not 7 - a caret from `Text.size()` is the failure that only shows up in somebody else's language
  - **What was left was deliberately left**, and named three things: `SDL_StartTextInput` on the window, the per-frame hand-off of `TypedText()`, and the editing rules themselves. All three are the bullet below, and the entry is retired
- [x] `D00117` closed: **you can type into a `TextBox`.** `gui::Typing.hpp` is the editing - `Type(store, Typing)` over the focused box, inserting at the caret, replacing a selection, deleting one *character* on Backspace, extending with shift-arrows, and treating Return as done on a single-line box and as a line break on a `MultiLine` one. The client asks SDL for text only while `gui::FocusedTextBox` answers something and hands `Translator::TypedText()` straight into the world once a frame, **before** `Router::Update`, because the characters were aimed at whatever held the focus when they arrived rather than at the box a press is about to move it to
  - **The text lives in `Label::Text` and nowhere else**, which is rule 2 again and the reason `Type` is a free function over a store rather than an editor object: a pending string anywhere would disagree with a script that wrote `TextBox.Text` while somebody was typing. `src/Utf8.hpp` is the single crossing between a character index and a byte offset, shared with `Focus` - two copies of that arithmetic are two answers that are both right in English
  - **`Type` clamps the caret before reading it, and that is the crash nobody writes a test for.** `TextBox.Text` is a plain property the class table writes, with no setter to hook, so a handler replacing the text with something shorter is ordinary and every offset from the old caret is then past the end. The clamp sits at the one reader that indexes by it. `examples/Interface.luau` had exactly that handler and now reports into a second label instead, because clamping keeps it safe and does not give the person their sentence back
  - **`enterPressed` is a real answer at last.** Roblox's argument was hard `false` under this entry's name; Return releasing a box now carries `GuiEvent::Entered`, which the router never sets and the caller builds from `TypeResult::Released` - no press happened, so there is nothing for `Router::Update` to have picked
  - **Nothing in the shipped tree had ever called `gui::InstallGuiServices`**, found while proving the rest: no real world had a `GuiService`, so `gui::Focus` refused every press and the entire focus half was inert outside its own suite - the shape of "the seam was right and one end was never connected" that this version has now hit three times. `examples::LoadScene` calls it beside `scene::InstallServices`, which covers every `--script` world in the client and the server; the editor still installs none
  - **Proved through the shipped client with no keyboard attached.** `--type TEXT` synthesises the SDL event `--click NAME` has focused a box for, and a headless run of a probe scene reports `text=Hé😀 bytes=7 cursor=4` - three characters, seven bytes, the caret counted in the first. A windowed run logs `text input: on - SDL ok` on the frame the press lands, which is the half no test can reach
- [x] `D00120` closed: `Player.Backpack` holds `Tool`s and the engine understands one. The entry named its own reopen trigger as *a joint, or an attachment strong enough to carry a handle*, and pointed at `scene::Attachment`; the answer is neither. `ResolveAttachments` runs in `PreRender` and resolves a frame rather than moving a part - its own header says a caller wanting a weld is asking for what that pass does not promise - where `scene::CharacterLimb` is already an anchored part carried by a root at a fixed offset, which is what `Characters.hpp` chose over `Motor6D` for the whole body. **A held handle is one more part in that formation**, so the class needed no constraint solver, no new pass and no new component: it needed the row a forearm already has. Three things came free with it and none was built - the offsets replicate, the handle's per-tick `Transform` stops crossing because `scene.CharacterLimb` is already `replication`'s suppressor for it, and a client poses the handle from the root it interpolated. `D00115`'s second consumer
  - **Equipping is the parent and there is no flag beside it**, which is what makes the wire rule fall out with nothing added: `scene::PlayerOwning` answers the owner for anything under a `Player`, so a stowed tool reaches exactly one client and a held one - under a `Model` in `Workspace` - reaches everybody. `server.replication` asserts both ends of that over a real socket, two clients, one `StarterPack` template and four handle-counted assertions
  - **Who may move one is the authority, through two doors that already existed.** A client that can reparent its own tool can duplicate it, and the write survives until the next delta contradicts it - an inventory that works sometimes rather than a refusal. `ecs::Store::SetProperty` has refused every property write in a replica since v0.3 and is what refuses a `LocalScript`'s `tool.Parent = ...`; `EquipTool` and `UnequipTool` make the same refusal on `Store::AdoptOnly`, which is `scene::TakeDamage`'s pair one class along. `UpdateToolGrips` is deliberately on the other side of that line - it reparents nothing, so a replica runs it and poses a handle whose tool arrived over the wire
  - **Death and departure needed no code, and asserting that is how it was established.** A corpse keeps what it was holding - `D00121`'s rule that a body stays where it fell - and `LoadCharacter` destroys it with the tool in it, then refills `Backpack` from `StarterGear`: the two-container rule `Services.hpp` already states, unchanged. A departing player's `Backpack` goes with the `Player` and their held tool with the character `ReclaimOrphanedCharacters` collects
  - **One property, because one property has a reader** - `Tool.Grip`. Roblox's `CanBeDropped`, `Enabled`, `RequiresHandle`, `ToolTip` and `TextureId` have no drop input, no activation channel and no inventory behind them, and a property nothing acts on is precisely what this entry refused the class for. `BackpackItem` is skipped for the same reason: an abstract base registered here is an instantiable class that does nothing in the insert palette
  - **An older bug fell out of it.** `CharacterLimb` put its `core::CFrame` before its `ecs::Entity`, so it carried a four-byte hole *and* four bytes of tail padding that `Reserved` was named to prevent - eight uninitialised bytes into every save file and every wire delta, in the one module whose `AGENTS.md` forbids exactly that. Found by comparing a restored row against a recomputed one and watching two byte-identical limbs disagree; the members are widest-first now and the struct is forty bytes with no holes
- [x] `D00104` closed outright: the `.rbxmx` half shipped beside the binary one, and **every row of Rojo's file table is now built**. `bake::ReadRobloxModelXml` reads the XML container into the same `RobloxModel` the binary reader produces, `studio::RojoSync` picks the reader by extension and nothing below that differs, and `UnbuiltKind` - the function that named the mappings this engine could not build - is deleted rather than left returning nothing. The case that matters is neither reader's: `bake/tests/RobloxModel.cpp` reads one model written in both containers and compares the trees field by field, because everything downstream is written against one type and the day the two stop agreeing, half of it starts behaving differently depending on which file an author exported
  - **The entry called this a vendor decision before a feature, and the vendor decision went the other way - for a reason the entry could not have known.** The engine already had *two* hand-written XML readers: `game::Xml`, written at v0.7 on the stated ground that the famous XML attacks are attacks on features a save file does not need, and a private tag scanner inside `Svg.cpp` since v0.13. So vendoring would not have removed a hand-written parser from the repository; it would have added a library beside one. `Svg.cpp`'s copy became `bake/src/Xml.hpp` and now serves both formats, `mono.vendor/AGENTS.md` carries the argument under a new "Not adding one" section beside the LZ4 precedent, and `D00128` files the consolidation rule 1 still prevents - `game` is L10 and `bake` is L9, so one reader serving both needs the parser to move *down*, exactly as the box filter moved to `assets` earlier this version
  - **The three attacks have tests rather than a note saying they were considered.** A `<!DOCTYPE` or `<!ENTITY` is refused at the token level, so there is no code that could expand an entity and therefore no option that could switch one on; an entity reference that is not one of the five predefines or a numeric character reference is refused *again* where it is read, because a reference silently dropped makes a bomb look like a typo; and nothing recurses, so depth is a count somebody bounded rather than the C stack running out with no file named. Each is red when the defence is removed, proved by mutating the shipped source
  - **A document-wide entity sweep is right for SVG and wrong here, and a real file proved it.** An SVG never unescapes, so a reference has to be caught by a sweep over the whole document; an `.rbxmx` holds CDATA, and a real model in the corpus carries the Luau pattern `"[&;]"` inside a script - a sweep refuses that file while naming an entity nobody wrote. So the refusal moved to each point a reference is actually read, with CDATA exempt because CDATA is text
  - **A script's source is a `CDATA` section**, which is this container's version of the `ProtectedString` row the binary half warned about, and it was found the same way: by reading real files. `Content` and `BinaryString` were the other surprise - separate elements in XML for what the binary container stores as one `String`, so refusing them would have made a `Decal` imported from `.rbxmx` lose a texture the same model keeps as `.rbxm`, which is a smaller subset on paper and a disagreement in practice
  - **A malformed value costs its property here, where the binary reader has to refuse the file.** A `PROP` chunk's bytes are positional and a payload that ran short has lost the cursor; an XML element carries its own end tag. Same rule against the format in front of it - never guess, and never lose more than the thing that was not understood
- [x] `D00004` closed by decision rather than by its trigger: `Engine::core` no longer links Crypto++, and `engine::core::Random` is **SplitMix64's finaliser** - Steele, Lea and Flood's `mix64variant13` (OOPSLA 2014), spelled as Vigna's public-domain `splitmix64.c` spells it - over the packed `(salt, index)` pair. The interface did not move, which is what the entry had said it was built for. **The entry was right that this saves the shipped programs nothing and the fresh numbers say so a third time**: relinked with `-Wl,-Map` on the `release` preset, `client` and `server` carry 43 of `libcryptopp.a`'s 173 members and 6,117 `CryptoPP::` symbols *either side of the change*, `cdn` carries 37 and 5,712, and not one member is first-caused by `libengine_core.a` before or after. What it is worth is the program that does not exist: a `main` calling only `Random::Float` goes from **1,552,030 bytes of text, 5,647 symbols and 36 members to 1,732 bytes and none of either**, reproducing the entry's figure to the byte
  - **The argument is dependency hygiene at L0 and one row of `THIRD_PARTY_NOTICES.md`, not size, and the code says so where somebody would otherwise read a size win into it.** `net` and `assets` still link Crypto++ and are still what puts it in all three programs, so the notices file was checked and deliberately not edited. `SecureWipe` went with `Random` because it was the only other call in `core` reaching the library, and `CryptoPP::SecureWipeBuffer` is a four-line volatile loop
  - **Nothing relied on it being unpredictable, and that was checked first.** Every security-sensitive site already names `core::Random` in a comment refusing it - `net::Handshake`, `replication::Hello`, `assets::GrantKey`, `network::SessionKey` - and `HttpService:GenerateGUID` is the apparent exception that is not one: it draws deterministically on purpose so a recording replays, has no first-party caller, and says in its own comment and in `engine.d.luau` that the value is never a token or a secret. **It was equally guessable before**, since its inputs are a draw counter and an FNV-1a hash of the world's *name* - twelve lines of Python knowing only the string `"scriptcall"` reproduce `EA90FF9A-9A3E-498F-B626-EA711F6C37F4`, the exact literal the suite pinned under SHA-256, so the hash never bought unpredictability and dropping it removes none
  - **It is also about twenty-five times faster, which was never an argument for it.** `Random::Float` 47 ns → **2 ns** and a three-salt position 146 ns → **7 ns** on the `bench` preset, against 1 ns for a bare xorshift32 in the same run, so `Random.hpp`'s five paragraphs warning that it was a load-time budget are deleted rather than softened
  - **Three suites outside `core` pinned values from the old mixer and are red until they are re-derived, which is the v0.13 re-examination's stated cost arriving.** `script/tests/ScriptCall.cpp` holds two copies of a GUID literal that is `C03C1E18-C515-4449-AA47-612C9B59D267` now - **the two VMs do not disagree and the failure reads as though they do**, since `CHECK(luau == javascript)` passes and Catch2 prints the probe's name, "GenerateGUID draws the same sequence in both", as context on every assertion in the case; `replication/tests/SnapshotBuffer.cpp` asserts `bufferedFrozen == 0` on a loss pattern that is `Random::Float(arrivalNumber, seed)` and nothing else, so a different seed restores it; and `mono.unified_server_client/tests/Harness.cpp` asserts `DrawnX < ClientX`, which had a latent assumption about which way the placeholder world's entity happens to move and now reads the other way. Left to the modules that own them rather than edited from here
  - **`just determinism` and `just replay-check` could not have caught a wrong answer here and still cannot** - both compare two runs of one binary, so both are green on a generator that changed and agrees with itself. `core/tests/Random.cpp` pins actual values instead, anchored on `0xE220A8397B1DCDAF`, the first output every reference implementation of SplitMix64 prints for seed zero
- [x] `D00005` closed by the owner's decision rather than by its trigger: **there is no GitHub CI and there is not going to be one**, and what replaces the deferral is `.githooks/pre-push` - checked in, pointed at by `core.hooksPath`, installed by `just install-hooks` which `just setup` now runs - building the `ci` preset before a push leaves the machine. The entry existed because `just preset=ci check` had twice been recorded as passing while the preset did not compile, and it did not compile a **third** time when this was picked up: `-Werror=dangling-reference` at `mono.cdn/src/Settings.cpp:125`, a `std::span` iterated straight off a temporary `Flag`, which `mono.client/src/Settings.cpp` has read through a named span since the day it was written
  - **The `ci` preset line under the internal/scripting-exposed split above says `just preset=ci build` exits 0, and it was written the same day the file carrying that warning was committed.** Both cannot be true. Recorded rather than quietly corrected, because that is the failure the entry was reopened for, arriving inside the version that closed it
  - **The hook builds and does not test, and the cost is measured rather than guessed**: ~1 s warm, ~1 min after a day of `dev`-preset drift, ~3 min from an empty `.cache/build/ci/`, against ~3 min for the whole of `just preset=ci check` of which `test-all` is 2.5. A warning going fatal is a compile-time property; the suites are `just check`'s, and they go red for whoever else's module is half-finished - a gate that fails for work that is not yours is a gate that gets skipped
  - Proved by breaking it, per rule 6: an unused local in `mono.tools/linecount` and `git push` refuses with `-Werror=unused-variable` named, `git push --no-verify` goes through, and the same push takes 1.4 s once the line is gone. A deletion-only push compiles nothing. `just determinism` and `just replay-check` are byte-identical either side
- [x] an instance created after a client joined arrives with its **name** and its **class**, which it never had. `DefaultReplicatedComponents` read "the `scene.` prefix, or `ecs.Hierarchy`", so `ecs.InstanceName` and `ecs.InstanceClass` crossed in a join snapshot - `Store::Save` carries every component - and never in a delta: an entity the world already held was named on a client and an entity created while that client was connected was not. It was found from the replica rather than reasoned about, as four children of a `Player` called nothing, and `server.replication` had been counting a client's tools by walking `ecs::Entity` handles because it could not ask for a child called `Backpack`. It asks by name now, for both containers and the `Sword` inside them
  - **A `ClassId` is a registration index, so the class crosses as its registered name - and that is `ecs`'s serialiser rather than a `TypeDescriptor::Wire` fitted over it.** Rule 4 with a second edge: `Hierarchy`'s handles are safe because `Store::Load` restores the directory *exactly*, and nothing restores the class table that way - `Classes::Register` runs wherever the code needing a tree runs and `RegisterGuiClasses` is called lazily on first use, so two processes of one build can number the same class differently depending on which of them opened an interface. Changing the registration fixes the save file and the studio's edit stream in the same breath and leaves one answer to how a class is written down. A name the receiver has never heard of leaves the entity **untyped and otherwise whole**, which is the render half's answer to a pipeline it cannot build rather than `RojoSync`'s substituted `Folder` - substituting is right for a project written against a class tree this engine implements a fraction of, and here it would make `:IsA` tell a confident lie about two builds that do not match
  - **Signed rather than said once in the creation message, and that is what a name costs.** A `Structure` message is reliable and would have carried both for one copy and nothing per tick - but `.Name` is a writable property a script sets whenever it likes, and a fact that crosses only at birth is the v0.7 recolour bug in a different component. So they go through the same `Unconfirmed` walk every other value does, which is a hash of two four-byte columns per tick and **zero bytes on a tick where nothing was created or renamed**. That is also why no string table was added: a per-connection dictionary to compress a class name sent once per instance would save nothing on the ticks that matter
  - **The packer assumed every row of a component was the same width, and it was already wrong before this.** `Authority::Pack` took one stride per component - the entry's bytes over its rows - and sliced each row back out at `row * stride` after the priority sort had reordered them, which is right for a `Transform` and false for anything writing a name: `scene.Visual` writes two and has been replicated since v0.4, so two parts whose meshes are spelt differently was enough. It was invisible because a demo world's mesh names are all empty and therefore all four bytes long. An offset and a length per row now; the receiver never needed changing, since `WriteComponents` always read the values sequentially
  - **The allowlist itself was the bug, so it is a list and a test rather than a prefix.** A prefix with one exception written into it cannot fail loudly - a component added to `ecs` simply does not appear, on the wire or in the suite that was meant to cover it. `engine.replication.defaults` now walks every registered `ecs.` component and requires each to be named on one side or the other, so a fourth is red rather than silent; `ecs.AttributeTable` is the one named exclusion and carries the catalogues' reason. Rule 6. Every case was checked against a mutation of the shipped source: the prefix rule restored, the fixed stride restored, and the raw `ClassId` serialiser restored, and each is red
- [x] `D00128` closed: **there is one XML reader in this engine now, and it is at L1.** There were three - `game::Xml` since v0.7, `Svg.cpp`'s private scanner since v0.13 and `bake/src/Xml.hpp` extracted from it earlier this version - and rule 1 was the entire obstacle to there being one, because `bake` is L9 and `game` is L10. `core/Xml.hpp` is the scanner all three callers run on, `bake/src/Xml.{hpp,cpp}` is deleted, and `game/Xml.cpp` lost its recursive-descent parser and kept the save format's document model, its limits and its writer. Every existing case at all three callers passes unchanged, which is the evidence that matters for a refactor
  - **The tier was chosen against what the parser depends on rather than against convenience, and the entry's own candidate was one tier too high.** The scanner opens no file, links no vendor, allocates no global and names no other module's type, so its height is the height of the standard library. `assets` was named in the entry because `assets::ResizeImage` had made exactly this move at v0.15 - but that one is arithmetic over an `assets::TextureData` and followed its own dependency, and **`game` does not link `assets` at all**, so consolidating there would have put content addressing, BLAKE3 and Crypto++ underneath the save format to gain a string scanner. The proof that L1 is right is that `mono.tools/architecture/expected_graph.json` did not change: every caller already links `core`, so the consolidation needed no new edge
  - **The writer did not go with the reader, and that was decided rather than skipped.** It writes this format's dialect - the declaration, tab indentation, `<x />` for an empty element, a `]]>` split across two CDATA sections - has one caller, and can have no second one below L10 because nothing in `bake` writes XML. A writer at L1 that only `game` calls would be an API nobody reaches for and a second place to keep one format true. `EscapeXml` stayed with it: unescaping faces hostile input and belongs at the reader; escaping is the writer's half
  - **The two refusal policies are both in the shared header and neither is the default, because collapsing them is the one way to get this wrong.** An SVG never unescapes, so its references have to be caught by a sweep over the whole document; an `.rbxmx` holds CDATA, and a real model in the corpus carries the Luau pattern `"[&;]"` inside a script - a sweep refuses that file while naming an entity nobody wrote. The save format takes `.rbxmx`'s route for `.rbxmx`'s reason, since a world's scripts are CDATA too. Each direction has a case that goes red: making `ReadContent` unescape a CDATA section reddens "an rbxmx script's ampersand is source and not a reference", the same case in `game`, and the CDATA half of the SVG's DOCTYPE case
  - **The save format changed in four ways, three narrowing and one not, all stated rather than discovered later.** An element may carry at most `XmlLimits::MaximumAttributes` (1024, against the nine `<Game>` writes) - the one count a document stated that the reader had been taking on trust; an entity reference must terminate within nine characters of its `&`, which every reference either format writes does - `&#x10FFFF;` is the longest legal one and fits - and `EscapeXml` has never written a numeric one; and a handful of *rejected* documents are now called something else - `<!FOO` is `Refused` where it was `Malformed`, `</>` is `Malformed` where it was `Mismatched`. **The widening is text between the declaration and the root**, which is now stepped over rather than refused: `junk<Game/>` and a CDATA section before the root are still `Malformed`, but `<?xml?>junk<Game/>` reads, because the scanner steps over comments and the declaration inside one call and catching the gap needs either an option nobody else wants or a second copy of the grammar in the save reader - the thing `D00128` existed to remove. The run expands to nothing, since no entity can be declared and a document still needs a `<Game format="N">` root to load. **Every document the v0.7 reader accepted is accepted and produces the same tree**, `Refused` survives the mapping because it is the only status that means somebody tried something, and `just determinism` and `just replay-check` are byte-identical either side
  - **The three attacks now have tests at four places rather than two**, because a defence that holds in the scanner and is not reached by a caller is a defence nobody has. Every new case was proved red by mutating the shipped source: the `<!` refusal removed, the unknown-entity refusal removed, the code point bound removed, the attribute bound removed, the name rule removed, the namespace-prefix option ignored, and - for the case that asserts 100,000 levels of nesting scan without a stack - **the scanner made recursive**, which turns that scan into a segmentation fault
- [x] **`D00014`'s first argument answered without QUIC: `net::CongestionControl`, and the send rate is a function of the path at last.** The algorithm is **Copa** (Arun and Balakrishnan, NSDI 2018) - a delay-based window steering toward a standing queue of two packets at the bottleneck - and the reason it is not a NewReno-style AIMD window is that a loss-based controller *finds the bottleneck by filling its buffer*, which is the mechanism rather than a side effect: on a home router with 300 ms of buffer that is 300 ms added to every input a player sends. Measured against a modelled 250 kB/s bottleneck with exactly that buffer: the rate converges on capacity and the standing queue settles at **9 to 13 ms**, two to three packets, where a loss-based controller settles at the buffer. `LinkSettings::BytesPerTick` survives as a hard ceiling with the controller underneath it - a game may legitimately refuse to spend more than N on one player on a path that would carry ten times that - and what it stopped being is a *rate*
  - **No second acknowledgement path, and that constraint shaped the whole thing.** The delay is `ReliableSender`'s RFC 6298 estimate arriving at `Link::RecordRoundTrip`, which `replication::Session` has called on every inbound packet since v0.9; the estimator grew the **variance** it never had, because a controller told only the mean cannot tell a wireless link's 15 ms swing from 15 ms of queue, and the pair of cases proving that is the pair that would otherwise have shipped a link permanently backed off. The loss signal is holes in `PacketHeader::Acknowledge`/`AcknowledgeBits` - fields `ReliableReceiver::Acknowledging` already stamps on every outgoing packet whatever its channel. `ReliableSender` reads them to retire payloads and `Link::ObserveAcknowledgement` reads them to find out whether the path dropped something. **Nothing new crosses the wire and `replication` needed no edit at all**
  - **`SendsOverBudget` did not have to stop meaning what it means**, which `D00014` predicted it would. Two counters rather than one: that one is a number somebody configured being enforced, `SendsOverAllowance` is the path refusing, and the difference is that a caller answers the first by changing the number and cannot answer the second by changing anything. `render`'s debug panel documents the first meaning against `D00007` and is untouched
  - **Copa's mode switch had to be restated and was measured being wrong first.** The paper detects a competing buffer-filler by asking whether the queue is ever nearly empty, which holds because its per-acknowledgement window oscillates hard enough to empty it. A window steered once a tick settles at its target and empties nothing, so the paper's predicate read a **solo** 250 kB/s path as contested and ratcheted its own standing queue from 9 ms to 190 ms. The question underneath survives the discretisation: if this end reduces its window round trip after round trip and the delay does not follow it down, the queue is not this flow's. Against a neighbour holding 200 ms the mode engages and `TargetQueuePackets` moves AIMD-style - the neighbour's own law - and when the queue drains the mode ends and the latency comes back
  - **Clocked by acknowledgements rather than by a timer**, which is what makes one implementation behave on a loopback and on a satellite: `Link::Advance` closes the observation period when the far side has acknowledged everything outstanding when it opened. The bug that made this necessary is worth recording - the first version paced against `AssumedRoundTripSeconds` and throttled the whole of `replication`'s suite, because a harness whose round trip is genuinely zero never leaves a timer-clocked ramp. Cold start is RFC 6928's initial window **once**, ten datagrams on the opening tick, paced from the second
  - **Twenty-nine mutations, twenty-nine red**, four of them needing a case that did not exist. The two worth naming are the ones review would not have found: measuring the round-trip variance *after* moving the mean survives every directional assertion because the wrong order is still in the right direction, so it is pinned as arithmetic; and a tick's length measured against the last time anybody named a time rather than against the last `Advance` reads every tick as a stall and clamps the allowance to sixty bytes, because the packets that arrived earlier in the tick named the same instant. `just determinism` and `just replay-check` are unaffected twice over - `net` reads no clock and nothing here is random, and `mono.server` does not serve clients on the replay path at all
- [x] `replication` reads the path the congestion controller measured, rather than beside it. Two connections, both of them the shape `net/AGENTS.md` warns about - a number this module could have had and was not asking for
  - **The round trip crosses with its variance.** `Session` is the only thing in the engine that measures one and the controller is the only thing that reads one, and `Link::RecordRoundTrip` had gained an RFC 6298 RTTVAR parameter that this call was defaulting away. Without it the controller's noise threshold falls back to a one-millisecond floor, so a jittery wireless path reads as a standing queue and the link narrows for a queue that is not there - on exactly the connections least able to spare it. Red on the mutation: the variance the link reports is **0.0 ms** with the argument dropped and non-zero with it, after a single measured trip, because RFC 6298 seeds the variance at half the first sample and no jitter has to be arranged to see the field arrive
  - **The two byte budgets stopped being two independent opinions, and the link's is the authority.** `AuthoritySettings::BytesPerTick` is a number somebody typed and `ConnectionStats::SendAllowanceBytes` is what the path was measured doing, so `Authority::Pack` spends the lower of the two. Packing past the allowance never sent more - `Link::Reserve` refuses the excess and `Unsent` rebuilds it next tick - it spent the encode and handed the shortfall to the *transport* instead of to the priority scheduler, which is the thing that exists to decide what a client sees when not all of it fits. `Authority::SetAllowance` is told rather than reading, because this module holds no link: the same division `SetPriority` and `Rewind::Record` are built on, and `Listener::Advance` is the one caller. A host that never wires it keeps `SIZE_MAX` and is unchanged
  - **The case is a comparison and not a `min()`.** One world run twice, differing only in what the *link* says it will carry, because a case checking that the minimum is computed passes against a wiring that never calls `SetAllowance` - the authority's own ceiling is still in force and the number still looks plausible. It is exactly that: both mutations, the missing `Listener` call and a `Pack` reading only its own settings, produce **10272 bytes on both runs**, and the fix bounds the narrow one at the link's 2 KB with the world still converging
- [x] **`D00031` closed: the editor understands `Enum.NormalId`, and what it took was the option the entry declined three times.** The finding that decided it is that there is no definitions-file spelling - `parseDeclaration` and `parseTypeAlias` both reach `parseName`, a single identifier, so a `declare` statement cannot produce a dotted type at all and every write to `importedTypeBindings` in Luau's `Analysis/` is a `require` or a host reaching into the frontend. `mono.tools/scriptcheck` was already that host, which is why `just typecheck` accepted a line an editor underlined for three versions. luau-lsp is now the same host: one hunk in `WorkspaceFolder::loadDefinitionFile`, beside the `mutateRegisteredDefinitions` hook whose Roblox override ends with the identical operation against an API dump
  - **The mechanism is a checked-in `.patch`, and it is a third shape `mono.vendor/AGENTS.md` did not have.** That file said a patch goes upstream or into a fork; neither fitted - the loop keys on the `Enum_` prefix this repository's generator emits and nobody else's, so there is nothing to send upstream, and a fork is a remote to own and a push to make for one hunk. `mono.vendor/patches/luau-lsp-dotted-enum-types.patch` is visible in a normal diff, reproducible from a clean clone with `just luau-lsp`, and fails **loudly** the moment upstream moves the code it edits - which is the property the no-copied-files rule exists to protect rather than an exception to it. `update = none` on that submodule is the other half: nothing else in the repository runs `git submodule update` against it, so nothing silently reverts the patch under a built binary. The section added there says why it does not generalise to a vendor the engine links
  - **Hover was the open question and it needed the one-line addition**, measured both ways over a real `textDocument/hover` round-trip rather than inferred: without it hover reads `local side: Enum_NormalId`, because the type's `name` is what `toString` prints and a binding alone does not change it. Roblox's path sets `ctv->name = "Enum." + name` for exactly that reason and this does the same; `scriptcheck` got the same line, so a diagnostic from `just typecheck` and one from the editor name the same thing
  - **Rule 6, which is the difference between closed and fixed.** `just typecheck-editor` runs `luau-lsp analyze` over every example through the server an editor actually runs and is in `just check` - **1.5 s**, against 11 minutes of CPU once for that tool's own Luau - 39 s wall on 24 cores, measured from a deleted build tree and an unpatched clone. Proved by mutation in both directions: reverse-applying the patch makes it report `Portals-1-world.luau(410,39): Unknown type 'Enum.NormalId'` and exit 1, and a deliberately broken patch stops `just luau-lsp` with the path named. Two numbers the entry stated were also wrong - it said 35 enums and then 36 where `scriptcheck` reports **38**, and it was written about `Enum.Material`, which this engine does not have

- [x] **a client runs scripts in the world it does not own, which is the half of `D00116` that was blocking the other two.** `client::BuildReplicatedWorld` opens a VM through `game::StartWorldScripts` with `script::HostRole::OfClient` - the same loader the studio's Play and a dedicated server use, so the heartbeat is on the fixed tick delta in one place rather than three. What a replica needs on top is that its scripts *arrive after* the VM: it is empty when the runtime opens and fills from the wire, so `replica-scripts` asks each tick what has landed and `Runtime::RunNewScripts` starts each instance exactly once. `RunWorldScripts` is unchanged and still starts everything it finds, because that is a host saying "start this game"
  - **The rule is a class one and a container one, and `ScriptsIn` only had the first.** Roblox's class half has been there since v0.6 - a `Script` is the server's - and `script::ClientScriptsIn` adds the containment half: a `LocalScript` runs when it is under this viewer's own `Player` or under `ReplicatedFirst`, so somebody else's player and the `StarterPlayerScripts` template are excluded by *where they are* rather than by a list of names. Folding it into `ScriptsIn` was refused: single player is a host that owns its world, and a `LocalScript` an author parked in `Workspace` still runs there
  - **Every button in every replicated world was silent, and `gui::Router` was never the problem.** `Client::Draw` compiled, routed and delivered into `Rendered` - the local scene - while a person's `PlayerGui` is a subtree of their own `Player`, which is a row in the replica. So a press was picked correctly, produced the right event and was handed to a VM that was not the button's. `Client::InterfaceWorld` answers the replica once the join has completed, and `client.replica.scripts` drives a real router into a script rather than synthesising a `GuiEvent` - the shape that would have passed against the broken code
  - **The trust boundary is `ecs::Store`'s and nothing new was added to it.** A client script cannot write a property (`SetProperty` refuses in an adopt-only store) and cannot mint an instance (`MayMintAuthoritative`), both raise in the binding rather than failing silently so the author can tell a refusal from a value the next delta overwrote, and a script can only name rows `Authority::SetInterest` chose to send. The store is made adopt-only *before* the VM opens now, in `Client::BeginConnecting` and in `PlayLink::Start`, because opening one asks the store whether minting is legal and an index taken in that window is one the server is also handing out
  - **`gui::InstallGuiServices` reached its third and fourth callers**: the replicated path and `Editor::PrepareWorld`, the line `D00117` recorded as owed. It **completes** a replica rather than furnishing one - no `gui.` component is replicated, so a client is shown a `GuiService` that is a name and a class, and `Select` and `Focus` both answer `false` without the state on it. It mints only where minting is legal, which is what makes it safe once a tick on a world filling from a snapshot
  - **A VM opened over a replica said "refusing CreateInstance" on every join**, because `OpenWorkspace` calls `scene::InstallServices` so that a hand-built world always has a `Workspace`. The fallback was already correct and the error-level line read as a fault; both bindings ask the store first now and resolve whatever the authority sent

- [x] **and the scripts it runs are the authority's now, which is the other half of that.** `replication::DefaultReplicatedComponents` admitted `scene.` and three `ecs.` rows, so a `LocalScript` crossed as a name and a class with no program on it and a `ScreenGui` crossed with no `gui.Element` - a client ran the scripts its own host had put in the replica and drew an interface nobody had authored. The whole `gui.` prefix crosses now, less `gui.Resolved`, `gui.SpatialCanvas` and `gui.GuiServiceState`, and five `script.` rows are named beside the `ecs.` three. `client.replica.arrival` drives it over a real `Listener` and `Connector`: a program that exists only in the authority's `SourceCache` runs on the client, and a `TextLabel` authored there compiles to a draw command here
  - **The rule is one sentence and it decides every row: what the authority decided crosses, what the machine looking at the world decided does not.** A label's words are the first; where the layout put that label on *this* screen, and which `TextBox` has *this* keyboard, are the second. `gui.GuiServiceState` is the one that would have been wrong rather than merely wasteful - one row per world holding a focus, so two people typing would be two clients writing one row
  - **A program is stored once and mirrored onto the instance, because a resource cannot be filtered by interest.** `script::SourceCache` is one table for the whole world, so replicating it would put `ServerScriptService`'s programs on every client - the leak `scene::VisibleToClients` closed for the instances themselves, re-opened for their contents. `script.Program` is a derived row with one writer; `MirrorSourcePrograms` never writes one for a `Script`, so a server's logic is not one predicate away from being public. `SourceCache::Generation` is what makes a quiet tick cost a 64-bit compare - **29 µs a tick over fifty scripts of a kilobyte at `-O0`, against 200 µs for a hash of the same programs**, which is what signing them would have cost to learn that nobody had typed anything
  - **A person typing into a replicated `TextBox` keeps what they typed.** `gui::Type` writes `Label::Text` in the replica, so the two ends are meant to disagree - and both the delta path and the anti-entropy audit would have put the authority's empty string back a few ticks later. `SuppressWhenTagged("gui.Label", "gui.Entry")` turns off both at once, because a `TextBox` is the only class carrying an `Entry`. The stated cost is that a script writing `TextBox.Text` after a join does not reach that client, which is the trade Roblox makes from the other side
  - **A row larger than a datagram is staged rather than refused for ever.** `Pack` has no unit smaller than a row, so four kilobytes of Luau built a message `Link::Reserve` turned away - which looks exactly like backpressure, so `Unsent` put it back and the same row was rebuilt every tick for the life of the connection. `Authority::StageOversize` captures just those entities into the blob a preface already uses and `Replica` applies it as an overlay. A full re-snapshot was tried first and measured 81 ticks of streaming per event, during which the client is told about nothing at all
  - **Two version numbers moved and both refuse rather than misread.** `script.LuaSourceContainer` wrote its path as a `core::Name`'s *interning index* - a save file that resolved a script's path to whatever the reading process happened to have interned in that slot - so it writes text now, `ecs::Store::SNAPSHOT_VERSION` is 4 and `replication::PROTOCOL_VERSION` is 9
  - **`ChangeDetection::Observed` is turned on where it is declared.** A detector without a matching `Store::Observe` sends nothing and reports nothing, and there were three hosts to forget it in; `Authority::Survey` observes what the table declares, once per `Publish`, on the world it was handed

- [x] **`D00008` closed as decided rather than as done: the single-player `ALLOW_TIER_ESCAPE` stays undeclared.** The question was whether a `client`-tier program would need to link a `server`-tier library, and it was answered three times as no. `--connect` talks to another process over a socket, so it links no server code; `mono.client --game FILE.agame` plays single player under `HostRole::OfBoth` and took `Engine::game` rather than `Mono::server`, because a game file needs a format and a VM and not a hosted server. Twice the thing that looked like the trigger was not it
  - **What made closing it safe is that the mechanism stopped being untried.** `mono.unified_server_client` and `mono.studio` both declare the escape, for reasons that are theirs and not this entry's, and `expected_graph.json` is where both are visible. So whoever eventually needs a third is copying a line with a working precedent, which is the argument for not adding one now: an escape with no user is what the comment in `mono.client/CMakeLists.txt` says not to add, and what somebody reaches for later to do something unrelated
  - **There is a third option to weigh before the escape, and it needs no escape at all.** A single-player client that spawns `mono.server` as a child and connects over loopback costs a process and buys the crash isolation `parallel/process` already argues for. The distinction worth keeping straight is that the escape is about linking rather than about connecting
- [x] vendor patching builds from a copy: `mono.vendor/patches/<name>/` is the contract, `scripts/vendor-tree.sh` archives the pinned commit into `.cache/vendor/` and patches it there, and a patched submodule is asserted pristine. No more permanently modified vendor in every `git status`
- [x] `D00019` - both Luaus bumped together to `fd83819b`, the revision luau-lsp `d5df9af` pins. The 0.732 ceiling this entry stood on for eight versions is gone rather than raised, because upstream did the `ConstraintSolver::reportError` work itself. `-include cstdint` came off after building without it
- [x] `D00106`'s TypeScript half - `--sourceMap` from the transpile, `script::SourceMap` reads it, and a `.ts` that throws names the line its author wrote instead of one in generated JavaScript. The QuickJS breakpoint half is untouched and the entry stays open
- [x] SDL 3.2.31 to 3.4.14, additive - all 219 SDL symbols the engine names still exist, so nothing under `mono.*` changed. It re-checked `D00046` rather than moving it: 3.4.14 still has no GPU timestamp query
- [x] `server.replication`'s "what ReplicatedFirst holds arrives before the world it covers" no longer samples. Two defects, both of them the case measuring the scheduler: it read its four facts *between* polls, and one `Connector::Poll` drains the socket and applies everything that was in it, so a loaded machine hands the preface and the world to the same call. `Replica::OnPreface` fires inside the apply, before a byte of the world behind it, and `Connector` passes it through - a hook a loading screen wants anyway, since the flag it replaces cannot name the moment. The second was a fixed iteration count used as a deadline: the join finished inside 1200 iterations idle and needed about 1400 under load, so `Remote::Wait` bounds by wall clock instead and every `Join` in the suite goes through it. Measured after: 6 of 6 pass under saturating load where it was 6 of 6 failures, and the whole 35-case server suite passes under the same load. `engine.replication`'s "the preface is observable when a poll drains both blobs at once" reproduces the drain deterministically and checks that a between-polls sampler sees nothing while the hook still does
- [x] the windowed client exits its `--frames` run. It was not the render loop: `Run()` drew its frames, printed its statistics and returned 0, and `~Client` then hung - `Client::Shutdown` released the GPU device while `InterfacePass` still held the raw pointer it borrows, so `~InterfacePass` released its glyph atlas through a freed device and blocked forever on a mutex that had been freed with it. **Nothing caught it because teardown had two paths**: `Renderer::Shutdown` was guarded by `if (Window)`, so every headless check this repository has walked an order the shipped client never takes. One path now, the interface pass shut down ahead of the device it borrowed, and `SDL_Quit` paired with the `SDL_Init` a headless run also makes. `just client-exit` runs both modes under a `timeout` and fails on a hang, which is the one outcome a `TEST_CASE` cannot report; `client-smoke` gained the same bound
- [x] mirrors are cut off left and right on any viewport that is not square. Not culling: `FitExtents` clamps a surface camera's fit to the viewer's frustum so close-up panes keep their texels, `FrustumCorners` builds that frustum from `ActiveCamera::AspectRatio`, and **nothing outside the test suite had ever written that field** - so the clamp was a *square* screen and the pane outside it projected past the texture's `0..1`, where `opaque.frag` draws flat tint. On the 1631x599 panel it was reported from that is 37% of the width the viewer could see; vertical was always right because the field of view is the vertical one. `scene::SetViewportSize` is the writer, called per frame by the client and by each studio panel after whatever names the eye, and the fit case asserts both directions: covered at 1631x599, and *not* covered on the same screen with the field back at its default
- [x] the viewport camera's `FieldOfView` could not be edited in the properties panel. `EnsureViewerCamera` assigned the whole `Camera` component every frame, so an author's value held for one frame and came back as 69.9008 degrees - the default 1.22 radians, which is the editor's fingerprint rather than a script's. `NearPlane` and `FarPlane` went the same way, and it was Edit, Run and Play alike rather than Run only. Measured through a headless editor's control port in all three modes, before and after. `studio::ViewerLensToWrite` keeps the lens until somebody takes it and then leaves it alone, so the fly speed can still stretch the far plane on a camera nobody has touched


### v0.16

- [x] do a stress test with 200 (headless) clients connecting to the single server and optimise everything where possible. Always produce a baseline\_flamegraph.ext, then every iteration make a iter\(N\)\_flamegraph.ext so you can compare as you develop. `mono.tools/loadtest`, `just stress`, `scripts/flamegraph.py`, and five iterations in `.cache/stress/RESULTS.md` - p95 tick 1251 ms to 482 ms, clients joined 55 to 180.
- [x] the two hot frames `.cache/stress/RESULTS.md` leaves: the occlusion raycast in the priority score, and `Authority::BuildComponents`. Five iterations in `.cache/stress/RESULTS.md` - a walk along the ray in `spatial` (Amanatides and Woo, 10.67 ms to 12.90 us on the benchmark's fine-cell row), `Authority::SetPriorityRefinement` so occlusion is asked only about the rows in contention, `Client::Unconfirmed` as a sorted vector, and a bounded rotating recovery walk. p95 tick 416 ms to 280 ms, throughput to clients 6.6 MB/s to 12.3 MB/s, 200 of 200 clients joined.
- [x] `ShapeCast` walks the thick line of a long sweep and de-duplicates from the first centre-cell neighbourhood shared with each proxy. A measured crossover keeps the volume walk when its small envelope opens fewer cells.
- [x] setup a CDN mode that is:  1. server distributes from cdn (default) - when clients connect, the server itself connects to the cdn and streams assets as needed to the client (client has no authority for it except rate-limited retries only to the server). 2. server reroutes client - when client connects to server, it tells the clients where all the cdns are that are configured on the server
- [x] concise `RUNNING.md` tutorial for publishing a CDN store and serving it from a folder or HTTP origin
- [x] concise `RUNNING.md` tutorial for pointing a game server at a custom CDN
- [x] concise `RUNNING.md` tutorial for adding a local store or remote origin to Studio
- [x] concise `RUNNING.md` tutorial for connecting a client using the server-provided CDN, grant and publisher key, with an alternative mode that pins the client's own CDN and key
- [x] universe properties in explorer: selecting the universe root exposes its name, execution mode, catch-up cap, bus budget and world count in Properties, with authored tuning saved in the game file
- [x] all user interface elements render in the client and Studio, including interactive ScreenGui, lit and depth-tested SurfaceGui and BillboardGui collectors, and nested ViewportFrame scenes
- [x] portals use recursive off-axis projections, seam-clipped and cloned geometry, through-seam lighting and effects, and paired physics proxies and crossings so bodies and objects remain continuous at the opening
- [x] portal mouths use per-view void-side captures: each destination renders without its portal panes, keeps scene and through-portal lighting, then composites nested captures back onto the exact opening. `Portal.Enabled` disables capture, lighting, seam geometry, crossing and pane opening while retaining the authored link.
- [x] audited the declared property surface against runtime consumers: Lighting now drives per-world sun, ambient, outdoor light, fog, recursive portal views and particles; service fixture identity is read-only; light controls are class-specific; and unsupported GUI, local-shadow and selection-fill controls are no longer advertised as working properties
